into two domains. Orient
S^{2}
and denote by
D^{+}
the domain of
S^{2}
, for which
\psi
is the boundary and for which at each point of
\psi
, where
\psi
is differentiable, the direction along
\psi
together with a direction outside the domain is positive. Similarly we define
D^{+}_{i}
. One can observe then that the closures of
D^{+}_{i}
converge in the Hausdorff sense to the closure of
D^{+}
.

Orient
\mathbb{RP}^{3}
and recall that
\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
is future-oriented. The future-orientation of
\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
induces a future-orientation on
\partial\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
. Further, this induces an orientation on every spacelike curve on
\partial\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
by demanding that at every point, where the curve is differentiable, the direction along the curve, a future direction along
\partial\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
and a direction outwards
\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
form a positive triple in
\mathbb{RP}^{3}
. In particular, this induces an orientation on all
\Lambda_{\rho}
. When
\rho_{i}
converge to
\rho
, the convergence of
\Lambda_{\rho_{i}}
to
\Lambda_{\rho}
comes from the convergence of graphs under
\partial\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}\cong\mathbb{RP}^{1}\times\mathbb{RP}^{1}
. Thus, this convergence is oriented.

Now return to our problem. Due to Corollary
3.7
,
{\rm cl}(\widetilde{C}_{\rho_{i}})
converge to
{\rm cl}(\widetilde{C}_{\rho})
. Since these are closely convex sets,
\partial\widetilde{C}_{\rho_{i}}
converge to
\partial\widetilde{C}_{\rho}
. Due to Lemma
3.6
,
\Lambda_{\rho_{i}}
converge to
\Lambda_{\rho}
. Pick
p\in{\rm int}(\widetilde{C}_{\rho})
. For all large enough
i
, we have
p\in{\rm int}(\widetilde{C}_{\rho_{i}})
. We project
\partial\widetilde{C}_{\rho_{i}}
and
\partial\widetilde{C}_{\rho}
onto the sphere of directions from
p
, which we denote by
S^{2}
. Then
\Lambda_{\rho_{i}}
and
\Lambda_{\rho}
are homeomorphically projected onto simple closed Lipschitz curves on
S^{2}
, which we denote by
\psi_{i}
and
\psi
, and
\partial^{\pm}\widetilde{C}_{\rho_{i}}
,
\partial^{\pm}\widetilde{C}_{\rho}
are projected homeomorphically onto domains bounded by
\psi_{i}
and
\psi
. Due to our observation and by construction, the closures of the former domains converge to the closure of the latter. Hence
{\rm cl}(\partial^{\pm}\widetilde{C}_{\rho_{i}})
converge to
{\rm cl}(\partial^{\pm}\widetilde{C}_{\rho})
.
∎

Just in the same way one shows

Lemma 3.12
.

The sets
{\rm cl}(\partial^{\pm}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho_{i}})
converge to
{\rm cl}(\partial^{\pm}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho})
.

We need it for

Corollary 3.13
.

The sets
\mathbb{RP}^{3}\backslash\widetilde{\Omega}^{+}_{\rho_{i}}
converge to
\mathbb{RP}^{3}\backslash\widetilde{\Omega}^{+}_{\rho}
.

Proof.

Define
\widetilde{\Omega}^{--}_{\rho}
to be the future of
\partial^{+}\widetilde{C}_{\rho}
in
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}
. This is a convex domain, whose boundary is
\partial^{+}\widetilde{C}_{\rho}\cup\Lambda_{\rho}\cup\partial^{-}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}
. We define similarly
\widetilde{\Omega}^{--}_{\rho_{i}}
. Lemmas
3.6
,
3.11
and
3.12
imply that
\partial\widetilde{\Omega}^{--}_{\rho_{i}}
converge to
\partial\widetilde{\Omega}^{--}_{\rho}
. Hence,
\widetilde{\Omega}^{--}_{\rho_{i}}
converge to
\widetilde{\Omega}^{--}_{\rho}
. It remains to observe that

\mathbb{RP}^{3}\backslash\widetilde{\Omega}^{+}_{\rho_{i}}={\rm cl}(\widetilde{\Omega}^{--}_{\rho_{i}})\cup(\mathbb{RP}^{3}\backslash\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho_{i}}),

\mathbb{RP}^{3}\backslash\widetilde{\Omega}^{+}_{\rho}={\rm cl}(\widetilde{\Omega}^{--}_{\rho})\cup(\mathbb{RP}^{3}\backslash\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho})

and use Corollary
3.8
and Lemma
3.9
.
∎

3.4.5
Spaces of bent surfaces

Define
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}=\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}(\rho_{\circ},V):=\mathcal{R}\times(\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3})^{V}
. We will denote its elements by
(\rho,\widetilde{f})
where
\widetilde{f}:V\rightarrow\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
. Fix a lift
V\rightarrow\widetilde{V}
that we call canonical. Using it, we extend
\widetilde{f}
to a
\theta_{\rho}
-equivariant map
\widetilde{f}:\widetilde{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
. We will sometimes consider elements of
V
as elements of
\widetilde{V}
via the canonical lift. For
v\in V
we denote by
\widetilde{V\backslash v}
the preimage of
V\backslash v
in
\widetilde{V}
. Denote by
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{w}_{-}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}
the subset of
(\rho,\widetilde{f})
such that
\widetilde{f}(V)\subset\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}
. Due to Lemma
3.9
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{w}_{-}
is open. We say that
\widetilde{f}
is in a
(future-)convex position
if
\widetilde{f}(V)\subset(\widetilde{\Omega}^{+}_{\rho}\cup\partial^{+}\widetilde{C}_{\rho})
,
\widetilde{f}
is injective and for every
v\in V
we have
\widetilde{f}(v)\notin{\rm int}({\rm conv}(\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{V\backslash v}))).
We say that
\widetilde{f}
is in a
strictly (future-)convex position
if
\widetilde{f}(V)\subset\widetilde{\Omega}^{+}_{\rho}
and for every
v\in V
we have
\widetilde{f}(v)\notin{\rm conv}(\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{V\backslash v}))
. Note that in Part 1 we deal only with maps in future-convex/strictly future-convex position, so we will omit the word future in this context. However, we will resume to use it in Part 2, where we will need similarly to define a
past-convex position
. We denote the subset of
(\rho,\widetilde{f})
when
\widetilde{f}
is in convex position by
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{c}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{w}
and the subset when
\widetilde{f}
is in strictly convex position by
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{s}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{c}
. Due to Corollary
3.13
, the latter is open in
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{w}
, thereby it is a manifold of dimension
3(n-k)
. For
(\rho,\widetilde{f})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{w}
define
{\rm conv}(\widetilde{f}):={\rm conv}(\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{V}))
. Due to Lemma
3.5
,
{\rm cl}(\widetilde{C}_{\rho})\subset{\rm conv}(\widetilde{f})
. The boundary of
{\rm conv}(\widetilde{f})
consists of
\Lambda_{\rho}
, a future-convex and a past-convex spacelike surfaces. We denote the future-convex one by
\Sigma(\widetilde{f})
. When
(\rho,\widetilde{f})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{c}
, the past-convex one is
\partial^{-}\widetilde{C}_{\rho}
.

In a similar way we define
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}=\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}(\rho_{\circ},V):=T_{\rho_{\circ}}\mathcal{R}\times(\mathbb{R}^{2,1})^{V}
and denote its elements by
(\tau,\widetilde{f})
where
\widetilde{f}:\widetilde{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2,1}
is a
\iota_{\tau}
-equivariant map. Define
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{w}
as the subset of those
(\tau,\widetilde{f})
that
\widetilde{f}(V)\subset\widetilde{\Omega}_{\tau}^{+}
; define
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{c}
as the subset of those that, in addition, are injective and for every
v\in V
we have
\widetilde{f}(v)\notin{\rm int}({\rm conv}(\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{V\backslash v})))
; define
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{s}
as the subset of those that for every
v\in V
we have
\widetilde{f}(v)\notin{\rm conv}(\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{V\backslash v}))
.
Note that
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}
naturally has a structure of a vector space and the other spaces are cones in it. We use the notation
{\rm conv}(\widetilde{f})
,
\Sigma(\widetilde{f})
similarly as above.

Lemmas
3.5
and
3.6
imply

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
(\rho_{i},\widetilde{f}_{i})\rightarrow(\rho,\widetilde{f})
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{w}
. Then
{\rm conv}(\widetilde{f}_{i})\rightarrow{\rm conv}(\widetilde{f})
.

Moreover, the same proof as the proof of Lemma
3.11
imply

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
(\rho_{i},\widetilde{f}_{i})\rightarrow(\rho,\widetilde{f})
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{w}
. Then
{\rm cl}(\Sigma(\widetilde{f}_{i}))\rightarrow{\rm cl}(\Sigma(\widetilde{f}))
.

The group
G_{-}
acts on
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}
from the left by conjugation on
\mathcal{R}
and by isometries on
(\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3})^{V}
. This action is free and properly discontinuous. Furthermore,
\pi_{1}S
acts on
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}
from the left fiberwise, via
\theta_{\rho}
on
\{\rho\}\times(\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3})^{V}
. This action is free and properly discontinuous on
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{w}
. These two actions commute and we denote the quotient by
\mathcal{P}_{-}^{w}
. Its elements are the pairs
(\rho,f)
where
\rho\in\mathcal{T}
and
f:V\rightarrow\Omega_{\rho}
. Define the subsets
\mathcal{P}_{-}^{s},\mathcal{P}_{-}^{c}
in the obvious manner. We denote by
{\rm conv}(f)
,
\Sigma(f)
the projections of
{\rm conv}(\widetilde{f})
,
\Sigma(\widetilde{f})
for some lifts
(\rho,\widetilde{f})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{w}
. In the same way we define
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{w}\supset{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{c}\supset{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{s}
. They all have a coned structure;
{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{w}
and
{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{s}
are coned manifolds. Their elements are the pairs
(\tau,f)
where
\tau\in T_{\rho_{\circ}}\mathcal{T}
and
f:V\rightarrow\Omega^{+}_{\tau}
.

One might expect that the surfaces
\Sigma(\widetilde{f})
are (locally) polyhedral, i.e., around every point they coincide with a part of the boundary of a convex polyhedron in
\mathbb{RP}^{3}
(by a polyhedron we mean the convex hull of finitely many points). Curiously, while it holds for
(\tau,\widetilde{f})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{w}
(see
[
34
, Lemma 2.7]
), it does not hold for
(\rho,\widetilde{f})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{w}
. The main reason for this is that
\partial^{+}\widetilde{C}_{\rho}
can be non-polyhedral. As a convex spacelike surface, it is endowed with the intrinsic metric, see details in Section
A.
. There is an isometry
\mathbb{H}^{2}\rightarrow\partial^{+}\widetilde{C}_{\rho}
. The surface
\partial^{+}\widetilde{C}_{\rho}
is totally geodesic apart from a closed set of complete geodesics of
\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
. The preimage of this set is a geodesic lamination in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
invariant with respect to a Fuchsian representation of
\pi_{1}S
, which we denote by
\rho^{+}\in\mathcal{R}
. The data of how
\partial^{+}\widetilde{C}_{\rho}
is bent in
\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
defines a transverse measure on the geodesic lamination. Denote the
\rho^{+}
-projection of the obtained measured lamination to
S
by
\lambda^{+}
, which is a measured geodesic lamination on
S
. It is called the
bending lamination
of
\partial^{+}C_{\rho}
. The measure of an isolated leaf of
\lambda^{+}
is the exterior dihedral angle in
\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
of the respective bending line of
\partial^{+}\widetilde{C}_{\rho}
. Measured geodesic laminations on
S
naturally form a PL-manifold
\mathcal{ML}=\mathcal{ML}(S)
, homeomorphic to a
(6k-6)
-dimensional ball. For an introduction to measured geodesic laminations we refer to
[
12
,
56
]
, and for the details of this construction we refer to
[
57
,
20
]
. This is similar to the geometry of quasi-Fuchsian hyperbolic 3-manifolds, see, e.g.,
[
22
,
14
]
.

From the work of Mess
[
57
]
, any measured geodesic lamination can appear as
\lambda^{+}
. In particular, it can have non-isolated leaves. In this case,
\partial^{+}\widetilde{C}_{\rho}
is non-polyhedral and
\Sigma(\widetilde{f})
can be non-polyhedral as well. In particular, the image of
\widetilde{f}
can belong to
\partial^{+}\widetilde{C}_{\rho}
, in which case
\Sigma(\widetilde{f})=\partial^{+}\widetilde{C}_{\rho}
. Even if
\widetilde{f}
is in a strictly convex position,
\Sigma(\widetilde{f})
still can have nonempty intersection with
\partial^{+}\widetilde{C}_{\rho}
, in which case it can fail to be polyhedral. All non-polyhedrality, however, anyway comes only from the intersection with
\partial^{+}\widetilde{C}_{\rho}
.

Let
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{RP}^{3}
be a (locally) convex embedded surface. If
p\in\Sigma
does not belong to the relative interior of any segment belonging to
\Sigma
, we call
p
a
vertex
of
\Sigma
. If it belongs to the relative interiors of two such segments with distinct tangents,
p
is called
regular
. Otherwise, it is called an
edge-point
. A
face
of
\Sigma
is the closure in
\Sigma
of a connected component of the set of regular points. An
edge
is the closure of a maximal segment in
\Sigma
consisting from edge-points. We say that
\Sigma
is
bent
if the set of vertices is discrete. It is
strictly polyhedral
if it is polyhedral and each face is isomorphic to a (compact affine) polygon. These notions are local and extend to convex surfaces in anti-de Sitter spacetimes (and in locally projective manifolds in general). For every
(\rho,\widetilde{f})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{w}
,
\Sigma(\widetilde{f})
is a bent surface. It is strictly polyhedral if and only if the timelike distance between
\Sigma(\widetilde{f})
and
\partial^{+}\widetilde{C}_{\rho}
is positive. A proof is the same as in the hyperbolic case, see
[
64
, Corollary 3.19]
. In such case, we also say that
\widetilde{f}
is strictly polyhedral. This notion extends to the elements of
\mathcal{P}_{-}^{w}
. The set of vertices of
\Sigma(f)
will be denoted by
V(f)\subseteq V
. We have
(\rho,f)\in\mathcal{P}_{-}^{s}
if and only if
V(f)=V
. We denote the respective subsets of strictly polyhedral elements by
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-,sp}^{c}
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-,sp}^{s}
,
\mathcal{P}_{-,sp}^{c}
,
\mathcal{P}_{-,sp}^{s}
. As for
(\tau,\widetilde{f})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{c}
, it was shown in
[
34
, Lemma 2.7]
that in fact
\widetilde{f}
is always strictly polyhedral.

Pick
\rho\in\mathcal{T}
and consider
\Omega_{\rho}
. By construction, for every Cauchy surface
\Sigma\subset\Omega_{\rho}
there exists a homeomorphism
\zeta:\Sigma\rightarrow S
, which is defined up to isotopy. For
(\rho,f)\in\mathcal{P}_{-}^{c}
,
\zeta:\Sigma(f)\rightarrow S
can be chosen so that
\zeta\circ f
is the identity on
V
. Then such
\zeta
is chosen up to
h\in\mathcal{H}
. If
(\rho,f)\in\mathcal{P}_{-,sp}^{c}
and such
\zeta
is chosen for
\Sigma(f)
, it pushes forward the edges of
\Sigma(f)
to a celluation of
(S,V)
. A
celluation
of
(S,V)
is defined similarly as a triangulation with the difference that now we allow cells with arbitrary number of vertices as faces and also allow them to contain some points of
V
in the interior. The notions of equivalence and weak equivalence apply also to celluations. By a
face celluation
of
(\rho,f)
we will mean a celluation of
(S,V)
as above, which is then defined up to weak equivalence. We will abuse the terminology and say that a celluation
\mathscr{C}_{1}
of
(S,V)
is a subdivision of a celluation
\mathscr{C}_{2}
if
\mathscr{C}_{1}
is weakly equivalent to a subdivision of
\mathscr{C}_{2}
in a straightforward sense. All these notions apply also to the elements of
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{c}
. It was shown in
[
34
, Lemma 2.13]
that for any
(\tau,f)\in\mathcal{P}_{0}^{c}
there exists a neighborhood
U\ni(\tau,f)
such that for every
(\tau^{\prime},f^{\prime})\in U
the face celluation of
f^{\prime}
is a subdivision of the face celluation of
f
. The same proof works to prove the same claim for
(\rho,f)\in\mathcal{P}_{-,sp}^{c}
. We can also speak about face celluations of
\widetilde{f}
for
(\rho,\widetilde{f})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-,sp}^{c}
or
(\tau,\widetilde{f})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{c}
, where we mean the respective decompositions of
(\widetilde{S},\widetilde{V})
and the equivalences are
\pi_{1}S
-invariant.

3.5
Blow-up of the space of bent surfaces

3.5.1
Convergence of domains of discontinuity at the blow-up

Consider a continuous curve
\rho_{t}:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathcal{R}
with
\rho_{0}=\rho_{\circ}
, differentiable at
t=0
with
\dot{\rho}_{0}=\tau\in T_{\rho_{\circ}}\mathcal{R}
. As
t\rightarrow 0
, we have

Lemma 3.16
.

The sets
g_{t}^{*}\Lambda_{\rho_{t}}
converge to
\Lambda_{\tau}
.

Proof.

Let
h_{t}:=h_{\rho_{t}}
be the respective homeomorphisms conjugating
\rho_{t}
to
\rho_{\circ}
given by (
3.5
). Since the embedding
\mathcal{R}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{QS}
is smooth, one can differentiate the path
h_{t}
at zero and get a vector field
\dot{h}_{0}
. By differentiating the condition

\rho_{\circ}=h_{t}^{-1}\rho_{t}h_{t},
we get that
\dot{h}_{0}
satisfies (
3.6
). By uniqueness, it coincides with
\xi_{\tau}
. Now we pass to the co-Minkowski coordinate chart, and notice that in this chart
g_{t}^{*}
acts by preserving
z_{1},z_{2}
and multiplying
z_{3}
by
1/t
. Then for any
p\in\mathbb{RP}^{1}
, the
g_{t}^{*}
-images of
(p,h_{t}(p))\in\mathbb{RP}^{1}\times\mathbb{RP}^{1}\cong\partial\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
converge to
(p,\dot{h}_{0}(p))\in T\mathbb{RP}^{1}\cong\partial^{*}\mathbb{R}^{2,1}
as
t\rightarrow 0
. Pick any
\mathbb{R}
-chart for
\mathbb{RP}^{1}
, so
h_{t}
,
h
,
\dot{h}_{0}
become
\mathbb{R}
-valued functions defined on a domain in
\mathbb{R}
. Since the embedding
\mathcal{R}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{QS}
is smooth, by considering the Fréchet derivative of
h_{t}
at
t=0
in the uniform topology, it follows that in the chart
(h_{t}-h_{0})/t
converge to
\dot{h}_{0}
uniformly. This implies that
\Lambda_{\tau}
is the Hausdorff limit of
g_{t}^{*}\Lambda_{\rho_{t}}
, as desired.
∎

By taking the convex hulls, we get

Corollary 3.17
.

The sets
g_{t}^{*}{\rm cl}(\widetilde{C}_{\rho_{t}})
converge to
{\rm cl}(\widetilde{C}_{\tau})
.

By passing to the dual sets, we obtain

Corollary 3.18
.

The sets
g_{t}{\rm cl}(\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho_{t}})
converge to
{\rm cl}(\widetilde{\Omega}_{\tau})
.

Lemma 3.19
.

The sets
g_{t}\Lambda_{\rho_{t}}
converge to
\Lambda_{\rho_{\circ}}
.

Proof.

Due to Lemma
3.6
,
\Lambda_{\rho_{t}}
converge to
\Lambda_{\rho_{\circ}}
. However,
\Lambda_{\rho_{\circ}}
is pointwise fixed by all
g_{t}
and every point of
\Lambda_{\rho_{\circ}}
has a basis of neighborhoods
\{U_{i}\}
such that for all
t
and
i
we have
g_{t}U_{i}\subset U_{i}
. From this and from the equivalence of the Hausorff topology on the space of closed subsets of
\mathbb{RP}^{3}
to the Vietoris topology, it follows that
g_{t}\Lambda_{\rho_{t}}
converge to
\Lambda_{\rho_{\circ}}
.
∎

Corollary 3.20
.

The sets
g_{t}{\rm cl}(\widetilde{C}_{\rho_{t}})
converge to
{\rm cl}(\widetilde{C}_{\rho_{\circ}})
.

The next three results are obtained the same as Lemmas
3.11
,
3.12
and Corollary
3.13
.

Lemma 3.21
.

The sets
g_{t}{\rm cl}(\partial^{-}\widetilde{C}_{\rho_{t}})
converge to
{\rm cl}(\widetilde{C}_{\rho_{\circ}})
.

Lemma 3.22
.

The sets
g_{t}{\rm cl}(\partial^{+}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho_{t}})
converge to
{\rm cl}(\partial^{+}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\tau})
.

Corollary 3.23
.

The sets
g_{t}(\mathbb{RP}^{3}\backslash\widetilde{\Omega}^{+}_{\rho_{t}})
converge to
\mathbb{RP}^{3}\backslash\widetilde{\Omega}^{+}_{\tau}
.

3.5.2
K
-surfaces foliation

We will rely on an important theorem of Barbot–Beguin–Zeghib
[
5
]
:

Theorem 3.24
.

For every
\rho\in\mathcal{T}
there exists a smooth foliation of
\Omega^{+}_{\rho}
by Cauchy surfaces of constant Gauss curvature.

By the Gauss equation, the sectional curvature at a point of a surface with the Gauss curvature
K
is
-K-1
. The leaves of the foliation are strictly convex. By strictly convex we mean a smooth convex surface with non-degenerate shape operator.

Note that in
[
5
]
the authors state only that the foliation is continuous. However, in another paper
[
4
]
they prove that
\Omega_{\rho}
is foliated by CMC-surfaces (of constant mean curvature), and there they show that this foliation is smooth (in fact, analytic). It was observed that if
\Sigma
is a CMC-surface of mean curvature
H
, then its normal evolution in past at time
H/2+1+\sqrt{H^{2}/4+1}
is a future-convex
K
-surface with
K=H(H+\sqrt{H^{2}/4+1})/2
, see, e.g.,
[
20
, Proposition 7.1.4]
. Thus, the smoothness of the
K
-surface foliation also follows.

Pick
\rho\in\mathcal{R}
. Let
L\subset\Omega_{\rho}
be a leaf of the foliation from Theorem
3.24
and
\widetilde{L}\subset\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}
be its preimage. From Lemma
3.5
,
{\rm cl}(\widetilde{L})=\widetilde{L}\cup\Lambda_{\rho}
. Pick
p\in\Lambda_{\rho}
, let
\Pi
be the tangent plane at
p
to
\partial\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
. We claim

Lemma 3.25
.

For any
p_{i}\rightarrow p
,
p_{i}\in\widetilde{L}
, the supporting planes
\Pi_{i}
at
p_{i}
to
\widetilde{L}
converge to
\Pi
.

Proof.

Consider the dual surface
\widetilde{L}^{*}
, which consists from the points dual to the supporting planes to
\widetilde{L}
. Then it is a strictly past-convex
\theta_{\rho}
-invariant surface in
\widetilde{\Omega}^{-}_{\rho}
. From Lemma
3.5
,
{\rm cl}(\widetilde{L}^{*})=\widetilde{L}^{*}\cup\Lambda_{\rho}
. Let
q_{i}\in\widetilde{L}^{*}
be dual to
\Pi_{i}
. Up to subsequence, they converge to
p^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\rho}
. Let
\Pi^{\prime}
be the tangent plane at
p^{\prime}
to
\partial\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
. Since
(p^{\prime})^{*}=\Pi^{\prime}
, the respective subsequence of
\Pi_{i}
converge to
\Pi^{\prime}
. But then
\Pi^{\prime}
must pass through
p
. Since
\Lambda_{\rho}
is achronal,
p^{\prime}=p
, hence
\Pi^{\prime}=\Pi
. Using Lemma
3.10
, we get the desired result.
∎

Define now
\widetilde{L}^{+}=\widetilde{L}
and pick a leaf
\widetilde{L}^{-}
of a similar foliation of
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}^{-}
. Then
{\rm cl}(\widetilde{L}^{+})
and
{\rm cl}(\widetilde{L}^{-})
bound a convex set
C
with
\partial C=\widetilde{L}^{+}\cup\Lambda_{\rho}\cup\widetilde{L}^{-}
. Lemma
3.25
implies that at every point of
\Lambda_{\rho}
,
C
has a unique supporting plane. Since
\widetilde{L}^{\pm}
are strictly convex, we have

Corollary 3.26
.

\partial C
is
C^{1}
and touches
\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
along
\Lambda_{\rho}
.

3.5.3
Construction of the blow-up

The space
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}
is a real vector space and
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{s}
is an open cone in it. The representation
\theta_{\rho_{\circ}}
fixes a point in
\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
, which we assume to be
o
.
Let
o_{-}=(\rho_{\circ},\widetilde{f})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}
be the configuration with
\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{V})=o
. We have the identification
T_{o}\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}\cong\mathbb{R}^{2,1}
. This produces an identification
T_{o_{-}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}\cong\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}
.

Lemma 3.27
.

Let
x_{t}=(\rho_{t},\widetilde{f}_{t}):[0,1]\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}
be a
C^{1}
-curve with
x_{0}=o_{-}
and
\dot{x}_{0}=(\tau,\widetilde{f})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{w}
. Then for all small enough
t>0
we have
x_{t}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{w}
.

Proof.

Let
p_{t}:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
be a
C^{1}
-curve with
p_{0}=o
and
\dot{p}_{0}\in\widetilde{\Omega}^{+}_{\tau}
. Due to Corollary
3.18
,
g_{t}(\mathbb{RP}^{3}\backslash\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho_{t}})
converge to
\mathbb{RP}^{3}\backslash\widetilde{\Omega}_{\tau}
. Thereby,
g_{t}p_{t}\in g_{t}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho_{t}}
for all small enough
t
, which is equivalent to
p_{t}\in\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho_{t}}
.
From this we see that for all small enough
t
, for each
v\in V
we have
\widetilde{f}_{t}(v)\in\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho_{t}}
.
∎

Our main technical result is

Lemma 3.28
.

Let
x_{t}=(\rho_{t},\widetilde{f}_{t}):[0,1]\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}
be a
C^{1}
-curve with
x_{0}=o_{-}
and
\dot{x}_{0}=(\tau,\widetilde{f})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{s}
. Then for all small enough
t>0
we have
x_{t}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-,sp}^{s}
and the face celluation of
\widetilde{f}_{t}
is a subdivision of the face celluation of
\widetilde{f}
.

Note that we do not mean that the face celluations of
\widetilde{f}_{t}
are weakly equivalent for all small enough
t
.

Proof.

By the same argument as in the proof of Lemma
3.27
, only using Corollary
3.23
instead of Corollary
3.18
, we get that for all small enough
t
, for each
v\in V
we have
\widetilde{f}_{t}(v)\in\widetilde{\Omega}^{+}_{\rho_{t}}
.
It remains to show that for all small enough
t
,
\widetilde{f}_{t}
is in a strictly convex position, is strictly polyhedral and that its face celluation is a subdivision of the face celluation of
\widetilde{f}
. We first describe the proof idea. Suppose for simplicity that the face celluation of
\widetilde{f}
is a triangulation. Then this triangulation together with the positions of points
\widetilde{f}_{t}(\widetilde{V})
allow us to define a simplicial surface
F_{t}
. We will first observe that, provided
t
is small enough,
F_{t}
is locally convex. We will need then to show that
F_{t}
is globally convex, i.e.,
F_{t}\subset{\rm conv}(F_{t})
, or, equivalently, every locally supporting plane is globally supporting. We will use some ideas of Stoker, who showed that a closed smooth locally convex surface in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
is necessarily globally convex, see
[
77
]
. However, this fact is notably false for non-closed surfaces. But what helps us is that, provided
t
is small enough,
F_{t}
is spacelike for anti-de Sitter geometry, which then restricts its global behavior.

Now we pass to the details.
Consider the Minkowski chart. We perturb it slightly so that
{\rm cl}(\widetilde{\Omega}^{+}_{\rho_{\circ}})
is contained in the domain of the perturbed chart. In what follows we will consider only small enough
t
so that
{\rm cl}(\widetilde{\Omega}^{+}_{\rho_{t}})
is also contained there, which is possible, since
{\rm cl}(\widetilde{\Omega}^{+}_{\rho_{t}}\cup\widetilde{C}_{\rho_{t}})
are properly convex sets converging to
{\rm cl}(\widetilde{\Omega}^{+}_{\rho_{\circ}})
as
t\rightarrow 0
, due to Lemmas
3.11
and
3.12
. We consider the perturbed chart as a vector space with the orientation induced from
\mathbb{RP}^{3}
, and equip it with a Euclidean metric.
For a plane
\Pi
we say an
orientation of
\Pi
for a choice which half-space with respect to
\Pi
to call positive, and which to call negative. For an oriented triple of distinct points
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\in\Pi
, we say that their order
induces an orientation on
\Pi
, by calling positive the half-space towards which the vector
(p_{2}-p_{1})\times(p_{3}-p_{1})
points. Here and in what follows the cross product and the scalar product are Euclidean.

We make two observations. First, we claim that for every quadruple
\widetilde{v}_{1},\widetilde{v}_{2},\widetilde{v}_{3},\widetilde{v}_{4}\in\widetilde{V}
such that
\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{v}_{1})
,
\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{v}_{2})
,
\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{v}_{3})
,
\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{v}_{4})
are affinely independent, for all small enough
t
, the points
\widetilde{f}_{t}(\widetilde{v}_{1})
,
\widetilde{f}_{t}(\widetilde{v}_{2})
,
\widetilde{f}_{t}(\widetilde{v}_{3})
,
\widetilde{f}_{t}(\widetilde{v}_{4})
are also affinely independent, and
\widetilde{f}_{t}(\widetilde{v}_{4})
lies in the half-space of the same sign with respect to the plane spanned by
\widetilde{f}_{t}(\widetilde{v}_{1}),\widetilde{f}_{t}(\widetilde{v}_{2}),\widetilde{f}_{t}(\widetilde{v}_{3})
, as the sign of the half-space with respect to the plane spanned by
\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{v}_{1}),\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{v}_{2}),\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{v}_{3})
containing
\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{v}_{4})
, where we consider the planes oriented by the order of points
\widetilde{v}_{1},\widetilde{v}_{2},\widetilde{v}_{3}
. Indeed, we consider the function

\zeta(t):=\langle\widetilde{f}_{t}(\widetilde{v}_{4})-\widetilde{f}_{t}(\widetilde{v}_{1}),(\widetilde{f}_{t}(\widetilde{v}_{2})-\widetilde{f}_{t}(\widetilde{v}_{1}))\times(\widetilde{f}_{t}(\widetilde{v}_{3})-\widetilde{f}_{t}(\widetilde{v}_{1}))\rangle.

Note that
\zeta(0)=\zeta^{\prime}(0)=\zeta^{\prime\prime}(0)=0
, but

\zeta^{\prime\prime\prime}(0):=\langle\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{v}_{4})-\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{v}_{1}),(\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{v}_{2})-\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{v}_{1}))\times(\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{v}_{3})-\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{v}_{1}))\rangle\neq 0.

Thus, for all small enough
t
, the function
\zeta(t)
has the same sign as
\zeta^{\prime\prime\prime}(t)
, and our claim holds.

Second, we notice that the plane spanned by
\widetilde{f}_{t}(\widetilde{v}_{1})
,
\widetilde{f}_{t}(\widetilde{v}_{2})
,
\widetilde{f}_{t}(\widetilde{v}_{3})
converges to the plane passing through
o
that is parallel in the Minkowski chart to the plane spanned by
\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{v}_{1})
,
\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{v}_{2})
,
\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{v}_{3})
as
t\rightarrow 0
. Note that this means that for all small enough
t
such a plane is spacelike for
\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
. Further, the union of any two planes that correspond to adjacent faces in the face celluation of
\widetilde{f}
is future-convex for all small enough
t
.

For the moment, we suppose that the face celluation of
\widetilde{f}
is a triangulation
\mathscr{T}
of
(\widetilde{S},\widetilde{V})
.
We consider
\widetilde{S}
oriented so that the positive normals to faces of
\widetilde{f}
point to the concave side. For
t>0
, to every triangle
T
of
\mathscr{T}
we associate an oriented plane
\Pi_{t}(T)
spanned by the respective points of
\widetilde{f}_{t}(\widetilde{V})
. We assume that
t
is small enough so that (1) for every
T
and every
\widetilde{v}\in\widetilde{V}
that is adjacent to at least one vertex of
T
in
\mathscr{T}
,
\widetilde{f}_{t}(\widetilde{v})
is in the negative half-space with respect to
\Pi_{t}(T)
. Due to the
\theta_{\rho_{t}}
-invariance, it is enough to check this only for finitely many cases, hence this indeed holds for all small enough
t
because of the first observation above. Moreover, we set
t
small enough so that (2) every
\Pi_{t}(T)
is spacelike for
\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
, does not intersect
\widetilde{C}_{\rho_{t}}
, and for every
\Pi_{t}(T)
and
\Pi_{t}(T^{\prime})
of adjacent
T
and
T^{\prime}
, the intersection of the negative half-spaces is future-convex for
\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
. Because of the
\theta_{\rho_{t}}
-invariance, again it is enough to check this for finitely many cases,
hence it is indeed true for all small enough
t
because of the second observation above.
We extend
\widetilde{f}_{t}
to a simplicial map
F=F_{t}:\widetilde{S}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
with respect to
\mathcal{T}
. Due to assumption (1),
F
is a PL-immersion, i.e., is locally injective. (The local injectivity is non-trivial only at vertices, where it means that the links are embedded.) Furthermore,
F
is locally convex with respect to the orientation, i.e., the Euclidean dihedral angle of every edge, determined by the orientations of the adjacent faces, is less than
\pi
. Next, assumptions (1) and (2) together mean that
F
is locally future-convex, which implies that for each face the past directions with respect to
\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
are positive. Observe that assumption (1) concerns only with polyhedral geometry, while assumption (2) is about anti-de Sitter geometry.

Let
\mathbb{S}^{2}
be the Euclidean unit sphere (in the perturbed Minkowski chart). Consider the limit set
\Lambda=\Lambda_{\rho_{t}}\subset\partial\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
. Look at the Euclidean Gauss map on
\partial\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
, sending a point on
\partial\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
to the exterior unit normal to
\partial\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
. Its restriction to
\Lambda
is a homeomorphism onto a Jordan curve
J\subset\mathbb{S}^{2}
. Denote the components of
\mathbb{S}^{2}\backslash J
by
J^{+}
and
J^{-}
. Consider the foliations of
\widetilde{\Omega}^{\pm}_{\rho_{t}}
by
K
-surfaces, given by Theorem
3.24
. We denote the respective leaves by
\widetilde{L}_{K}^{\pm}
. Due to Corollary
3.26
, the surface
\overline{L}_{K}:=\widetilde{L}^{+}_{K}\cup\Lambda\cup\widetilde{L}^{-}_{K}
is a strictly convex
C^{1}
-surface, which touches
\partial\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
along
\Lambda
.
Thereby, the Euclidean Gauss map on
\overline{L}_{K}
also sends
\Lambda
onto
J
. Assume that the notation
J^{+}
,
J^{-}
is chosen so that the Euclidean Gauss map on
\widetilde{L}^{+}_{K}
has values in
J^{+}
.

Let
\Pi
be a spacelike plane disjoint with
\widetilde{C}_{\rho_{t}}
that intersects
\widetilde{\Omega}^{+}_{\rho_{t}}
. Let
\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
be its Euclidean normal, directing in the past with respect to
\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
. Then
\Pi
is tangent to a unique
\widetilde{L}^{+}_{K}
for some
K
. Thus,
\nu\in J^{+}
.

Let
(\widetilde{S},\mathscr{T}^{*})
be a
\pi_{1}S
-invariant celluation of
\widetilde{S}
dual to
\mathscr{T}
. We now apply the Euclidean Gauss map to the parameterized surface
F
. Recall that
F
is simplicial, immersed and locally convex. We consider its Gauss map as a map
G:(\widetilde{S},\mathscr{T}^{*})\rightarrow\mathbb{S}^{2}
, sending the topological dual cell of each vertex homeomorphically onto the respective geometric cell in
\mathbb{S}^{2}
, respecting the vertices. Then
G
is a local homeomorphism. Due to assumption (2) on
t
and the observation just above,
G
values in
J^{+}
.

Now define
\widetilde{S}^{\bdia}
as the abstract union
\widetilde{S}\cup\Lambda
. We define a topology on
\widetilde{S}^{\bdia}
using the map
F
: open sets are of the form
(U\cap\Lambda)\cup F^{-1}(U)
for open
U\subset\mathbb{RP}^{3}
. Then
\widetilde{S}^{\bdia}
is compact. We extend
G
to
\widetilde{S}^{\bdia}
by sending
s\in\Lambda
to the exterior normal to
\partial\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
at
s
. We claim that this extension is continuous. Indeed, let
p_{i}\in\widetilde{S}
converge to
s\in\Lambda
. Up to subsequence, we can assume that
p_{i}
belong to the orbit of a single cell
C
of
\mathscr{T}^{*}
. It is enough to assume that
p_{i}
are in the orbit of a vertex of
C
. Each
F(p_{i})
is in the interior of a face of
F
, let
\Pi_{i}
be the plane containing the face. All
\Pi_{i}
belong to the orbit of a plane
\Pi
. Up to subsequence,
\Pi_{i}
converge to a plane
\Pi^{\prime}
. Since
F(p_{i})
converge to
s
, we have
\Pi^{\prime}\ni s
. On the other hand, let
\widetilde{L}_{K}^{+}
be the
K
-surface tangent to
\Pi
. Then it is tangent to all
\Pi_{i}
. By Corollary
3.26
, any subsequence of
\Pi_{i}
can converge only to planes tangent to
\partial\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
at points of
\Lambda
. Hence,
\Pi^{\prime}
is the tangent plane to
\partial\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
at
s
. Then Lemma
3.10
implies that the initial sequence
\Pi_{i}
converges to
\Pi^{\prime}
. It follows that
G
is continuous.

Because
G
extends continuously to
\widetilde{S}^{\bdia}
, which is compact, and
G(\Lambda)=J
, we see that
G|_{\widetilde{S}}
is proper as a map to
J^{+}
. Since
G|_{\widetilde{S}}
is a local homeomorphism,
G|_{\widetilde{S}}
is a covering map onto
J^{+}
. But since
J^{+}
is simply connected, it is a homeomorphism.

We now claim that for every
s\in\Lambda
and every globally supporting plane
\Pi
to
F(\widetilde{S})\cup\Lambda
, its exterior normal
\nu
is in
J\cup J^{-}
. Indeed, suppose that
\nu\in J^{+}
. Then there exists
p\in F(\widetilde{S})
such that the parallel plane
\Pi^{\prime}
to
\Pi
at
p
is locally supporting
F(\widetilde{S})
. Let
\widetilde{L}^{+}_{K}
be the
K
-surface tangent to
\Pi^{\prime}
. Due to Corollary
3.26
,
\Pi^{\prime}
belongs to the side of
\nu
from
\Pi
. Then
p
belongs to the wrong side of
\Pi
, which is a contradiction.

We now claim that every locally supporting plane to
F(\widetilde{S})
is globally supporting. Indeed, pick such a plane
\Pi
with an exterior normal
\nu\in J^{+}
. Thus, there is corresponding
q\in\widetilde{S}
such that
G(q)=\nu
. Suppose that there are points of
F(\widetilde{S})
from both sides of
\Pi
. Consider points
p_{1}
,
p_{2}\in{\rm cl}(F(\widetilde{S}))=F(\widetilde{S})\cup\Lambda
on each side from
\Pi
that are at the maximal Euclidean distance from
\Pi
. The planes that are parallel to
\Pi
through
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
are globally supporting
F(\widetilde{S})\cup\Lambda
. Then the exterior normals to these planes are in the opposite directions. One of them is
\nu
, let it be at
p_{1}
. Then
p_{1}\notin\Lambda
. But then there is
q_{1}\in\widetilde{S}
,
q_{1}\neq q
, such that
G(q_{1})=G(q)=\nu
. It follows that
G
is not injective, which is a contradiction. In turn, this implies that
F
is injective and convex, i.e.,
F(\widetilde{S})\subset\partial{\rm conv}(F(\widetilde{S}))=\partial{\rm conv}(F(\widetilde{V}))
, and is equal to a component of
(\partial{\rm conv}(F(\widetilde{V})))\backslash\Lambda
. Note that it means that
F(\widetilde{S})
is future-convex for
\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
, so
F(\widetilde{S})=\Sigma(\widetilde{f}_{t})
. In particular,
\widetilde{f}_{t}
is strictly polyhedral and its face celluation is
\mathscr{T}
.

Now suppose that the face celluation of
\widetilde{f}
is not simplicial, denote it by
\mathscr{C}
. Let
\mathscr{T}_{1},\ldots,\mathscr{T}_{r}
be representatives of all weak equivalence classes of
\pi_{1}S
-invariant triangulations subdividing
\mathscr{C}
. We choose
t
small enough so that assumptions (1) and (2) work for all triangles of every
\mathscr{T}_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,r
, but the part of assumption (2) on the adjacent faces is meant only for the adjacent faces along the edges of
\mathscr{C}
. For a fixed
t
and a given non-triangular face
Q
of
\mathscr{C}
with vertex set
\widetilde{V}_{Q}
we look at
{\rm conv}(\widetilde{f}_{t}(\widetilde{V}_{Q}))
. Its future-convex part provides a decomposition of
Q
. We do this for a representative of the
\pi_{1}S
-orbit of every face. We obtain a celluation
\mathscr{C}_{t}
subdividing
\mathscr{C}
. Then the same argument as for the triangulation case shows that
\mathscr{C}_{t}
is the face celluation of
\widetilde{f}_{t}
.
∎

Fix an affine connection on
\mathcal{T}
, lift it to a
G
-invariant connection on
\mathcal{R}
. Together with the standard connection on
\mathbb{R}^{2,1}
, this produces an affine connection on
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}
. Denote by
\mathcal{E}:\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}
the exponential map. It produces a diffeomorphism between a pierced neighborhood
U
of
o_{-}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}
with a lower cone in
T_{o_{-}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}\cong\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}
. We say that this induces a lower-cone structure on
U
(based at
o_{-}
). We then make the blow-up on
U
, and glue it with the rest of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}
. Denote the resulting manifold with boundary by
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\vee}
. It is independent on the choice of connection on
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}
. We have an identification
\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\vee}\cong\mathbb{S}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0})
. Define
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\vee}^{w}:=\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{w}\cup\mathbb{S}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{w})\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\vee}
and in the same way define
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\vee}^{c}
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\vee}^{s}
. We have

Lemma 3.29
.

\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\vee}^{w}
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\vee}^{s}
are submanifolds with boundary of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\vee}
.

This follows from Lemmas
3.27
,
3.28
and

Lemma 3.30
.

In
\mathbb{R}^{m}
let
A
be an open subset of the upper half-space
\{x:x_{m}>0\}
, and
B
be an open subset of the hyperplane
\{x:x_{m}=0\}
. For
x\in B
let
r_{x}:[0,+\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
be the vertical positive ray based in
x
and parameterized by the
m
-th coordinate. Assume that for every
x\in B
there exists
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(x)
such that for all
t\in(0,\varepsilon)
we have
r_{x}(t)\in A
. Then
A\cup B
is a submanifold with boundary of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we suppose that
A\subset\{x:0<x_{m}<1\}
. Define
C:=\{x:0\leq x_{m}\leq 1\}
. Define a function
f:B\rightarrow[0,1]
by

f(x):=\inf\{y_{m}:y\in r_{x},y\notin A,y\neq x\}.

We have
0<f(x)\leq 1
. It is easy to see that
f
is lower semi-continuous. Indeed, if
x^{i}\rightarrow x
and
a=\liminf f(x^{i})
, then, up to subsequence, there exists
y^{i}
such that
y^{i}\in C\backslash A
,
y^{i}_{m}\rightarrow a
,
y^{i}\in r_{x^{i}}
. Then
y^{i}\rightarrow y
such that
y\in C\backslash A
,
y_{m}=a
and
y\in r_{x}
. Hence,
f(x)\leq a
.

This means that for every
x\in B
there exist a neighborhood
U
of
x
in
B
and
a>0
such that for all
x^{\prime}\in U
we have
f(x^{\prime})\geq a
. Hence, the subset

\{y:y\in r_{x^{\prime}}\textrm{~for~some~}x^{\prime}\in U,0\leq y_{m}<a\}

belongs to
A\cup B
. Thus,
A\cup B
is a submanifold with boundary.
∎

We also define
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\vee,sp}^{s}:=\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-,sp}^{s}\cup\mathbb{S}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{s})\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\vee}^{s}
. We can specify Lemma
3.29
.

Lemma 3.31
.

\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\vee,sp}^{s}
is a submanifold with boundary of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\vee}
.

The group
G
acts on
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}
smoothly, freely and properly, preserving the bundle structure
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}\cong\mathcal{R}\times(\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3})^{V}
. Denote the quotient by
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{\prime}\cong\mathcal{T}\times(\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3})^{V}
, and denote the image of
o_{-}
by
o_{-}^{\prime}
. The subspace
B
of coboundaries in
T_{\rho_{\circ}}\mathcal{R}
is naturally isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{2,1}
and is tangent to the
G
-orbits in
\mathcal{R}
. Denote by
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}_{0}
the quotient of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}
by
B
. Using the exponential map
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}:\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{\prime}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{\prime}
we define the blow-up
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}_{\vee}
with
\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}_{\vee}\cong\mathbb{S}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{\prime})
. It is easy to show that the projection
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\vee}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}_{\vee}
is a submersion. (Note that
G
does not act on
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\vee}
, thereby this statement is not immediate, but it is easy to see that the submersions
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{\prime}
and
\mathbb{S}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0})\rightarrow\mathbb{S}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{\prime})
glue together to a submersion.) The subset
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{w}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}
is
G
-invariant and projects to
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{w^{\prime}}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{\prime}
. Similarly, the subset
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{w}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}
is
B
-invariant and projects to
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{w^{\prime}}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{\prime}
. Define
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\vee}^{w^{\prime}}:=\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{w^{\prime}}\cup\mathbb{S}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{w^{\prime}})\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\vee}^{\prime}
, which is a submanifold with boundary.

The group
\pi_{1}S
acts on
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{w^{\prime}}
by coned maps, freely and properly discontinuously. Hence, the quotient
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{w}
is a coned manifold. Next,
\pi_{1}S
acts on both
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{w^{\prime}}
,
\mathbb{S}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{w^{\prime}})
by diffeomorphisms, freely and properly discontinuously. We claim that the action is smooth as the action on
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\vee}^{w^{\prime}}
. Indeed, let
\phi:\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{\prime}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{\prime}
be an action by an element
\gamma\in\pi_{1}S
. Note that it is smooth, and is a self-diffeomorphism on
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{w^{\prime}}
. Due to Lemma
3.27
, the exponential map
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
sends diffeomorphically some lower cone in
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{w^{\prime}}
spanning
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{w^{\prime}}
onto a set
X\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{w^{\prime}}
, which then inherits a lower-cone structure based at
o^{\prime}_{-}
. We have
\partial X_{\vee}\cong\mathbb{S}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{w^{\prime}})
. Pick
x\in\partial X_{\vee}
. Since
\phi
is smooth and fixes
o_{-}^{\prime}
, there exists a lower cone
Y\subset X
such that
\partial Y_{\vee}\ni x
and
\phi(Y)\subset X
. Since
\phi
is smooth on
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{\prime}
, the map
\grave{\phi}
on
Y\times[0,+\infty)
is smooth. Lemma
3.2
yields that
\phi
has a smooth blow-up
\phi_{\vee}
on
Y_{\vee}
. It is easy to check that the restriction of
\phi_{\vee}
on
\partial Y_{\vee}
coincides with the restriction of the action of
\gamma
on
\mathbb{S}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{w^{\prime}})
. Hence, the action of
\pi_{1}S
on
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\vee}^{w^{\prime}}
is smooth, and thereby it is a free properly discontinuous action by diffeomorphisms. Denote the quotient by
{\mathcal{P}}_{\vee}^{w}
. Its interior is identified with
\mathcal{P}_{-}^{w}
and its boundary is identified with
\mathbb{S}(\mathcal{P}_{0}^{w})
. The map
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\vee}^{w}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{w}
is a submersion. We define the subsets
\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{s}\subset\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{c}\subset\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{w}
in the obvious manner.

3.6
The intrinsic metric map

Recall the notion of
intrinsic metric
of spacelike convex surfaces in
\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
from Appendix
A.
.

Lemma 3.32
.

Consider
(\rho,f)\in\mathcal{P}_{-}^{c}
. The intrinsic metric
d
of
\Sigma(f)
is a concave hyperbolic cone-metric and
V(d)=V(f)
.

Proof.

Pick a lift
(\rho,\widetilde{f})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{c}
. Let
p\in\Sigma(\widetilde{f})
,
p\notin V(\widetilde{f})
. We will prove that the intrinsic metric of
\Sigma(\widetilde{f})
is locally isometric to
\mathbb{H}^{2}
at
p
. Pick a supporting plane
\Pi
to
\Sigma(\widetilde{f})
at
p
and a compact convex set
K\subset\Pi
such that
\Sigma(\widetilde{f})
is locally a graph over
K
via the exponential normal map
\mathcal{E}_{\Pi}
from
\Pi
, that this graph over
K
does not contain points of
V(\widetilde{f})
and that
p\in{\rm int}(K)
. Consider a sequence
\psi_{i}\subset K
of closed convex polygonal curves converging to
\partial K
uniformly so that they bound compact convex sets
K_{i}\subset K
with
p\in{\rm int}(K_{i})
for all
i
. There exists a compact convex set
K^{\prime}
such that for all
i
we have
K^{\prime}\subset K_{i}
and
p\in{\rm int}(K^{\prime})
.

Let
\psi^{\prime}_{i}
be the polygonal curves in
\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
obtained from connecting the vertices of
\psi_{i}
lifted to
\Sigma(\widetilde{f})
. Let
\Sigma_{i}
be the future-convex parts of
{\rm conv}(\psi^{\prime}_{i})
. We consider the pull-backs to
K^{\prime}
of the intrinsic metrics of the parts of
\Sigma_{i}
and
\Sigma(\widetilde{f})
that are cut out by
\mathcal{E}_{\Pi}(K^{\prime})
. Denote the obtained metrics on
K^{\prime}
by
d_{i}
,
d^{\prime}
. We note that a priori
d^{\prime}
might be not the same as the pull-back of
d
, the intrinsic metric of
\Sigma(\widetilde{f})
, as some of the shortest paths for
d
with endpoints in
K^{\prime}
can escape
K^{\prime}
. However, due to Lemma
A.8
, there exists a neighborhood
U_{p}\ni p
in
K^{\prime}
such that for all large enough
i
the shortest paths for
d_{i}
and
d^{\prime}
with endpoints in
U_{p}
belong to
K^{\prime}
. Hence, over
U_{p}
we have
d^{\prime}=d
. Furthermore, by construction, there are local isometries
\phi_{i}:(U_{p},d_{i})\rightarrow\mathbb{H}^{2}
. We normalize them so that
\phi_{i}(p)=q\in\mathbb{H}^{2}
for all
i
. Due to Lemma
A.7
,
d_{i}\rightarrow d
uniformly on
K^{\prime}
.
We apply the Arzelà–Ascoli theorem and obtain that, up to subsequence,
\phi_{i}
converge uniformly to a local isometry
\phi:(U_{p},d)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}^{2}
.

Now suppose that
p\in V(\widetilde{f})
. There exists its neighborhood
U
in
\Sigma(\widetilde{f})
such that no edges pass through
U
except those that have an end in
p
. A proof is the same as in the hyperbolic case, see
[
64
, Lemma 3.10]
. We can assume that
U
is the intersection of
\Sigma(\widetilde{f})
with a convex body. Then every point
q\in U
is connected to
p
by a segment that belongs to
U
. This means that
\Sigma(\widetilde{f})
contains a piece of the boundary of a convex cone based at
p
. Since
p
does not belong to the relative interior of a segment that belongs to
\Sigma(\widetilde{f})
, the curvature of this cone is non-zero. Hence,
d
is a concave hyperbolic cone-metric and
V(d)=V(f)
.
∎

The discussion from Section
3.4.5
allows to consider
d
as a hyperbolic cone-metric on
(S,V)
, defined up to an element of
\mathcal{H}
. Hence, we have the
intrinsic metric map

\mathcal{I}_{-}:\mathcal{P}_{-}^{c}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{-}^{c}.

Moreover, the restriction of
\mathcal{I}_{-}
to
\mathcal{P}_{-}^{s}
has values in
\mathcal{D}_{-}^{s}
. We will denote it by
\mathcal{I}_{-}^{s}
as a map
\mathcal{P}_{-}^{s}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{-}^{s}
.

Lemma 3.33
.

The map
\mathcal{I}_{-}
is continuous.

Proof.

Pick
x\in\mathcal{P}_{-}^{c}
, define
d:=\mathcal{I}_{-}(x)\in\mathcal{D}_{-}^{c}
. Take a geodesic triangulation
\mathscr{T}
of
(S,V,d)
. Since
d
is CAT(0), all edges are unique shortest paths between the vertices. Let
x_{i}\rightarrow x
in
\mathcal{P}_{-}^{c}
. Pick their lifts
(\rho_{i},\widetilde{f}_{i})
converging to a lift
(\rho,\widetilde{f})
of
x
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{c}
. Lemmas
3.14
and
3.15
imply that
{\rm conv}(\widetilde{f}_{i})\rightarrow{\rm conv}(\widetilde{f})
and
{\rm cl}(\Sigma(\widetilde{f}_{i}))\rightarrow{\rm cl}(\Sigma(\widetilde{f}))
. Furthermore, due to Lemma
3.11
,
\partial{\rm conv}(\widetilde{f}_{i})\backslash\Sigma(\widetilde{f}_{i})\rightarrow\partial{\rm conv}(\widetilde{f})\backslash\Sigma(\widetilde{f})
. Hence, we are in the setting of Lemma
A.2
. Let
\widetilde{\mathscr{T}}
be the preimage of
\mathscr{T}
on
\widetilde{S}
. We pick a fundamental domain for
\widetilde{\mathscr{T}}
. Due to Lemma
A.2
, up to subsequence, for every edge in the fundamental domain, the respective shortest paths on
\Sigma(\widetilde{f}_{i})
converge in the Hausdorff sense to the respective shortest paths on
\Sigma(\widetilde{f}_{i})
, and also their lengths converge. This means that
d_{i}
are weakly
\mathscr{T}
-triangulable and converge to
d
in
\mathcal{D}_{-}^{c}
. Due to Lemma
3.10
, this holds then for the initial sequence.
∎

Lemma 3.34
.

The map
\mathcal{I}_{-}
is
C^{1}
on
\mathcal{P}_{-,sp}^{s}
.

The proof is just the same as the proofs of similar statements in other settings
[
64
, Lemma 3.33]
,
[
63
, Lemma 2.18]
,
[
34
, Lemma 2.14]
.

We also have the intrinsic metric map

\mathcal{I}_{0}:\mathcal{P}_{0}^{c}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{0}^{c}.

It is clear that
\mathcal{I}_{0}
is coned. Since the elements of
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{c}
are strictly polyhedral and for
(\tau,f)\in\mathcal{P}_{0}^{c}
the face celluation of nearby elements of
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{c}
is a subdivision of the face celluation of
f
, it is easy to see that it is continuous. We similarly consider its restriction
\mathcal{I}_{0}^{s}:\mathcal{P}_{0}^{s}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{0}^{s}
. Then
[
34
, Theorem 1.4]
states that

Theorem
M1
.

The map
\mathcal{I}_{0}^{s}
is a
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism.

We glue together the maps
\mathcal{I}_{-}
and
\mathbb{S}(\mathcal{I}_{0})
into the map

{\mathcal{I}}_{\vee}:\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{c}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{c}.

Now we show

Lemma 3.35
.

The map
{\mathcal{I}}_{\vee}
is continuous and is
C^{1}
on
\mathcal{P}_{\vee,sp}^{s}
.

We will need the following lemmas.

Lemma 3.36
.

Let
p_{t},q_{t}:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
be two
C^{1}
-curves with
p_{0}=q_{0}=o
such that
\dot{p}_{0},\dot{q}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{2,1}
are in a spacelike position. Then for all small enough
t
,
p_{t}
and
q_{t}
are in a spacelike position, and
\dot{d}_{A}(p_{t},q_{t})
is the Minkowski distance between
\dot{p}_{0}
and
\dot{q}_{0}
.

This is a routine computation using the Taylor expression of the anti-de Sitter metric tensor in the normal coordinates with respect to
o
.

Lemma 3.37
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}}
be a domain,
f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}:U\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m_{2}}
be
C^{1}
-maps, and
\xi:U\rightarrow 2^{[r]}
be a function with the following properties

(1) if for
x\in U
we have
\xi(x)=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{p}\}
, then
f_{i_{1}}(x)=\ldots=f_{i_{p}}(x)
and
df_{i_{1},x}=\ldots=df_{i_{p},x}
(the latter means that the differentials coincide as maps);

(2) if a sequence
x_{i}\rightarrow x
and
j\in\xi(x_{i})
for all
i
, then
j\in\xi(x)
.

Define a map
f:U\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m_{2}}
such that for every
x\in U
we have
f(x)=f_{j}(x)
for
j\in\xi(x)
. (Due to condition (1), this is well-defined.) Then
f
is a
C^{1}
-function on
U
.

Proof.

By considering the coordinate functions, it is enough to verify this for
m_{2}=1
.
First, we check the case
m_{1}=1
. Let us see that for every
x
,
f
is differentiable at
x
with the derivative
f^{\prime}_{j}(x)
,
j\in\xi(x)
. Indeed, for every sequence
t_{i}\rightarrow 0
, the sequence
x+t_{i}
can be divided into finitely many subsequences such that
f(x+t_{i})=f_{j}(x+t_{i})
,
j\in\xi(x)
, provided that
t_{i}
are small enough. For every such subsequence the limit

\lim_{i\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{f(x+t_{i})-f(x)}{t_{i}}

exists and is equal to
f^{\prime}_{j}(x)
, which are equal for all
j\in\xi(x)
. Hence,
f
is differentiable at
x
. The continuity of the derivative is immediate.

For general
m_{1}
we show that
f
is differentiable at
x
with differential
df_{j,x}
,
j\in\xi(x)
. Indeed, for every differentiable curve
x_{t}
,
x_{0}=x
, the function
f(x_{t})
is differentiable at
t=0
with the derivative given by
df_{j,x}(\dot{x}_{0})
,
j\in\xi(x)
. The continuity of the differentials is immediate.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.35
.

Pick
x\in\partial\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{c}\cong\mathbb{S}(\mathcal{P}_{0}^{c})
and let
(\tau,f)\in\mathcal{P}_{0}^{c}
be a representative of
x
. Let
\mathscr{C}
be the face celluation of
f
. Let
\mathscr{T}_{1},\ldots,\mathscr{T}_{r}
be representatives of all weak equivalence classes of triangulations of
(S,V)
subdividing
\mathscr{C}
. For every
(\tau^{\prime},f^{\prime})\in\mathcal{P}_{0}^{w}
sufficiently close to
(\tau,f)
and every
j=1,\ldots,r
the position of
f^{\prime}(V)
in
\Omega_{\tau^{\prime}}
and the triangulation
\mathscr{T}_{j}
determine a (possibly non-convex) simplicial Cauchy surface
\Sigma_{j}(f^{\prime})\subset\Omega_{\tau^{\prime}}
. Let
Y
be a small neighborhood of
(\tau,f)
so that for all
(\tau^{\prime},f^{\prime})\in Y
we have (1) all the triangles of all
\Sigma_{j}(f^{\prime})
are spacelike, and (2) all the angles of the cells of
\mathscr{C}
in the intrinsic metric of
\Sigma_{j}(f^{\prime})
are smaller than
\pi
. These properties continue to hold for the lower cone spanned by
Y
in
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{w}
, which we will denote by
Y
from now on. Let
\mathcal{I}_{0,j}:Y\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{0}
be the map sending
(\tau^{\prime},f^{\prime})\in Y
to the intrinsic metric of
\Sigma_{j}(f^{\prime})
.

Let
(\tau,\widetilde{f})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{c}
be a lift of
(\tau,f)
and
\widetilde{Y}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{w}
be a lift of
Y
containing
(\tau,\widetilde{f})
. We possibly reduce
\widetilde{Y}
to a smaller one, which we will still denote by
\widetilde{Y}
, the restriction of the exponential map
\mathcal{E}
to
\widetilde{Y}
is a diffeomorphism onto the image
\widetilde{X}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{w}
with the following property. For every
(\rho^{\prime},\widetilde{f}^{\prime})\in\widetilde{X}
projecting to
(\rho^{\prime},f^{\prime})\in\mathcal{P}_{-}^{w}
and for every
j=1,\ldots,r
let
\Sigma_{j}(f^{\prime})\subset\Omega_{\rho^{\prime}}
be the simplicial surface determined by
f^{\prime}(V)
and
\mathscr{T}_{j}
. We require
\widetilde{Y}
to be small enough so that all such
\Sigma_{j}(f^{\prime})
have only spacelike triangles. The subset
\widetilde{X}
inherits a lower-cone structure based at
o_{-}
, hence it has a blow-up
\widetilde{X}_{\vee}
. The maps
\mathcal{I}_{0,j}
lift to the maps
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{0,j}:\widetilde{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{0}
. Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{-,j}:\widetilde{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{-}
be the map sending
(\rho^{\prime},\widetilde{f}^{\prime})\in\widetilde{X}
to the intrinsic metric of
\Sigma_{j}(f^{\prime})
. The map
\mathcal{D}^{\sharp}_{\vee}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\vee}
is a covering. We can assume that
\widetilde{Y}
is so small that the images of all
\widetilde{I}_{-,j}
,
\mathbb{S}(\widetilde{I}_{0,j})
belong to a simply-connected subset of
\mathcal{D}_{\vee}
. We fix a lift of this subset to
\mathcal{D}^{\sharp}_{\vee}
and using this lift we consider
\widetilde{I}_{-,j}
,
\widetilde{I}_{0,j}
valued in
\mathcal{D}_{-}^{\sharp}
,
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\sharp}
. Identify
\mathcal{D}_{-}^{\sharp}(\mathscr{T}_{j})
with the cone
\Phi^{\mathscr{T}_{j}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{E(\mathscr{T}_{j})}
, and consider
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{-,j}
valued there. The corresponding map
\grave{\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}}_{-,j}
on
\widetilde{X}\times[0,1)
is smooth, thereby by Lemma
3.2
the map
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{-,j}
has a smooth blow-up
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{\vee,j}:\widetilde{X}_{\vee}\rightarrow\Phi^{\mathscr{T}_{j}}_{\vee}
. From Lemma
3.36
, its restriction to
\partial\widetilde{X}_{\vee}\cong\mathbb{S}(\widetilde{Y})
coincides with
\mathbb{S}(\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{0,j})
, where
\partial\Phi^{\mathscr{T}_{j}}_{\vee}
is identified with
\mathbb{S}(\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\sharp}(\mathscr{T}_{j}))
.

In particular, this means that the intrinsic angles of the triangles of the surfaces
\Sigma_{j}(f_{i})
,
(\rho,f_{i})\in X
, where
X
is the projection of
\widetilde{X}
to
\mathcal{P}_{-}^{w}
, converge to those of
\Sigma_{j}(f)
as
(\rho,f_{i})\rightarrow x
. This implies that, possibly after again reducing
\widetilde{Y}
and
\widetilde{X}
, the condition (2) on the angles of
\mathscr{C}
is also true for all
\Sigma_{j}(f^{\prime})
,
(\rho^{\prime},f^{\prime})\in X
.
Pick an arbitrary triangulation
\mathscr{T}
from
\mathscr{T}_{1},\ldots,\mathscr{T}_{r}
. Condition (2) means that the intrinsic metrics of all
\Sigma_{j}(f^{\prime})
,
(\rho^{\prime},f^{\prime})\in X
, are weakly
\mathscr{T}
-triangulable. From now on we consider
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{-,j}
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{0,j}
valued in
\mathcal{D}_{-}^{\sharp}(\mathscr{T})
,
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\sharp}(\mathscr{T})
, which we identify with the cone
\Phi^{\mathscr{T}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{E(\mathscr{T})}
. Then we can consider the blow-ups
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{\vee,j}
valued in
\Phi^{\mathscr{T}}_{\vee}
. Now we define
X_{\vee}:=X\cup\mathbb{S}(Y)\subset\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{w}
and define maps
{\mathcal{I}}_{\vee,j}:X_{\vee}\rightarrow\Phi^{\mathscr{T}}_{\vee}
by means of the commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{X}_{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\mathscr{T}}_{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{\vee,j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{I}}_{\vee,j}}

The maps
{\mathcal{I}}_{\vee,j}
are smooth. Define
X_{\vee,j}\subset X_{\vee}
as the subset of those
(\rho^{\prime},f^{\prime})\in X
and the classes of those
(\tau^{\prime},f^{\prime})\in Y
that
\Sigma_{j}(f^{\prime})
is convex. In such case
\Sigma_{j}(f^{\prime})
coincides with
\Sigma(f^{\prime})
. Let
\xi:X_{\vee}\rightarrow 2^{[r]}
be the corresponding partition function of
X_{\vee}
. At the common points, the values of
{\mathcal{I}}_{\vee,j}
coincide, and coincide with
{\mathcal{I}}_{\vee}
. The differentials of
{\mathcal{I}}_{\vee,j}
also coincide at the common points. For the Minkowski case this is Claim 2 in the proof of
[
34
, Lemma 2.13]
, for the anti-de Sitter case the proof is just the same. Clearly, the partition function satisfies condition (2) from Lemma
3.37
. Thus, if
x\in\mathbb{S}(\mathcal{P}_{0}^{s})\cong\partial\mathcal{P}_{\vee,sp}
, then Lemma
3.37
implies that
{\mathcal{I}}_{\vee}
is
C^{1}
on
X_{\vee}
, particularly it is
C^{1}
at
x
. Otherwise,
{\mathcal{I}}_{\vee}
is continuous at
x
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Now we obtain

Lemma
AdS1
.

The differential of
\mathcal{I}_{\vee}
is non-degenerate on
\partial\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{s}
.

Proof.

Due to Theorem
M1
, the vectors tangent to
\partial\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{s}
do not belong to the kernel of
d\mathcal{I}_{\vee}
. For every
x\in\partial\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{s}
we now need to check any tangent vector at
x
transversal to
\partial\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{s}
.

For
\widetilde{y}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{s}
projecting to
y\in\mathcal{P}_{0}^{s}
let
\widetilde{y}_{t}
,
t\in[0,1]
, be the curve
t\widetilde{y}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}
. Let
\widetilde{x}_{t}:=\mathcal{E}(\widetilde{y}_{t})
. From Lemma
3.28
, for all small enough
t>0
we have
\widetilde{x}_{t}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{s}
. Let
x_{t}\in\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{s}
be its projection. This is a curve emanating from
x_{0}=x\in\partial\mathcal{P}_{\vee}
with a non-zero tangent vector transversal to
\partial\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{s}
. Suppose that the face decomposition of
y
is a triangulation
\mathscr{T}
. From Lemma
3.28

\mathscr{T}
is the face decomposition of
x_{t}
for all small enough
t
. Consider the map
\phi_{\vee}^{\mathscr{T}}\circ\mathcal{I}_{\vee}
on
x_{t}
with values in the cone
\Phi^{\mathscr{T}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{E(\mathscr{T})}
. This gives a curve
d_{t}
,
t>0
. From Lemma
3.36
, it extends at
t=0
to a curve
d_{t}
with
\dot{d}_{0}=\phi_{0}^{\mathscr{T}}\circ\mathcal{I}_{0}(y)
. Thereby,
d{\mathcal{I}}_{\vee}(\dot{x}_{0})\neq 0
.

When the face decomposition of
y
is not a triangulation, one has to use the maps
\mathcal{I}_{-,j}
from the proof of Lemma
3.35
. The argument from the paragraph above shows that
d{\mathcal{I}}_{\vee,j}(\dot{x}_{0})\neq 0
for all
j
. By using Lemma
3.37
, we deduce that this is true for
{\mathcal{I}}_{\vee}
restricted to the curve
x_{t}
.
It follows that
d{\mathcal{I}}_{\vee}
is injective at
x
.
∎

4
Proof

Denote by
\mathcal{I}_{\vee}^{s}:\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{s}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{s}
the restriction of
\mathcal{I}_{\vee}
. Note that
\mathcal{I}_{\vee}^{s}
is the main object of our study, but for several steps of the proof we will need to employ
\mathcal{I}_{\vee}
.
In the next section we will prove

Lemma
AdS2
.

The map
\mathcal{I}_{\vee}^{s}
is proper.

From this we can establish Theorem
AdS1
.

Proof of Theorem
AdS1
.

From Lemma
3.33
and Lemma
AdS2
,
\mathcal{I}_{\vee}^{s}
is a proper continuous map between manifolds with boundary
\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{s}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{s}
, which have the same dimension. By construction,
\mathcal{I}_{\vee}^{s}
sends the boundary to the boundary. Hence
\mathcal{I}_{\vee}^{s}
has a well-defined degree. From Lemma
3.35
and Lemma
AdS1
,
\mathcal{I}_{\vee}^{s}
is
C^{1}
at
\partial\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{s}
and has a non-degenerate differential. Furthermore, from Theorem
M1
, when restricted to
\partial\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{s}
, it is a diffeomorphism onto
\partial\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{s}
. Thereby, it has degree one, which implies the surjectivity.

Next, every
y\in\partial\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{s}
has a neighborhood
U_{\vee}(y)
in
\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{c}
such that every element of
U_{\vee}(y)
has only one
\mathcal{I}_{\vee}^{s}
-preimage. Indeed, otherwise there exists a sequence
y_{i}
converging to
y
such that every
y_{i}
has at least 2 preimages. Pick two of them for each
y_{i}
, denote them by
x_{i}
and
x^{\prime}_{i}
. From Lemma
AdS2
, up to subsequence, they converge to
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{s}
. Due to continuity of
\mathcal{I}_{\vee}^{s}
and Theorem
M1
, we have
x=x^{\prime}\in\partial\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{s}
. But Lemma
AdS1
and the inverse function theorem imply that
\mathcal{I}_{\vee}^{s}
is locally injective around
x=x^{\prime}
, which is a contradiction.

The union of all
U_{\vee}(y)
for
y\in\partial\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{s}
is an open set
U_{\vee}
such that every element of
U_{\vee}
has only one
\mathcal{I}_{\vee}^{s}
-preimage. It follows that
U:=U_{\vee}\cap\mathcal{D}_{-}^{s}
is the desired set.
∎

Remark 4.1
.

We note that it is natural to call
U
a “neighborhood of zero” in
\mathcal{D}_{-}^{s}
. It is helpful, however, to distinguish between “strong” and “weak” neighborhoods of zero. Consider the space
\mathcal{D}_{-}^{\bullet}
, obtained from
\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{s}
by contracting the boundary to a single point
\bullet
, “the origin”, endowed with the quotient topology. We call this topology
strong
. In the sense of this topology, the set
U\cup\{\bullet\}
is indeed a neighborhood of
\bullet
in
\mathcal{D}_{-}^{\bullet}
.

There is, however, a natural weaker topology on
\mathcal{D}_{-}^{\bullet}
. Recall the atlas
\{\phi^{\mathscr{T}}_{-}\}
on
\mathcal{D}_{-}^{s}
from Section
3.3
, given by all triangulations
\mathscr{T}
of
(S,V)
. Every map
\phi^{\mathscr{T}}_{-}
can be naturally extended to
\bullet
by sending it to the origin of
\mathbb{R}^{E(\mathscr{T})}
. We now call
Y\subset\mathcal{D}_{-}^{\bullet}
open if and only if for every
\mathscr{T}
the intersection of
Y
with the domain of
\phi^{\mathscr{T}}_{-}
(extended to
\bullet
) is open as a subset of the image of
\phi^{\mathscr{T}}_{-}
in the induced topology from
\mathbb{R}^{E(\mathscr{T})}
. We call the obtained topology on
\mathcal{D}_{-}^{\bullet}

weak
. One can observe that the weak topology is indeed strictly weaker than the strong topology. To this purpose one may consider a single cone
C
in a vector space
X
with the origin
o
. The topology on
C\cup\{o\}
induced from
X
is strictly weaker than the topology obtained from contracting the boundary of
C_{\vee}
.

It would be tempting to interpret the uniqueness part of Theorem
AdS1
, e.g., as that there exists
r=r(h)
such that if for
d\in\mathcal{D}_{-}^{s}
its diameter is
<r
, then the realization of
d
is unique. However, this is not true, because the sets of metrics with diameter less than given
r
form a base of the neighborhoods of
\bullet
in the weak topology, but not in the strong topology.

5
Properness

The goal of this section is to prove Lemma
AdS2
. Let us reformulate it as

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
x_{i}\in\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{s}
be a sequence such that
y_{i}:={\mathcal{I}}_{\vee}(x_{i})
converge to
y\in\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{s}
. Then, up to subsequence,
x_{i}
converge to
x\in\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{s}
.

The proof is quite different depending on whether
y\in\mathcal{D}_{-}^{s}
or
y\in\partial\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{s}
with the latter case being more difficult. We note also that since the behavior of
{\mathcal{I}}_{\vee}
is completely understood on
\partial\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{s}
due to Theorem
M1
, it is enough to assume that all
x_{i}\in\mathcal{P}_{-}^{s}
, hence we can denote them by
(\rho_{i},f_{i})
. Also then
y_{i}\in\mathcal{D}_{-}^{s}
, and we can denote them by
d_{i}
.

5.1
Convergence of holonomies away from blow-up

The goal of this subsection is to obtain

Lemma 5.2
.

Under the conditions of Lemma
5.1
, let
y
be in
\mathcal{D}_{-}^{s}
(and we denote it by
d
). Then, up to subsequence,
\rho_{i}
converge to
\rho\in\mathcal{T}
.

For a measured geodesic lamination
\lambda
on
S
we denote by
E^{+}_{\lambda}:\mathcal{T}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}
the right earthquake map and by
E^{-}_{\lambda}:\mathcal{T}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}
the left earthquake map, see, e.g.,
[
56
, Section 7.2]
for a definition. Recall that the earthquake maps are continuous both with respect to the laminations and the metrics. We will need to employ the Kerckhoff–Thurston earthquake theorem
[
49
]
:

Theorem 5.3
.

For every
\rho_{0},\rho_{1}\in\mathcal{T}
there exist unique
\lambda^{+}
,
\lambda^{-}
such that
E^{+}_{\lambda^{+}}(\rho_{0})=\rho_{1}
and
E^{-}_{\lambda^{-}}(\rho_{0})=\rho_{1}
.

Fix
\rho\in\mathcal{T}
. Let
\rho^{+}=\rho^{+}(\rho),\rho^{-}=\rho^{-}(\rho)
be the points in
\mathcal{T}
corresponding to the intrinsic metrics of
\partial^{+}C_{\rho}
and
\partial^{-}C_{\rho}
. Let
\lambda^{+}=\lambda^{+}(\rho)
and
\lambda^{-}=\lambda^{-}(\rho)
be the bending laminations of
\partial^{+}C_{\rho}
and
\partial^{-}C_{\rho}
, introduced in Section
3.4.5
. Recall the fundamental result of Mess
[
57
]
:

Theorem 5.4
.

We have

E^{+}_{\lambda^{+}}(\rho_{\circ})=\rho^{+},~~~~~E^{+}_{\lambda^{+}}(\rho^{+})=\rho,

E^{-}_{\lambda^{-}}(\rho)=\rho^{-},~~~~~E^{-}_{\lambda^{-}}(\rho^{-})=\rho_{\circ}.

Recall from Appendix
A.
that for a convex Cauchy surface
\Sigma\subset\Omega_{\rho}
and
\gamma\in\pi_{1}S
,
l_{\Sigma}(\gamma)
is the infimum of lengths of curves in the free homotopy class of
\gamma
on
\Sigma
. Similarly, for
\rho\in\mathcal{T}
and
\gamma\in\pi_{1}S
define
l_{\rho}(\gamma)
to be the length of the closed geodesic in the class of
\gamma
in the hyperbolic metric on
S
determined by
\rho
. We can show

Lemma 5.5
.

For every future-convex Cauchy surface
\Sigma\subset\Omega_{\rho}
and every
\gamma\in\pi_{1}S
we have
l_{\Sigma}(\gamma)\leq l_{\rho^{+}}(\gamma)
.

Proof.

Pick
\gamma\in\pi_{1}S
.
By Lemma
B.3
,
\Sigma
can be approximated by smooth strictly future-convex surfaces. Due to Lemma
A.15
, for arbitrary
\varepsilon
there exists such a surface
\Sigma^{\prime}
with
l_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(\gamma)\geq l_{\Sigma}-\varepsilon
. A strictly future-convex surface necessarily belongs to the strict past of
\partial^{+}C_{\rho}
. Hence, Lemma
B.1
implies that
l_{\rho^{+}}(\gamma)\geq l_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(\gamma)\geq l_{\Sigma}-\varepsilon
. Since
\varepsilon
is arbitrary, this finishes the proof.
∎

Now for a metric
d
on
S
we define its length function
l_{d}:\pi_{1}S\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
in the obvious way.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
d_{i}\in\mathcal{D}_{-}
be a precompact sequence and
\rho_{i}\in\mathcal{T}
be such that for every
i
and every
\gamma\in\pi_{1}S
we have

l_{d_{i}}(\gamma)\leq l_{\rho_{i}}(\gamma).

Then the sequence
\rho_{i}
is precompact in
\mathcal{T}
.

Proof.

Since
\{d_{i}\}
is precompact, for any
\rho\in\mathcal{T}
we can choose representatives of the metrics
d_{i}
on
S
and a hyperbolic metric
h
representing
\rho
so that there exists
C>0
and the identity map
(S,h)\rightarrow(S,d_{i})
is
C
-Lipschitz. Hence, for every
\gamma\in\pi_{1}S
we have

l_{d_{i}}(\gamma)\leq Cl_{\rho}(\gamma).

We can assume that
C>1
. Recall that for
\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}
the asymmetric Thurston distance is defined as

d_{{\rm Th}}(\rho^{\prime},\rho):=\sup_{\gamma\in\pi_{1}S}\ln\frac{l_{\rho^{\prime}}(\gamma)}{l_{\rho}(\gamma)},

see
[
79
]
. Hence,
d_{{\rm Th}}(\rho,\rho_{i})\leq\ln C
. Thus, by
[
60
]
,
\{\rho_{i}\}
is precompact.
∎

Now we have all in hands to prove Lemma
5.2
.

Proof of Lemma
5.2
.

By Lemma
5.5
, for every
i
and every
\gamma\in\pi_{1}S
we have
l_{d_{i}}(\gamma)\leq l_{\rho^{+}_{i}}(\gamma)
, where
\rho^{+}_{i}=\rho^{+}(\rho_{i})
. Hence, Lemma
5.6
implies that
\rho^{+}_{i}
belong to a compact subset of
\mathcal{T}
. Thereby, up to subsequence,
\rho^{+}_{i}
converge to
\rho^{+}\in\mathcal{T}
. Due to Theorem
5.3
and the continuity of the earthquake map, the bending laminations
\lambda^{+}_{i}=\lambda^{+}(\rho_{i})
converge to a measured lamination
\lambda^{+}
. Due to Theorem
5.4
,
\rho_{i}
converge to
\rho\in\mathcal{T}
. ∎

5.2
Convergence of holonomies at the blow-up

Here we show

Lemma 5.7
.

Under the conditions of Lemma
5.1
, let
y
be in
\partial\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{s}
. Then
\rho_{i}
converge to
\rho_{\circ}
.

First we make a quick excursion into group actions on (metric) trees. Set
\Gamma:=\pi_{1}S
. A pair of a metric tree
\Psi
and of a
\Gamma
-action on
\Psi
by isometries is called a
\Gamma
-tree. We will denote the pair by
\Psi
, assuming implicitly some
\Gamma
-action. A
\Gamma
-tree is
minimal
if it does not contain a proper
\Gamma
-invariant subtree. It is called
small
if the stabilizer of each arc is cyclic. For a
\Gamma
-tree
\Psi
we denote by
l_{\Psi}:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
the
length function
of
\Psi
, i.e., for
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
l_{\Psi}(\gamma)
is equal to

\inf_{p\in\Psi}d(p,\gamma p).

The
\Gamma
-equivariant isometry class of a small minimal
\Gamma
-tree
\Psi
is determined by
l_{\Psi}

[
74
]
(furthermore, finitely many
\gamma
are enough to distinguish it). We topologize the space of such classes by its embedding into
\mathbb{R}^{\Gamma}
via the length functions. Denote the resulting space by
\mathcal{MT}
. Note that there is a natural
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
-action on
\mathcal{MT}
by multiplication. There is a distinguished degenerate tree
\Psi_{0}\in\mathcal{MT}
consisting of a single point, which serves as an origin. Consequently,
\Psi_{0}
is distinguished by that
l_{\Psi_{0}}=0
.

Given a measured geodesic lamination
\lambda
on
S
, there is a natural construction of a dual
\Gamma
-tree to
\lambda
, see, e.g.,
[
48
, Chapter 11]
. In particular, the degenerate tree
\Psi_{0}
corresponds to the empty lamination. Recall that measured geodesic laminations form a space
\mathcal{ML}
with a natural
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
-action. Skora showed in
[
74
]

Theorem 5.8
.

This construction provides a
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
-equivariant homeomorphism
\mathcal{ML}\cong\mathcal{MT}
.

Consider now
\rho\in\mathcal{R}
and the domain
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}\subset\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
. Recall that we divide its anti-de Sitter boundary into two components, the future-convex one
\partial^{+}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}
and the past-convex one
\partial^{-}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}
. They are, however, not spacelike. Let us say that the set of points of
\partial^{\pm}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}
that admit a spacelike supporting plane is the
spacelike part
of
\partial^{\pm}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}
. The spacelike part of each component is a
\Gamma
-tree. We denote the one on
\partial^{+}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}
by
\Psi^{+}(\rho)
. A geometric observation shows that it is dual to
\lambda^{-}(\rho)
via the Skora duality, see
[
8
]
.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
\rho\in\mathcal{T}
,
\Sigma\subset\Omega_{\rho}
be a future-convex Cauchy surface. Then for every
\gamma\in\pi_{1}S
, we have
l_{\Psi^{+}(\rho)}(\gamma)\leq l_{\Sigma}(\gamma)
.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.24
, there exists a
K
-surface
L_{K}
in the strict past of
\Sigma
. From Lemma
B.1
,
l_{L_{K}}(\gamma)\leq l_{\Sigma}(\gamma)
. Also from Lemma
B.1
, the function
l_{L_{K}}(\gamma)
is decreasing in
K
. It follows from the results of Belaraouti
[
8
, Theorem 2.5]
that
l_{\Sigma_{K}}\rightarrow l_{\Psi^{+}(\rho)}
as
K\rightarrow\infty
. This implies that
l_{\Psi^{+}(\rho)}(\gamma)\leq l_{\Sigma}(\gamma)
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.7
.

Since
d_{i}\rightarrow y\in\partial\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{s}
, for all
\gamma\in\pi_{1}S
we have
l_{d_{i}}(\gamma)\rightarrow 0
. Thereby, Lemma
5.9
implies that
l_{\Psi^{+}(\rho_{i})}\rightarrow 0
. Hence,
\Psi^{+}(\rho_{i})\rightarrow\Psi_{0}
and, due to Theorem
5.8
,
\lambda^{-}(\rho_{i})
converge to the empty lamination. It follows from Theorem
5.4
that
\rho_{i}
converge to
\rho_{\circ}
.
∎

5.3
Cosmological time and canonical decomposition

In order to finish the proof of Lemma
5.1
, we need an important tool, namely
the cosmological time
. It was introduced in
[
2
]
, its significance for the study of Minkowski spacetimes was demonstrated in
[
10
,
15
]
. For a simultaneous treatment of the cosmological time in Minkowski and anti-de Sitter geometries we refer to
[
9
]
.

Pick
\rho\in\mathcal{R}
. As we discussed in Section
5.2
, the spacelike part of
\partial^{+}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}
is a metric tree
\Psi^{+}
dual to
\lambda^{-}
. Denote this spacelike part by
\partial^{+}_{s}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}
. The cosmological time of
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}
is a function
\textsc{ct}_{\rho}:\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{>0}
that assigns to
p\in\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}
the supremum of lengths of the timelike segments
pq
with
q\in\partial\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}
where
\vec{pq}
is directed to the past of
p
. Note that there exists a unique
q
such that
pq
realizes
\textsc{ct}_{\rho}(p)
. We denote such
q
by
\eta_{\rho}(p)
. By construction of
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}
, note that
\eta_{\rho}(p)\in\partial^{+}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}
. Furthermore,
\eta_{\rho}(p)\in\partial^{+}_{s}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}
. See
[
9
, Proposition 6.3.7]
. The function
\textsc{ct}_{\rho}
is
\pi_{1}S
-invariant and projects to a function on
\Omega_{\rho}
, which we continue to denote by
\textsc{ct}_{\rho}
. Similarly for
\tau\in T_{\rho_{\circ}}\mathcal{R}
one defines
\partial^{+}_{s}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\tau}
,
\textsc{ct}_{\tau}
and
\eta_{\tau}
for
\widetilde{\Omega}^{+}_{\tau}
. We will denote the
r
-level surface of
\textsc{ct}_{\rho}
or
\textsc{ct}_{\tau}
by
L_{r}(\rho)
,
L_{r}(\tau)
respectively.
When
\rho
or
\tau
is clear from the context, we sometimes write just
ct
,
\eta
,
L_{r}
.

Recall that
\partial^{-}C_{\rho}
is the past-convex boundary component of
C_{\rho}
. It turns out that
\partial^{-}C_{\rho}=L_{\pi/2}(\rho)
. The function
\textsc{ct}_{\rho}
is
C^{1,1}
on the past of
\partial^{-}C_{\rho}
, see, e.g.,
[
18
, Lemma 4.3]
. On the other hand,
\textsc{ct}_{\tau}
is
C^{1,1}
everywhere on
\Omega^{+}_{\tau}
, see, e.g.,
[
9
, Proposition 3.3.3]
.
We will need the following result of Bonsante
[
15
, Theorem 6.7]
:

Lemma 5.10
.

Consider
\tau\in T_{\rho_{\circ}}\mathcal{R}
. There exists a neighborhood
U\ni\tau
in
T_{\rho_{\circ}}\mathcal{R}
and a continuous map
\Phi:U\times\widetilde{S}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2,1}
such that

(1) for every
\tau^{\prime}\in U
,
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, the map
\Phi(\tau^{\prime},.,r):\widetilde{S}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2,1}
is a
\theta_{\tau^{\prime}}
-equivariant map onto
L_{r}(\tau^{\prime})
;

(2) for every
\tau^{\prime}\in U
,
p\in\widetilde{S}
, the set
\Phi(\tau^{\prime},p,\mathbb{R}_{>0})
is a gradient line of
\textsc{ct}_{\tau^{\prime}}
.

Note that Bonsante does not formulate explicitly these properties of his map, but they follow from his proof. We will also need an anti-de Sitter version of this. The proof of Bonsante mildly uses some special features of Minkowski geometry, thus we give our account of the proof, following the ideas of Bonsante.

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
\rho_{i}\rightarrow\rho
in
\mathcal{R}
and
p_{i}\rightarrow p
in
\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
, where
p\in\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}
. Define
\eta_{i}:=\eta_{\rho_{i}}
,
\textsc{ct}_{i}:=\textsc{ct}_{\rho_{i}}
. Then
\eta_{i}(p_{i})\rightarrow\eta(p)
and
\textsc{ct}_{i}(p_{i})\rightarrow\textsc{ct}(p)
. (Note that due to Corollary
3.8
,
\eta_{i}(p_{i})
and
\textsc{ct}_{i}(p_{i})
are well-defined for all large enough
i
.)

Proof.

Let
C_{i}
and
C
be the sets of points in
\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
that are in causal relation to
p_{i}
,
p
respectively. Then
{\rm cl}(C_{i})
converge to
{\rm cl}(C)
as subsets of
\mathbb{RP}^{3}
. Note that
\partial^{+}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}\cap C
is compact. Let
K
be its compact neighborhood in
\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
.
Pass to a subsequence realizing
\limsup\textsc{ct}_{i}(p_{i})
.
By Lemma
3.12
,
{\rm cl}(\partial^{+}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho_{i}})
converge to
{\rm cl}(\partial^{+}\Omega_{\rho})
. Hence for all large enough
i
the points
\eta_{i}(p_{i})
belong to
K
. Thereby, up to subsequence, they converge to a point
q\in\partial^{+}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}
. We get

\textsc{ct}(p)\geq\limsup\textsc{ct}_{i}(p_{i}).

On the other hand, since
{\rm cl}(\partial^{+}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho_{i}})
converge in the Hausdorff sense to
{\rm cl}(\partial^{+}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho})
, there exists a sequence
q_{i}\in{\rm cl}(\partial^{+}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho_{i}})
such that
q_{i}
converge to
\eta(p)
. Thereby,

\textsc{ct}(p)\leq\liminf\textsc{ct}_{i}(p_{i}).

Thus,
\lim\textsc{ct}_{i}(p_{i})=\textsc{ct}(p)
and
q=\eta(p)
. The latter means that
\eta_{i}(p_{i})\rightarrow\eta(p)
.
∎

Lemma 5.12
.

Consider
\rho\in\mathcal{R}
. There exists a neighborhood
U\ni\rho
in
\mathcal{R}
and a continuous map
\Phi:U\times\widetilde{S}\times(0,\frac{\pi}{2})\rightarrow\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
such that

(1) for every
\rho^{\prime}\in U
,
r\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
, the map
\Phi(\rho^{\prime},.,r):\widetilde{S}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
is a
\theta_{\rho^{\prime}}
-equivariant map onto
L_{r}(\rho^{\prime})
;

(2) for every
\rho^{\prime}\in U
,
p\in\widetilde{S}
, the set
\Phi(\rho^{\prime},p,(0,\frac{\pi}{2}))
is a gradient line of
\textsc{ct}_{\rho^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

Pick a smooth
\theta_{\rho}
-invariant Cauchy surface in
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}
and parameterize it as the image of a
\theta_{\rho}
-equivariant embedding
\phi(\rho,.):\widetilde{S}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
.
By the Ehresmann–Thurston theorem
[
22
, Theorem 1.7.1]
, there exists a neighborhood
U
of
\rho
in
\mathcal{R}
and a smooth map
\phi:U\times\widetilde{S}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
