such that for every
\rho^{\prime}\in U
the map
\phi(\rho^{\prime},.)
is a
\theta_{\rho^{\prime}}
-equivariant embedding. Provided that
U
is small enough, the resulting surfaces are spacelike. Thus, their images in
\Omega_{\rho^{\prime}}
are embedded compact spacelike surfaces, hence they are Cauchy. Now for every
\rho^{\prime}\in U
,
p\in\widetilde{S}
and
r\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
we define
\Phi(\rho^{\prime},p,r)
to be the point on the
r
-level surface of
\textsc{ct}_{\rho^{\prime}}
that is on the same gradient line of
\textsc{ct}_{\rho^{\prime}}
as
\phi(\rho^{\prime},p)
. One can check that due to Lemma
5.11
, the map
\Phi
is continuous. By construction, it satisfies the desired properties.
∎

We will need a compactification of some ends of
\mathcal{P}_{-}^{c}
. Pick a compact neighborhood
U_{-}
of
\rho_{\circ}
in
\mathcal{T}
, let
\widetilde{U}_{-}
be its lift to a compact neighborhood of
\rho_{\circ}
in
\mathcal{R}
. Let
\mathcal{P}_{-}^{c}(U_{-})
be the subset of
(\rho,f)\in\mathcal{P}_{-}^{c}
where
\rho\in U_{-}
. Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{c}(\widetilde{U}_{-})
be the similarly defined subset of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}
. We need to describe a compactification of
\mathcal{P}_{-}^{c}(U_{-})
. First, notice a natural compactification coming from the closure of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{c}(\widetilde{U}_{-})
in
\mathcal{R}\times(\mathbb{RP}^{3})^{V}
: it consists in adding to
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{c}(\widetilde{U}_{-})
the configurations
(\rho,\widetilde{f})
such that (1)
\widetilde{f}
is not necessarily injective and values in
{\rm cl}(\widetilde{\Omega}^{+}_{\rho})
, where
{\rm cl}(.)
is the closure in
\mathbb{RP}^{3}
, and (2)
\widetilde{f}
is in a convex position. This is a compactification of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{c}(\widetilde{U}_{-})
and projects to a compactification of
\mathcal{P}_{-}^{c}(U)
. However, we will need a rougher one. Namely, let
\partial_{s}^{+}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}
be the spacelike part of
\partial^{+}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}
. To obtain a compactification of
\mathcal{P}_{-}^{c}(U)
, it is enough to replace (1) by the condition that
\widetilde{f}
values in
\widetilde{\Omega}^{+}_{\rho}\cup\partial^{+}\widetilde{C}_{\rho}\cup\partial^{+}_{s}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}
. Heuristically, this is because the action of
\pi_{1}S
on
\partial^{+}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}
is not proper and the quotient space is not Hausdorff. Let us add such configurations to
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{c}(\widetilde{U}_{-})
and denote the obtained topological space by
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{\bdia}(\widetilde{U}_{-})
. Denote the quotient space by
{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{\bdia}(U_{-})
.

Lemma 5.13
.

The space
{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{\bdia}(U_{-})
is compact.

Proof.

It is enough to consider the case of
|V|=1
, so
V=\{v\}.

Consider a sequence
(\rho_{i},f_{i})\in{\mathcal{P}}_{-}(U_{-})
. Lift
\rho_{i}
to
\widetilde{U}_{-}
, assume that, up to subsequence, they converge to
\rho\in\widetilde{U}_{-}
. Pick a neighborhood
U
of
\rho
and a map
\Phi:U\times\widetilde{S}\times(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
from Lemma
5.12
. Pick a compact fundamental domain
D\subset\widetilde{S}
for the
\pi_{1}S
-action on
\widetilde{S}
. Then for every
\rho^{\prime}\in U
,
\Phi(\rho^{\prime},D,(0,\frac{\pi}{2}))
is a fundamental domain for the
\theta_{\rho^{\prime}}
-action on the past of
\partial^{-}\widetilde{C}_{\rho^{\prime}}
in
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho^{\prime}}
. Denote this domain by
D_{\rho^{\prime}}
. Using Lemma
5.11
, one sees that
{\rm cl}(D_{\rho_{i}})
converge in the Hausdorff sense to
{\rm cl}(D_{\rho})
as subsets of
\mathbb{RP}^{3}
. Pick a representative
\widetilde{f}_{i}
of
f_{i}
such that
\widetilde{f}_{i}(v)\in D_{\rho_{i}}
. Then, up to subsequence,
\widetilde{f}_{i}(v)
converge to
\widetilde{f}(v)\in{\rm cl}(D_{\rho})
. But
({\rm cl}(D_{\rho})\backslash\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho})\subset\partial^{+}_{s}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}
, which finishes the proof.
∎

Now for
\rho\in\mathcal{R}
we describe the the canonical decomposition of
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}
, following
[
5
, Section 5.4]
. More exactly, it is the decomposition of the strict past of
\partial^{-}\widetilde{C}_{\rho}
. Every
p\in\partial^{+}_{s}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}
determines a block defined as the intersection of
\eta_{\rho}^{-1}(p)
with the strict past of
\partial^{-}\widetilde{C}_{\rho}
. If
p
corresponds to a vertex of the respective metric tree, the block is called
solid
, otherwise it is called
thin
. For every edge of the metric tree the union of the respective thin blocks
is called a
Misner block
. In turn, this projects to a decomposition of
\Omega_{\rho}
.

Let
\lambda^{-}
be the bending lamination of
\partial^{-}C_{\rho}
. From the viewpoint of the geometry of
\partial^{-}C_{\rho}
, the isolated components of
\lambda^{-}
correspond to the Misner blocks, every non-isolated component of
\lambda^{-}
determines a thin block, which does not belong to any Misner block, and the components of
\partial^{-}C_{\rho}\backslash\lambda^{-}
correspond to the thick blocks.

5.4
Convergence of marked points away from blow-up

In this subsection we prove Lemma
5.1
, provided that
y\in\mathcal{D}_{-}
(and hence we denote it by
d
).
The main technical tool is the following result.

Lemma 5.14
.

For every
\varepsilon>0
and every compact set
U\subset\mathcal{T}
there exists
\alpha>0
such that for every future-convex Cauchy surface
\Sigma\subset\Omega_{\rho}
,
\rho\in U
, if
\sys(\Sigma)\geq\varepsilon
, then

\inf_{p\in\Sigma}\textsc{ct}_{\rho}(p)\geq\alpha.

Note that when
U
is a point, this is the anti-de Sitter part of
[
5
, Theorem 3.5]
. There seem to be small inaccuracies in their proof in the anti-de Sitter case, which, however, are easy to fix. Namely,
[
5
, Proposition 6.1]
relies on the fact that, for a given
\rho\in\mathcal{R}
, the maps
\zeta_{r_{1},r_{2}}:L_{r_{1}}\rightarrow L_{r_{2}}
,
r_{1}>r_{2}
, along the gradient flow of
ct
are 1-Lipschitz. The paper
[
15
]
is cited, which, however, tackles only the Minkowski situation. This claim is actually wrong in anti-de Sitter geometry, as one can see by considering a Misner block, for which the metric can be written explicitly and the tangent vectors transverse to the foliation expand in the wrong direction. Nevertheless, the correction is

Lemma 5.15
.

The map
\zeta_{r_{1},r_{2}}
is
\cos^{-1}(r_{1})
-Lipschitz.

This is shown in
[
8
, Proposition 6.13]
. This helps us with a corrected version of
[
5
, Proposition 6.1]
.

Lemma 5.16
.

Let
0<r<\pi/2
,
\chi:[0,1]\rightarrow\Omega_{\rho}
be a spacelike rectifiable curve in the past of
L_{r}
and
\chi^{\prime}
be its projection to
L_{r}
along the gradient flow of
ct
. Then

{\rm len}(\chi)\leq{\rm len}(\chi^{\prime})\cos^{-1}(r);

|\textsc{ct}(\chi(0))-\textsc{ct}(\chi(1))|\leq{\rm len}(\chi^{\prime})\cos^{-1}(r).

The next lemma is
[
5
, Proposition 6.2]
. Despite its proof in
[
5
]
relies on
[
5
, Proposition 6.1]
, which, as we mentioned, should be corrected, the proof of
[
5
, Proposition 6.2]
is correct as it is because it uses
[
5
, Proposition 6.1]
only inside solid and thin blocks, where the claim of
[
5
, Proposition 6.1]
actually holds as stated.

Lemma 5.17
.

Let
0<r<\pi/2
,
\chi:[0,1]\rightarrow\Omega_{\rho}
be a spacelike rectifiable curve in the past of
L_{r}
and
\chi^{\prime}
be its projection to
L_{r}
along the gradient flow of
ct
. Assume that
\chi
belongs to a single block of the canonical decomposition. Then for an absolute constant
C>1
we have

C^{-1}\exp^{-1}({\rm len}(\chi^{\prime}))\leq\frac{\textsc{ct}(\chi(0))}{\textsc{ct}(\chi(1))}\leq C\exp({\rm len}(\chi^{\prime})),

{\rm len}(\chi)\leq C{\rm len}(\chi^{\prime})\exp({\rm len}(\chi^{\prime}))\textsc{ct}(\chi(0)).

From these we deduce

Lemma 5.18
.

Let
\rho\in\mathcal{T}
,
0<r<\pi/2
and let
\Sigma\subset\Omega_{\rho}
be a future-convex Cauchy surface in the past of
L_{r}
. Denote by
A_{r}
the area of
L_{r}
and denote by
\sys(\Sigma)
the systole of
\Sigma
. Let
\delta
be the diameter of the hyperbolic metric on
S
given by
\rho^{-}
. Then for an absolute constant
C>0
we have

\inf_{p\in\Sigma}\textsc{ct}(p)\geq\frac{C\exp(-\delta)\sys^{2}(\Sigma)\cos(r)}{A_{r}\exp\left(\frac{2A_{r}}{\sys(\Sigma)\cos(r)}\right)}.

A proof is identical to the proof of the counterpart in Minkowski geometry given in
[
34
, Section 3.4.2]
, provided that we use Lemma
5.16
instead of
[
34
, Lemma 3.21]
, which is different only in multiplication by
\cos^{-1}(r)
. We will also need an expression for
A_{r}
, see
[
9
, p. 188]
. To state it, recall that the length functions of hyperbolic metrics extend from
\pi_{1}S
to the length functions over
\mathcal{ML}
.

Lemma 5.19
.

We have

A_{r}=-2\pi\sin^{2}(r)\chi(S)+l_{\rho^{-}}(\lambda^{-})\sin(r)\cos(r).

We can now prove Lemma
5.14
.

Proof of Lemma
5.14
.

We show that every
\rho\in\mathcal{T}
has a neighborhood
Z
, for which the statement of the lemma holds. First suppose that
\rho\neq\rho_{\circ}
. Define the width of the convex core
C_{\rho}
as the supremum of the lengths of timelike segments inside
C_{\rho}
. The width is positive if and only if
\rho\neq\rho_{\circ}
. One can see that by Corollary
3.7
, the width is continuous in
\rho
. Hence, there exists a compact neighborhood
Z
of
\rho
over which the width is at least
w_{0}>0
. Thereby, for
r=\frac{\pi-w_{0}}{2}
and for all
\rho^{\prime}\in Z
the level surface
L_{r}(\rho^{\prime})\subset\Omega_{\rho^{\prime}}
belongs to
C_{\rho^{\prime}}
. Hence, for every
\rho^{\prime}\in Z
every future-convex Cauchy surface in
\Omega_{\rho^{\prime}}
is in the past of
L_{r}(\rho^{\prime})
. Furthermore, note that from Lemma
5.19
and Theorem
5.4
the area of
L_{r}(\rho^{\prime})
is continuous in
\rho^{\prime}
. Hence, there exists an upper bound on the area of
L_{r}(\rho^{\prime})
over
Z
, and we get the desired result from applying Lemma
5.18
.

Now we treat
\rho=\rho_{\circ}
. We claim that for every
r>0
there exist a compact neighborhood
Z
of
\rho_{\circ}
in
\mathcal{T}
and
\alpha>0
such that for a future-convex Cauchy surface
\Sigma\subset\Omega_{\rho^{\prime}}
,
\rho^{\prime}\in Z
, if the infimum of
\textsc{ct}_{\rho^{\prime}}
over
\Sigma
is
\leq\alpha
, then the supremum is
\leq r
. Indeed, otherwise for some
r
there exists a sequence
\rho_{i}
converging to
\rho_{\circ}
and
\Sigma_{i}\subset\Omega_{\rho_{i}}
such that the infima of
\textsc{ct}_{\rho_{i}}
over
\Sigma_{i}
go to zero, but the suprema are at least
r
. Lift the universal covers of
\Sigma_{i}
to
\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
. From Lemma
5.13
, one can choose the lifts so that there exist
p_{i},q_{i}\in\widetilde{\Sigma}_{i}
such that
p_{i}\rightarrow o
, and
q_{i}\rightarrow q\in\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho_{\circ}}
. The segment
oq
is timelike, but is the limit of segments
p_{i}q_{i}
, which are spacelike since
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{i}
are Cauchy surfaces in
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho_{i}}
. This is a contradiction.

Thereby, for some neighborhood
Z
of
\rho_{\circ}
and some
r>0
, for every
\rho^{\prime}\in Z
every future-convex Cauchy surface in
\Omega_{\rho^{\prime}}
with the infimum of
\textsc{ct}_{\rho^{\prime}}
at most
\alpha
belongs to the past of
L_{r}(\rho^{\prime})
and we can apply Lemma
5.18
to it, as well as the bound on
A_{r}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.1
for
y\in\mathcal{D}_{-}
.

By Lemma
5.2
, up to subsequence,
\rho_{i}
converge to
\rho
. By Lemma
5.14
, there exists
\alpha>0
such that for every
v\in V
we have
\textsc{ct}_{\rho_{i}}(f_{i}(v))\geq\alpha
. Hence, from Lemma
5.12
, up to subsequence, for every
v\in V
the sequence
f_{i}(v)
converges to some
f(v)\in\Omega^{+}(\rho)\cup\partial^{+}C_{\rho}
. We need to see that
f
is injective. Suppose that for
v\neq w\in V
we have
f(v)=f(w)
. Lift all to
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{w}
, suppose that
\widetilde{v},\widetilde{w}\in\widetilde{W}
are lifts of
v,w
such that
\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{v})=\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{w})
. For every
i
, the segment
\widetilde{f}_{i}(\widetilde{v})\widetilde{f}_{i}(\widetilde{w})
is spacelike. We can pick an arbitrary timelike plane containing
\widetilde{f}_{i}(\widetilde{v})\widetilde{f}_{i}(\widetilde{w})
. Using the reverse triangle inequality in the timelike plane we see that
d_{i}(v,w)\leq d_{A}(\widetilde{f}_{i}(\widetilde{v}),\widetilde{f}_{i}(\widetilde{w}))
. Thus
d(v,w)\leq\liminf d_{A}(\widetilde{f}_{i}(\widetilde{v}),\widetilde{f}_{i}(\widetilde{w}))=0
, which is a contradiction, so
\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{v})\neq\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{w})
. Thus,
(\rho_{i},f_{i})
converge to
(\rho,f)\in\mathcal{P}_{-}^{c}
. But if
(\rho,f)\notin\mathcal{P}_{-}^{s}
, then Lemma
3.32
implies that
d\notin\mathcal{D}_{-}^{s}
, which is a contradiction.
∎

5.5
Convergence of marked points at the blow-up

5.5.1
Compactification at the blow-up

We will require a compactification of some ends of
\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{c}
, similar to the one for
\mathcal{P}_{-}^{c}
in Section
5.3
. First we need to describe a compactification of
\mathbb{S}(\mathcal{P}_{0}^{c})
. Pick a compact neighborhood
U_{0}
of zero in
T_{\rho_{\circ}}\mathcal{T}
, its compact lift to a neighborhood
\widetilde{U}_{0}
of zero in
T_{\rho_{\circ}}\mathcal{R}
and pick
\alpha>0
. We define
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{c}(\widetilde{U}_{0},\alpha)
as the subset of
(\tau,\widetilde{f})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{c}
where
\tau\in\widetilde{U}_{0}
and for all
v\in V
we have
\textsc{ct}_{\tau}(\widetilde{f}(v))\leq\alpha
. Now to conditions (1) and (2) used in the definition of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{\bdia}(\widetilde{U}_{-})
in Section
5.3
we also add (3): if for
v\in V
we have
\widetilde{f}(v)\in\widetilde{\Omega}^{+}_{\tau}
, then
\textsc{ct}_{\tau}(\widetilde{f}(v))\leq\alpha
. This produces
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{\bdia}(\widetilde{U}_{0},\alpha)
. We define
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\bdia}(U_{0},\alpha)
to be its quotient.
Then the space
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\bdia}(U_{0},\alpha)
is a compactification of
{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{c}(U_{0},\alpha)
. A proof goes the same way as the proof of Lemma
5.13
, just instead of Lemma
5.12
we use Lemma
5.10
and instead of Lemma
5.11
we use
[
15
, Propositions 6.2 and 6.5]
.
By applying scaling to
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\bdia}(U_{0},\alpha)
, we obtain the space
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\bdia}
, independent on the choices of
U_{0}
and
\alpha
, which is not a compactification of
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{c}
. However, it is easy to see that
\mathbb{S}(\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\bdia})
is a compactification of
\mathbb{S}(\mathcal{P}_{0}^{c})
.

As in Section
5.3
, let
U_{-}
be a compact neighborhood of
\rho_{\circ}
in
\mathcal{T}
and
\widetilde{U}_{-}
be its lift to
\mathcal{R}
. Define
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\vee}^{\bdia}(\widetilde{U}_{-}):=\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{\bdia}(\widetilde{U}_{-})\cup\mathbb{S}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{\bdia})\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\vee}
,
{\mathcal{P}}_{\vee}^{\bdia}(U_{-}):={\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{\bdia}(U_{-})\cup\mathbb{S}({\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{\bdia})
. We plan to show that
{\mathcal{P}}_{\vee}^{\bdia}(U_{-})
is compact. To this purpose we need a “blown-up” analogue of the argument from Section
5.3
. We will rely on the following elementary fact.

Lemma 5.20
.

In
\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
m\geq 2
, let
x^{i}
be a sequence converging to the origin
o
. Then there exists a
C^{1}
-curve
\chi:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
with
\chi(0)=o
containing infinitely many of
x^{i}
. Furthermore, if for a chosen coordinate system the coordinates
x^{i}_{m}
are monotonously decreasing, then we can choose
\chi
so that the projection
\chi_{m}
to the
m
-th axis is monotonously increasing.

Proof.

A proof basically follows from
[
68
, Theorem 3]
of Rosenthal. We only need to check the second claim. In
[
68
]
the author constructs a
C^{1}
-curve
\chi
that he calls
primitive
. The definition is inductive. For
m=2
a curve is primitive at
o
if it is locally convex. Suppose that
m>2
. Assume that a Euclidean metric is chosen so that the coordinate system is orthogonal. Consider the orthogonal projection
\chi^{\prime}
of
\chi
to the orthogonal plane to the tangent direction at
o
. Then
\chi
is primitive at
o
if
\chi^{\prime}
is either locally constant or is primitive at
o
.

Now we pass to our claim, which we prove by induction on
m
. If
m=2
the claim follows from convexity. Suppose that
m>2
. If
\dot{\chi}_{m}(0)\neq 0
, the claim is obvious. If
\dot{\chi}_{m}(0)=0
, we pass to the orthogonal projection
\chi^{\prime}
to the orthogonal plane to the tangent direction at zero. Clearly,
\chi^{\prime}
is not locally constant at zero. By induction, the claim is true for the projection
\chi^{\prime}_{m}
at the
m
-th axis. But
\chi^{\prime}_{m}=\chi_{m}
.
∎

Recall that in Section
3.5.3
we chose a
G
-invariant affine connection on
\mathcal{R}
. Consider the associated exponential map
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{R}}
from
\rho_{\circ}
. Assume that it sends homeomorphically a compact neighborhood
\widetilde{U}_{0}
of zero in
T_{\rho_{\circ}}\mathcal{R}
onto
\widetilde{U}_{-}
.
Let
t_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
be a sequence converging to zero,
\tau_{i}
be a sequence converging to
\tau
in
T_{\rho_{\circ}}\mathcal{R}
, define
\rho_{i}:=\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{R}}(t_{i}\tau_{i})
,
g_{i}:=g_{t_{i}}
,
\widetilde{\Omega}_{i}:=\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho_{i}}
.

Lemma 5.21
.

The sets
g_{i}{\rm cl}(\widetilde{\Omega}_{i})
converge to
{\rm cl}(\widetilde{\Omega}_{\tau})
as subsets of
\mathbb{RP}^{3}
.

Proof.

Pass to a subsequence such that
t_{i}
decreases monotonously. Pick
\varepsilon>0
, define
X:=(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\widetilde{U}_{0})\times[0,\varepsilon)
. By Lemma
5.20
, there exists a
C^{1}
-curve
(\tau_{s},t_{s}):[0,1]\rightarrow X
with
(\tau_{0},t_{0})=(\tau,0)
such that it contains a subsequence of
(\tau_{i},t_{i})
and
t_{s}
is an increasing function. Thus, we can reparameterize it as
(\tau_{t},t)
.
Define
\rho_{t}:=\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{R}}(t\tau_{t})
. This is a continuous curve, differentiable at
t=0
with
\dot{\rho}_{0}=\tau
. Now the claim follows from Corollary
3.18
. However, we proved it up to subsequence, which is not a problem because of Lemma
3.10
applied to the space of closed subsets of
\mathbb{RP}^{3}
endowed with the topology of Hausdorff convergence.
∎

By the same argument, using Corollary
3.23
instead of Lemma
3.18
, we show

Lemma 5.22
.

The sets
g_{i}{\rm cl}(\partial^{+}\widetilde{\Omega}_{i})
converge to
{\rm cl}(\partial^{+}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\tau})
.

Any point
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2,1}
belongs to
g_{t}\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
for all small enough
t
. We need now to consider simultaneously the Minkowski metric and the rescaled anti-de Sitter metrics. Let
\xi_{0}:\mathbb{R}^{2,1}\times\mathbb{R}^{2,1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be the
absolute Minkowski distance function
. It is equal to the spacelike distance on the pairs of points in spacelike Minkowski relation, the timelike distance on the pairs in timelike Minkowski relation and zero on the pairs in lightlike relation and on the diagonal. Similarly, let
\xi_{t}
be the absolute distance function of the rescaled anti-de Sitter metric: for
p,q\in(g_{t}\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}\cap\mathbb{R}^{2,1})
,
\xi_{t}(p,q)
is equal to the respective length of the segment between
p
and
q
that belongs to
\mathbb{R}^{2,1}
, provided that this segment belong to
g_{t}\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}\cap\mathbb{R}^{2,1}
. Hence,
\xi_{t}
is defined on the respective subset of
(g_{t}\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}\cap\mathbb{R}^{2,1})\times(g_{t}\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}\cap\mathbb{R}^{2,1})
of the pairs of points that can be connected by such segment. We consider all
\xi_{t}
as a single function
\xi
defined on the respective subset
Z\subset[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{2,1}\times\mathbb{R}^{2,1}
.

Lemma 5.23
.

The function
\xi
is continuous on
Z
.

Proof.

It is enough to check the continuity as
t\rightarrow 0
. For
(t,p,q)\in Z
,

\xi_{t}(p,q)=\frac{\xi_{1}(g_{t}^{-1}p,g_{t}^{-1}q)}{t}.

Now the Taylor expression of the anti-de Sitter metric tensor in the normal coordinates with respect to
o
implies that the right-hand expression converges to
\xi_{0}(p,q)
as
(t_{i},p_{i},q_{i})\in Z
converge to
(0,p,q)
.
∎

Note that if
p,q\in\mathbb{R}^{2,1}
are in spacelike (resp. timelike) relation for the Minkowski metric, then for all small enough
t
they are in spacelike (resp. timelike) relation for the rescaled anti-de Sitter metrics.

Consider
p\in\widetilde{\Omega}^{+}_{\tau}
. Due to Lemma
5.21
,
p\in g_{i}\widetilde{\Omega}_{i}
for all large enough
i
. Define
\textsc{ct}(p):=\textsc{ct}_{\tau}(p)
,
\eta(p):=\eta_{\tau}(p)
,
\textsc{ct}^{\vee}_{i}(p):=\frac{1}{t_{i}}\textsc{ct}_{\rho_{i}}(p)
,
\eta^{\vee}_{i}(p):=g_{i}\eta_{\rho_{i}}(p)
. We can now establish a “blown-up” analogue of Lemma
5.11
.

Lemma 5.24
.

Let
p_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{2,1}
be a sequence of points converging to
p\in\widetilde{\Omega}^{+}_{\tau}
.
Then
\eta^{\vee}_{i}(p_{i})\rightarrow\eta(p)
and
\textsc{ct}^{\vee}_{i}(p_{i})\rightarrow\textsc{ct}(p)
. (Note that
\eta^{\vee}_{i}(p_{i})
and
\textsc{ct}^{\vee}_{i}(p_{i})
are well-defined for all large enough
i
.)

Proof.

Let
K_{p}
be a compact neighborhood of
p
in
\widetilde{\Omega}^{+}_{\tau}
. For all large enough
i
, we have
K_{p}\subset g_{i}\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
. Denote by
C
the causal past of
K_{p}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2,1}
with respect to the Minkowski metric and denote by
C_{i}
the intersections of the causal past of
K_{p}
with respect to the rescaled anti-de Sitter metrics with
\mathbb{R}^{2,1}
. We claim that
{\rm cl}(C_{i})
converge to
{\rm cl}(C)
as subsets of
\mathbb{RP}^{3}
. Indeed, observe that any causal segment with respect to any rescaled anti-de Sitter metric that belongs to
\mathbb{R}^{2,1}
is timelike for the Minkowski metric. Hence, all
C_{i}\subset C
. On the other hand, any interior point of
C
is an interior point of
C_{i}
for all large enough
i
. Since
{\rm cl}(C)
is the closure of its interior, we derive the desired claim. Let
K
be a compact neighborhood of
C\cap\partial^{+}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\tau}
.

Pass to a subsequence realizing
\limsup\textsc{ct}^{\vee}_{i}(p_{i})
.
For all large enough
i,
the points
p_{i}
belong to
K_{p}
.
Thus, up to subsequence, the closures of the intersections of the causal past of
p_{i}
with respect to the rescaled anti-de Sitter metrics with
\mathbb{R}^{2,1}
converge to a subset of
C
.
From Lemma
5.22
,
g_{i}{\rm cl}(\partial^{+}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho_{i}})
converge to
{\rm cl}(\partial^{+}\Omega_{\tau})
. Hence, for all large enough
i
the points
\eta_{i}^{\vee}(p_{i})
belong to
K
. Thereby, up to subsequence, they converge to a point
q\in\partial^{+}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\tau}
. From Lemma
5.23
, we get

\textsc{ct}(p)\geq\limsup\textsc{ct}^{\vee}_{i}(p_{i}).

On the other hand, since
g_{i}{\rm cl}(\partial^{+}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho_{i}})
converge in the Hausdorff sense to
{\rm cl}(\partial^{+}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\tau})
, there exists a sequence
q_{i}\in g_{i}{\rm cl}(\partial^{+}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho_{i}})
such that
q_{i}
converge to
\eta(p)
. Thereby, from Lemma
5.23
,

\textsc{ct}(p)\leq\liminf\textsc{ct}^{\vee}_{i}(p_{i}).

Thus,
\lim\textsc{ct}^{\vee}_{i}(p_{i})=\textsc{ct}(p)
and
q=\eta(p)
. The latter means that
\eta^{\vee}_{i}(p_{i})\rightarrow\eta(p)
.
∎

Now we can establish the “blown-up” analogue of Lemma
5.12
.

Lemma 5.25
.

Consider
\tau\in T_{\rho_{\circ}}\mathcal{R}
. There exists a neighborhood
U\ni\tau
in
T_{\rho_{\circ}}\mathcal{R}
,
\varepsilon>0
and a continuous map
\Phi:U\times[0,\varepsilon)\times\widetilde{S}\times(0,\frac{\pi}{2})\rightarrow\mathbb{RP}^{3}
such that

(1.1) for every
\tau\in U
,
t\in(0,\varepsilon)
,
r\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
, the map
\Phi(\tau,t,.,r):\widetilde{S}\rightarrow\mathbb{RP}^{3}
is a
g_{t}\theta_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{R}}(t\tau)}g_{t}^{-1}
-equivariant map onto
g_{t}L_{tr}(\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{R}}(t\tau))
;

(1.2) for every
\tau\in U
,
t\in(0,\varepsilon)
,
p\in\widetilde{S}
, the set
\Phi(\tau,t,p,(0,\frac{\pi}{2}))
is the
g_{t}
-image of a gradient line of
\textsc{ct}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{R}}(t\tau)}
;

(2.1) for every
\tau\in U
,
r\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
, the map
\Phi(\tau,0,.,r):\widetilde{S}\rightarrow\mathbb{RP}^{3}
is a
\theta_{\tau}
-equivariant map onto
L_{r}(\tau)
;

(1.2) for every
\tau\in U
,
p\in\widetilde{S}
, the set
\Phi(\tau,0,p,(0,\frac{\pi}{2}))
is a gradient line of
\textsc{ct}_{\tau}
.

With Lemma
5.24
established, the proof of this Lemma is basically a repetition of the proof of Lemma
5.12
. The necessary changes are the following. We apply the Ehresmann–Thurston theorem to the representation variety of
\pi_{1}S
into
{\rm PGL}(4,\mathbb{R})
to get a neighborhood of
\theta_{\tau}
and a varying equivariant surface. We also observe that if a surface is spacelike for the Minkowski metric then it is spacelike for all the rescaled anti-de Sitter metrics. (Note that any plane in
\mathbb{RP}^{3}
intersects any
g_{t}\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
, which allows to define the notion of spacelikeness even when a surface is not fully contained in
g_{t}\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
.) Modulo these details, the rest of the proof is the same.

Finally, the proof of the next lemma now follows the same way as Lemma
5.13
, using Lemmas
5.24
and
5.25
in the appropriate places.

Lemma 5.26
.

The space
\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{\bdia}(U_{-})
is compact.

5.5.2
End of the proof

In this section we prove Lemma
5.1
for
y\in\partial\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{s}
, which finishes the proof of Lemma
5.1
. We consider a sequence
(\rho_{i},f_{i})\in\mathcal{P}_{-}^{s}
from Lemma
5.1
. Pick a compact neighborhood
\widetilde{U}_{-}
of
\rho_{\circ}
in
\mathcal{R}
projecting to
U_{-}
in
\mathcal{T}
as in Section
5.5.1
. Due to Lemma
5.7
, up to subsequence,
\rho_{i}
converge to
\rho_{\circ}
in
\mathcal{R}
. Hence, we may assume that
\rho_{i}\in\widetilde{U}_{-}
. Define
\textsc{ct}_{i}:=\textsc{ct}_{\rho_{i}}
.

Lemma 5.27
.

Up to subsequence,
(\rho_{i},f_{i})
converge in
\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{\bdia}(U_{-})
to
x\in\mathbb{S}({\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{\bdia})
.

Proof.

Note first that, up to subsequence, there exists
v\in V
such that
\textsc{ct}_{i}(f_{i}(v))\rightarrow 0
. Indeed, otherwise from Lemma
5.12
, up to subsequence,
(\rho_{i},f_{i})
converge to
(\rho_{\circ},f)\in\mathcal{P}_{-}^{w}
. Recall from Appendix
A.
that
l_{f,v}:\pi_{1}S\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
sends
\gamma
to the infimum of lengths of closed curves in
\Sigma(f)
based at
f(v)
in the class of
\gamma
. Lemma
A.16
shows that
l_{f_{i},v}\rightarrow l_{f,v}
. Clearly,
l_{f,v}
values in
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. On the other hand, if
d_{i}
converge to
\partial\mathcal{D}_{\vee}
,
l_{f_{i},v}
must converge to zero pointwise. This is a contradiction.

Let
v\in V
be such that
\textsc{ct}_{i}(f_{i}(v))\rightarrow 0
. Suppose that there exists
w\in V
such that
\textsc{ct}_{i}(f_{i}(v))
does not converge to zero. Due to Lemma
5.13
, up to subsequence, we can pick lifts
(\rho_{i},\widetilde{f}_{i})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}
such that
\rho_{i}\rightarrow\rho_{\circ}
,
\widetilde{f}_{i}(v)\rightarrow o
and
\widetilde{f}_{i}(w)\rightarrow p\in\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho_{\circ}}
. Thereby, the segments
\widetilde{f}_{i}(v)\widetilde{f}_{i}(w)
converge to a timelike segment
op
. However, the segments
\widetilde{f}_{i}(v)\widetilde{f}_{i}(w)
are spacelike. This is a contradiction.

Hence, for every
v\in V
we have
\textsc{ct}_{i}(f_{i}(v))\rightarrow 0
. Due to Lemma
5.13
, this means that, up to subsequence,
(\rho_{i},f_{i})
lift to
(\rho_{i},\widetilde{f}_{i})
that converge to
o_{-}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}
. Due to Lemma
5.26
, up to subsequence,
(\rho_{i},f_{i})
converge to
x\in\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{\bdia}(U_{-})
. Altogether, this means that
x\in\mathbb{S}({\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{\bdia})
.
∎

Consider
x\in\mathbb{S}({\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{\bdia})
from Lemma
5.27
. If
x\in\mathbb{S}(\mathcal{P}_{0}^{s})
, we are done. If
x\in\mathbb{S}(\mathcal{P}_{0}^{c}\backslash\mathcal{P}_{0}^{s})
, then due to Lemma
3.35
,
y
must be in
\mathbb{S}(\mathcal{D}_{0}^{c}\backslash\mathcal{D}_{0}^{s})
and we get a contradiction. Otherwise, pick a representative
(\tau,\widetilde{f})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}
of a lift of
x
to
\mathbb{S}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{\bdia})
. We have a dichotomy. The first option is that
(\tau,\widetilde{f})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{w}\backslash\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{c}
. Then
\widetilde{f}(V)\subset\Sigma(\widetilde{f})
, but, since
(\tau,\widetilde{f})\notin\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{c}
,
\widetilde{f}
is not injective. The second option is that
(\tau,\widetilde{f})\notin\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{w}
. By construction, we have
\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{V})\subset(\widetilde{\Omega}^{+}_{\tau}\cup\partial^{+}_{s}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\tau})
. Since
(\tau,\widetilde{f})\notin\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{w}
, we get
\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{V})\cap\partial^{+}_{s}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\tau}\neq\emptyset
.

Recall that in Section
3.5.3
we chose an affine connection on
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}
. Consider the associated exponential map
\mathcal{E}:\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}
. It is a homeomorphism from a neighborhood
\widetilde{X}_{0}
of
o_{0}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}
onto a neighborhood
\widetilde{X}_{-}
of
o_{-}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}
. Pick a section
\kappa:\mathbb{S}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0})\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}
. Then every
x\in\widetilde{X}_{-}
is uniquely represented by a pair
(z_{x},t_{x})
so that
z_{x}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}
is in the image of
\kappa
,
t_{x}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
\mathcal{E}(t_{x}z_{x})=x
. We may assume that our
(\tau,\widetilde{f})
is in the image of
\kappa
and that
(\rho_{i},\widetilde{f}_{i})=:x_{i}
are in
\widetilde{X}_{-}
. We define
t_{i}:=t_{x_{i}}
,
g_{i}:=g_{t_{i}}
,
\widetilde{\Omega}_{i}:=\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho_{i}}
. Also let
\tau_{i}
come from
z_{x_{i}}
. By construction,
g_{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}\rightarrow\widetilde{f}
as elements of
(\mathbb{RP}^{3})^{V}
.

Lemma 5.28
.

Let
P_{i}\subset\widetilde{\Omega}^{+}_{i}
and
P\subset\widetilde{\Omega}^{+}_{\tau}
be finite sets such that
g_{i}P_{i}
converge to
P
in
\mathbb{RP}^{3}
. Let
\widetilde{P}_{i}
and
\widetilde{P}
be their
\theta_{\rho_{i}}
- and
\iota_{\tau}
-orbits respectively. Let
\Sigma_{i}
and
\Sigma
be the future-convex boundary components of
{\rm conv}(\widetilde{P}_{i})
and
{\rm conv}(\widetilde{P})
. Then
g_{i}{\rm cl}(\Sigma_{i})
converge to
{\rm cl}(\Sigma)
as subsets of
\mathbb{RP}^{3}
.

Proof.

From Lemma
3.19
,
g_{i}\Lambda_{\rho_{i}}
converge to
\Lambda_{\rho_{\circ}}
. The set
\Lambda_{\rho_{i}}
is the limit set for
\widetilde{P}_{i}
. Due to
[
34
, Lemma 2.4]
,
\Lambda_{\rho_{\circ}}
is the limit set for
\widetilde{P}
. Hence,
{\rm conv}(\widetilde{P}_{i})
converges to
{\rm conv}(\widetilde{P})
. The rest of the proof goes the same way as the proof of Lemma
3.11
for
\rho\neq\rho_{\circ}
.
∎

In particular, in the both cases
g_{i}{\rm cl}(\Sigma(\widetilde{f}_{i}))\rightarrow{\rm cl}(\Sigma(\widetilde{f}))
. We now deal with the first case of the dichotomy. Then there are
\widetilde{v}\neq\widetilde{w}\in\widetilde{V}
such that
\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{v})=\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{w})
. Let
v,w\in V
be their projections and
a_{i}:=d_{i}(v,w)
. Note that
v\neq w
. Recall that
\xi_{t_{i}}
is the rescaled anti-de Sitter absolute distance function. From Lemma
5.23
, we have
\xi_{t_{i}}(g_{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}(\widetilde{v}),g_{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}(\widetilde{w}))\rightarrow 0
. By applying the reversed triangle inequality to timelike anti-de Sitter planes containing the segments
\widetilde{f}_{i}(\widetilde{v})\widetilde{f}_{i}(\widetilde{w})
, we obtain

\frac{a_{i}}{t_{i}}\leq\xi_{t_{i}}(g_{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}(\widetilde{v}),g_{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}(\widetilde{w}))\rightarrow 0.

(5.1)

We need two more ingredients to obtain a contradiction.

Lemma 5.29
.

Let
e
be an edge of
\Sigma(\widetilde{f})
between
\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{v}_{1})
and
\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{v}_{2})
. Let
\widetilde{V}_{1},\widetilde{V}_{2}\subset\widetilde{V}
be defined as maximal subsets of
\widetilde{V}
such that
\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{V}_{1})=\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{v}_{1})
,
\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{V}_{2})=\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{v}_{2})
. Then for all large enough
i
there exist
\widetilde{v}_{1}^{\prime}\in\widetilde{V}_{1}
,
\widetilde{v}_{2}^{\prime}\in\widetilde{V}_{2}
, depending on
i
, such that the segment
\widetilde{f}_{i}(\widetilde{v}_{1}^{\prime})\widetilde{f}_{i}(\widetilde{v}_{2}^{\prime})
belongs to
\Sigma(\widetilde{f}_{i})
.

Proof.

Suppose the converse, pass to a subsequence for which the claim does not hold.
Let
p
be the midpoint of
e
. Since
e
is an edge of
\Sigma(\widetilde{f})
and
\Sigma(\widetilde{f})
is strictly polyhedral, there exists a neighborhood
X
of
p
in
\mathbb{RP}^{3}
such that
X
is disjoint from
{\rm conv}(\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{V}\backslash\widetilde{V}_{1}))\cup{\rm conv}(\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{V}\backslash\widetilde{V}_{2}))
. Clearly,
g_{i}({\rm conv}(\widetilde{f}_{i}(\widetilde{V}\backslash\widetilde{V}_{1}))\cup{\rm conv}(\widetilde{f}_{i}(\widetilde{V}\backslash\widetilde{V}_{2})))
converge to
{\rm conv}(\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{V}\backslash\widetilde{V}_{1}))\cup{\rm conv}(\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{V}\backslash\widetilde{V}_{2}))
. Hence
X
is also disjoint from
g_{i}({\rm conv}(\widetilde{f}_{i}(\widetilde{V}\backslash\widetilde{V}_{1}))\cup{\rm conv}(\widetilde{f}_{i}(\widetilde{V}\backslash\widetilde{V}_{2})))
for all large enough
i
. On the other hand,
g_{i}{\rm cl}(\Sigma(\widetilde{f}_{i}))\rightarrow{\rm cl}(\Sigma(\widetilde{f}))
. Hence for all large enough
i
there exists
p_{i}\in X\cap g_{i}\Sigma(\widetilde{f}_{i})
. Then
p_{i}
belongs to the convex hull of three points from
g_{i}(\widetilde{f}_{i}(\widetilde{V})\cup\Lambda_{i})
. Since the claim does not hold, these three points are either from
g_{i}(\widetilde{f}_{i}(\widetilde{V}\backslash\widetilde{V}_{1})\cup\Lambda_{i})
or from
g_{i}(\widetilde{f}_{i}(\widetilde{V}\backslash\widetilde{V}_{2})\cup\Lambda_{i})
. In any case,
p_{i}\in g_{i}({\rm conv}(\widetilde{f}_{i}(\widetilde{V}\backslash\widetilde{V}_{1}))\cup{\rm conv}(\widetilde{f}_{i}(\widetilde{V}\backslash\widetilde{V}_{2})))
, which is a contradiction.
∎

Note that the subsets
\widetilde{V}_{1},\widetilde{V}_{2}
are finite. By passing to a subsequence, we assume that
\widetilde{v}_{1}^{\prime},\widetilde{v}_{2}^{\prime}
from Lemma
5.29
are fixed and denote them from now by
\widetilde{v}_{1},\widetilde{v}_{2}
. Note that the segment
\widetilde{f}_{i}(\widetilde{v}_{1})\widetilde{f}_{i}(\widetilde{v}_{2})
is geodesic in the intrinsic metric of
\Sigma(\widetilde{f}_{i})
. Hence, it projects to a geodesic arc
\chi_{i}
in
(S,d_{i})
between some
v_{1},v_{2}\in V
, which are possibly coinciding. Then
\chi_{i}
are representatives of the same class
\gamma
of arcs on
S\backslash V
up to isotopy. Let
b_{i}
be its length in
d_{i}
. We now apply Lemma
5.23
to
g_{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}(\widetilde{v}_{1}),g_{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}(\widetilde{v}_{2})
and see that there is
b>0
such that

\frac{b_{i}}{t_{i}}\geq b.

(5.2)

We claim that equations (
5.1
) and (
5.2
) are in contradiction with the fact that
y\in\partial\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{s}
. The idea is that the latter fact means that for
d_{i}
all the metric quantities (lengths of shortest curves in free homotopy classes, distances between marked points, diametes, etc) must go to zero with the same speed. To see this, we need

Lemma 5.30
.

Let
y_{t}:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{s}
be a
C^{1}
-curve with
y_{0}\in\partial\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{s}
and
y_{t}=d_{t}\in\mathcal{D}^{s}_{-}
for
t>0
. Then there exists a representative metric
d_{0}\in\mathcal{D}_{0}^{s}
of
y_{0}
such that
\frac{d_{t}}{t}\rightarrow d_{0}
in the Lipschitz sense as
t\rightarrow 0
.

Indeed, we lift
y_{t}
to
\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{\sharp}
. We can assume that there is a triangulation
\mathscr{T}
such that
y_{t}\in\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{\sharp}(\mathscr{T})
. Now Lemma
5.30
follows from the construction of
\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{\sharp}(\mathscr{T})
in Section
3.3
. The derivatives of the triangle lengths at
t=0
define
d_{0}\in\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\sharp}(\mathscr{T})
. To construct Lipschitz maps from
\frac{d_{i}}{t_{i}}
to
d_{0}
, pick a triangle
T
of
\mathscr{T}
. It is a hyperbolic triangle in
d_{t}
, we realize it on the hyperboloid
\mathbb{H}^{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2,1}
and send it radially to the Euclidean triangle in the spacelike plane that subtends the vertices in the realization. Then we send it to the Euclidean triangle of the realization of
T
in
d_{0}
by the respective affine map. This defines Lipschitz maps from
\frac{d_{i}}{t_{i}}
to
d_{0}
with Lipschitz constants converging to 1. Lemma
5.20
yields

Corollary 5.31
.

Let
d_{i}\in\mathcal{D}^{c}_{-}
be a sequence converging to
y\in\partial\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{s}
in
\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{s}
. Then there exist a representative metric
d\in\mathcal{D}_{0}^{s}
of
y
and a sequence
t_{i}\rightarrow 0
such that
\frac{d_{i}}{t_{i}}\rightarrow d
in the Lipschitz sense.

Corollary
5.31
implies that there is a sequence
t^{\prime}_{i}\rightarrow 0
such that

\frac{a_{i}}{t_{i}^{\prime}}\rightarrow a^{\prime}>0,~~~~~\frac{b_{i}}{t_{i}^{\prime}}\rightarrow b^{\prime}>0.

The second inequality and (
5.2
) imply that
t^{\prime}_{i}/t_{i}\rightarrow b/b^{\prime}>0
, while the first and (
5.1
) imply that
t^{\prime}_{i}/t_{i}\rightarrow 0
, which is a contradiction.

Hence, it remains to consider
(\tau,\widetilde{f})\notin\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{w}
. Then there is
\widetilde{v}\in\widetilde{V}
such that
\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{v})\in\partial^{+}_{s}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\tau}
. Note that due to Lemma
5.24
, this means that

\frac{\textsc{ct}_{i}(\widetilde{f}_{i}(\widetilde{v}))}{t_{i}}\rightarrow 0.

(5.3)

We claim that this implies that
\tau\neq 0
. Indeed, otherwise we get a similar contradiction as in the proof of Lemma
5.27
. Namely, then we have
\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{v})=o
. Since
(\tau,\widetilde{f})
is a representative of
x\in\mathbb{S}({\mathcal{P}}_{0})
, we have
(\tau,\widetilde{f})\neq o_{0}
. Thereby, there exists
\widetilde{w}\in\widetilde{V}
such that
\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{w})\neq o
, i.e.,
\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{w})\in\widetilde{\Omega}^{+}_{\tau}
. This means that the segment
\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{v})\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{w})
is timelike for the Minkowski metric. Then for all large enough
i
,
\widetilde{f}_{i}(\widetilde{v})\widetilde{f}_{i}(\widetilde{w})
are timelike for the anti-de Sitter metric. This is a contradiction, since they must be spacelike. Hence,
\tau\neq 0
.

Once again we invoke the theory of metric graphs, which we already employed in Section
5.2
. Recall that
\partial^{+}_{s}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\tau}
is the spacelike part of
\partial^{+}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\tau}
and is a metric
\Gamma
-tree for
\Gamma=\pi_{1}S
. We denote it by
\Psi^{+}(\tau)
. Denote by
\lambda^{-}(\tau)
the measured lamination dual to
\Psi^{+}(\tau)
via the Skora duality. See
[
9
,
10
]
for more details. In contrast to the anti-de Sitter case,
\lambda^{-}(\tau)
does not have a direct geometric interpretation in
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\tau}
.

Recall that since
\mathcal{ML}
admits a natural PL-structure, but no natural differentiable structure, one can define its tangent spaces, which are not vector spaces, but cones. See details in
[
12
]
.
Let
\lambda_{0}\in\mathcal{ML}
be the empty measured lamination. Since
\mathcal{ML}
also has the structure of a cone based at
\lambda_{0}
, the tangent space
T_{\lambda_{0}}\mathcal{ML}
can be identified with
\mathcal{ML}
itself. We have

Lemma 5.32
.

Let
\rho_{t}:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathcal{R}
be a continuous curve with
\rho_{0}=\rho_{\circ}
, differentiable at
t=0
with
\dot{\rho}_{0}=\tau\in T_{\rho_{\circ}}\mathcal{T}
. Then
\dot{\lambda}^{-}_{0}:=\frac{d}{dt}\lambda^{-}(\rho_{t})|_{t=0}=\lambda^{-}(\tau)
.

This is basically shown by Bonsante–Schlenker in
[
19
, Appendix B]
. Recall first the notion of infinitesimal earthquake. For every nonzero
\lambda\in\mathcal{ML}
the curves
E^{\pm}_{t\lambda}(\rho_{\circ})
are
C^{1}
(in fact, analytic, see
[
50
]
). We denote by
e^{\pm}_{\lambda}
the resulting vector fields on
\mathcal{T}
. Due to Theorem
5.4
, we have
\tau=e^{-}_{\dot{\lambda}^{-}_{0}}(\rho_{\circ})
. On the other hand, it is shown in the proof of
[
19
, Proposition B.3]
that
\tau=e^{-}_{\lambda^{-}(\tau)}(\rho_{\circ})
. Since the earthquake map from
\rho_{\circ}
is a PL-homeomorphism, we get
\dot{\lambda}^{-}_{0}=\lambda^{-}(\tau)
.

In the same way as in the proof of Lemma
5.21
we deduce from Lemma
5.20

Lemma 5.33
.

In our setting,
\frac{1}{t_{i}}\lambda^{-}(\rho_{i})
converge to
\lambda^{-}(\tau)
in
\mathcal{ML}
.

Since
\tau\neq 0
, the Skora duality, Theorem
5.8
, implies

Corollary 5.34
.

The trees
\frac{1}{t_{i}}\Psi^{+}(\rho_{i})
converge to non-trivial
\Psi^{+}(\tau)
in
\mathcal{MT}
.

Define
\Sigma_{i}:=\Sigma(\widetilde{f}_{i})
,
\alpha_{i}:=\inf_{\Sigma_{i}}\textsc{ct}_{i}(p)
,
\beta_{i}:=\sup_{\Sigma_{i}}\textsc{ct}_{i}(p)
.

Lemma 5.35
.

The sequence
\frac{\beta_{i}}{t_{i}}
is bounded.

Proof.

Suppose the converse. Then, up to subsequence,
\frac{\beta_{i}}{t_{i}}
increases to infinity. Due to (
5.3
),
\frac{\alpha_{t}}{t_{i}}\rightarrow 0
. Hence for all large enough
i
and some
\alpha>0
,
\Sigma_{i}
intersects
\widetilde{L}_{\alpha t_{i}}(\rho_{i})
, where the latter is a level surface of
\textsc{ct}_{i}
. We apply now Lemma
5.25
and get a neighborhood
U\ni\tau
in
T_{\rho_{\circ}}\mathcal{T}
,
\varepsilon>0
and the map

\Phi:U\times[0,\varepsilon)\times\widetilde{S}\times\left(0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right)\rightarrow\mathbb{RP}^{3}.

We define
\phi:U\times[0,\varepsilon)\times\widetilde{S}\rightarrow\mathbb{RP}^{3}
by
\phi(\tau,t,p):=\Phi(\tau,t,p,\alpha)
. Pick an intersection point of
g_{i}\widetilde{L}_{\alpha t_{i}}(\rho_{i})
and
g_{i}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{i}
. These points determine a sequence
p_{i}\in S
via the map
\phi
. Up to subsequence, they converge to
p\in S
. Pick lifts
\widetilde{p}_{i}\in\widetilde{S}
and
\widetilde{p}\in\widetilde{S}
so that
\widetilde{p}_{i}
converge to
\widetilde{p}
.
Then
\phi(\tau_{i},t_{i},\widetilde{p}_{i})
converge to
\phi(\tau,0,\widetilde{p})
.

Denote by
\widetilde{P}_{i}
the
\theta_{\rho_{i}}
-orbit of
g_{i}^{-1}\phi(\tau_{i},t_{i},\widetilde{p}_{i})
and by
\widetilde{P}
the
\iota_{\tau}
-orbit of
\phi(\tau,0,\widetilde{p})
. Denote by
\Sigma^{\prime}_{i}
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
the future-convex boundaries of the closed convex hull of
\widetilde{P}_{i}
and of
\widetilde{P}
respectively. Due to Lemma
5.28
,
g_{i}{\rm cl}(\Sigma_{i}^{\prime})
converge to
{\rm cl}(\Sigma^{\prime})
. Let
\beta
be the supremum of
\textsc{ct}_{\tau}
over
\Sigma^{\prime}
and
\beta^{\prime}_{i}
be the suprema of
\textsc{ct}_{i}
over
\Sigma_{i}^{\prime}
. We first claim that
\limsup\beta^{\prime}_{i}\leq\beta.

Pass to a subsequence realizing
\limsup\beta_{i}^{\prime}
. Pick a point
q_{i}
on
\Sigma_{i}^{\prime}
that realizes the supremum of
\textsc{ct}_{i}
. We project
q_{i}
along the
g_{i}
-images of gradient lines of
\textsc{ct}_{i}
to
g_{i}\widetilde{L}_{\alpha t_{i}}(\rho_{i})
and then to
S
via the map
\phi
. This gives us a sequence
s_{i}\in S
. Up to subsequence, it converges to
s\in S
. Let
\widetilde{s}_{i}\in\widetilde{S}
,
\widetilde{s}\in\widetilde{S}
be their lifts such that
\widetilde{s}_{i}
converge to
\widetilde{s}
. We may assume that
q_{i}
project to
\phi(\tau_{i},t_{i},\widetilde{s}_{i})
.

Because
{\rm cl}(\Sigma^{\prime}_{i})
converges to
{\rm cl}(\Sigma)
, up to subsequence,
q_{i}
converges to
q\in{\rm cl}(\Sigma)
. Because the projections of
q_{i}
converge to
\phi(\tau,0,\widetilde{s})
, we have
q\in\Sigma
. Thus
\limsup\beta^{\prime}_{i}\leq\beta
. Note, however, that every
\Sigma^{\prime}_{i}
is in the future of
\Sigma_{i}
. Hence,
\limsup\frac{\beta_{i}}{t_{i}}\leq\limsup\beta^{\prime}_{i}\leq\beta
.
∎

Let
A_{i}
be the area of
L_{\beta_{i}}(\rho_{i})
.

Lemma 5.36
.

The sequence
\frac{A_{i}}{t_{i}^{2}}
is bounded.

Proof.

From Lemma
5.19
, we have

A_{i}=-2\pi\sin^{2}(\beta_{i})\chi(S)+l_{\rho^{-}(\rho_{i})}(\lambda^{-}(\rho_{i}))\sin(\beta_{i})\cos(\beta_{i}).

The claim follows from Lemma
5.33
and
5.35
.
∎

Now we denote the systole of
d_{i}
by
a_{i}
.

Lemma 5.37
.

We have
\frac{a_{i}}{t_{i}}\rightarrow 0
.

Proof.

Suppose the converse, that
\limsup\frac{a_{i}}{t_{i}}\geq a>0
. From Lemma
5.18
we get

\alpha_{i}\geq\frac{Ca_{i}^{2}}{A_{i}\exp\left(\frac{2A_{i}}{a_{i}}\right)}.

From Lemma
5.36
, there exists
A>0
such that for all
i
we have
\frac{A_{i}}{t_{i}^{2}}\leq A
. Hence, up to subsequence, we get

\alpha_{i}\geq\frac{Ca^{2}}{A\exp\left(\frac{2At_{i}}{a}\right)}\geq\frac{Ca^{2}}{2A}.

It follows that
\frac{\alpha_{i}}{t_{i}}
does not converge to zero, which contradicts (
5.3
).
∎

Now we are ready to define
b_{i}
in this case and finish the proof.
Due to Corollary
5.34
, the trees
\frac{1}{t_{i}}\Psi^{+}(\rho_{i})
converge to
\Psi^{+}(\tau)
, which is non-trivial. Thus there exists
\gamma\in\Gamma
such that
l_{\Psi^{+}(\tau)}(\gamma)>0
. Define
b_{i}:=l_{d_{i}}(\gamma)
. From Lemma
5.9
, it follows that
\frac{1}{t_{i}}\liminf b_{i}>0
. Up to subsequence,
\frac{b_{i}}{t_{i}}\rightarrow b>0
. On the other hand, from Corollary
5.37
,
\frac{a_{i}}{t_{i}}\rightarrow 0
.

Now from Corollary
5.31
, there exists a sequence
t^{\prime}_{i}\rightarrow 0
such that

\frac{a_{i}}{t_{i}^{\prime}}\rightarrow a^{\prime}>0,~~~~~\frac{b_{i}}{t_{i}^{\prime}}\rightarrow b^{\prime}>0.

As in the previous case, the second inequality implies that
t^{\prime}_{i}/t_{i}\rightarrow b/b^{\prime}>0
, while the first implies
t^{\prime}_{i}/t_{i}\rightarrow 0
, which is a final contradiction.

Part 2

The goal of this part is to prove Theorem
AdS2
.

6
Changes in the setup

Our proof of Theorem
AdS2
follows the same path as the proof of Theorem
AdS1
. Let us describe the necessary changes in the setup.

We now have two finite non-empty sets
V^{\pm}\subset S
. As for the spaces of cone-metrics, we first define the spaces
\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}_{-}^{\sharp}:=\mathcal{D}_{-}^{\sharp}(V^{+})\times\mathcal{D}_{-}^{\sharp}(V^{-})
and
\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\sharp}:=\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\sharp}(V^{+})\times\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\sharp}(V^{-})
. These manifolds have natural atlases with charts valued in cones in
\mathbb{R}^{E(\mathscr{T}^{+})}\times\mathbb{R}^{E(\mathscr{T}^{-})}
for pairs of triangulations
(\mathscr{T}^{+},\mathscr{T}^{-})
of
(S,V^{\pm})
respectively. By blowing-up these cones as in Section
3.3
, we obtain the blown-up space
\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{\sharp}
, which is the union of
\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}_{-}^{\sharp}\cup\mathbb{S}(\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\sharp})
. The pure braid group
\mathcal{B}
acts on
\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}_{-}^{\sharp}
,
\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\sharp}
and
\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{\sharp}
. The quotients are denoted by
\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}_{-}
,
\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}_{\vee}
. We denote the elements of the first two by
(d^{+},d^{-})
. As before, we denote the respective subsets corresponding to the concave cone-metrics and to the concave cone-metrics with
V(d^{\pm})=V^{\pm}
by the superscripts
c
and
s
respectively.

As for the spaces of polyhedral surfaces, first we need the concept of
coned bundle
over a manifold. It is defined the same as a vector bundle, but the fiber is isomorphic to a cone in some vector space. A vector bundle minus the zero section is an example of a coned bundle (as our standard convention is to delete the origin from a cone). When we say that we consider a vector bundle as a coned bundle, we mean that we delete the zero section. One can define the spherization and the blow-up of a coned bundle in the same way how it was done for cones in Section
3.1
. Furthermore, one can define the blow-ups of pierced tubular neighborhoods of the zero section, similarly as we defined it for lower-cones.

We mostly denote the elements of
\mathcal{R}\times\mathcal{R}
by
\theta
, considered as representations of
\pi_{1}S
to
G_{-}
, though sometimes we write them as
(\rho^{l},\rho^{r})
. Following our standard convention, we denote the elements of
\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{T}
in the same way. Similarly, we denote the elements of
T\mathcal{R}
or
T\mathcal{T}
by
\iota
, considered as representations of
\pi_{1}S
to
G_{0}
, but sometimes we write them as
(\rho,\tau)
.

Consider the space

\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}:=T\mathcal{R}\times(\mathbb{R}^{2,1})^{V^{+}}\times(\mathbb{R}^{2,1})^{V^{-}}.

This is a vector bundle over
\mathcal{R}
. (Actually, a trivial one, since
\mathcal{R}
is homeomorphic to a ball.)
We consider it as a coned bundle. Denote its elements by
(\iota,\widetilde{f}^{+},\widetilde{f}^{-})
, where
\widetilde{f}^{\pm}:V^{\pm}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2,1}
.

Consider also the space

\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}:=\mathcal{R}\times\mathcal{R}\times(\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3})^{V^{+}}\times(\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3})^{V^{-}}.

We denote its elements by
(\theta,\widetilde{f}^{+},\widetilde{f}^{-})
, where
\widetilde{f}^{\pm}:V^{\pm}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
.
Let
O_{-}
be the submanifold of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}
consisting of all
(\theta,\widetilde{f}^{+},\widetilde{f}^{-})
with
\theta=(\rho,\rho)
for
\rho\in\mathcal{R}
and
\widetilde{f}^{+}(\widetilde{V}^{+})=\widetilde{f}^{-}(\widetilde{V}^{-})=o
. Let
N
be the subbundle of
T\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}
defined over
O_{-}
that is tangent to the product of all but the first factor of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}
. Then
N
complements
TO_{-}
in
T\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}
. There is a natural isomorphism
N\cong\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}
as of vector bundles over
\mathcal{R}
. We have chosen an affine connection on
\mathcal{R}
, which, together with the Levi–Civita connection on
\mathbb{R}^{2,1}
, gives a connection on
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}
. Consider the exponential map along
N
. It produces a diffeomorphism between a pierced tubular neighborhood of the zero section in
N
onto a pierced tubular neighborhood of
O_{-}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}
. We use it to construct the blow-up

\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\vee}:=\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}\cup\mathbb{S}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}).

We consider domains of discontinuity for
\theta\in\mathcal{R}\times\mathcal{R}
or
\iota\in T\mathcal{R}
described in Section
3.4.3
, but now we use the subscripts
\theta
or
\iota
instead of
\rho
or
\tau
, because now we vary all the holonomy. When
\theta\in\mathcal{R}\times\mathcal{R}
, we use the respective notation
\widetilde{C}_{\theta}
for the convex core of
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\theta}
,
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\theta}^{\pm}
for the respective connected components of
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\theta}^{\pm}\backslash\widetilde{C}_{\theta}
, etc., following the conventions of Section
3.4.3
.

Similarly to Section
3.4.5
we denote the subspaces of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}
with
\widetilde{f}^{\pm}(V^{\pm})\subset\widetilde{\Omega}_{\theta}^{\pm}
and
\widetilde{f}^{\pm}(V^{\pm})\subset\widetilde{\Omega}_{\iota}^{\pm}
by
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{w}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{w}
respectively. The space
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{w}
is a coned subbundle of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}
. The notions of convex and strictly convex position for maps
\widetilde{f}^{\pm}
are defined the same as in Section
3.4.5
with the only difference that now we need to distinguish between future-convex and past-convex positions. The future-convex position was defined in Section
3.4.5
. The past convex position for a map
\widetilde{f}^{-}:V^{-}\rightarrow\widetilde{\Omega}_{\theta}
or
\widetilde{f}^{-}:V^{-}\rightarrow\widetilde{\Omega}_{\iota}
is defined the same, but we require
\widetilde{f}^{-}(\widetilde{V}^{-})\subset\widetilde{\Omega}_{\theta}^{-}
or
\widetilde{f}^{-}(\widetilde{V}^{-})\subset\widetilde{\Omega}_{\iota}^{-}
respectively. Then a triple
(\theta,\widetilde{f}^{+},\widetilde{f}^{-})
is said to be in a (strictly) convex position if
\widetilde{f}^{+}
is in (strictly) future-convex and
\widetilde{f}^{-}
is in (strictly) past-convex positions. The respective subsets of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}
are denoted by
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{c}
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{c}
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{s}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{s}
. The space
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{s}
is a coned subbundle. We define the spaces
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\vee}^{w}
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\vee}^{c}
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\vee}^{s}
in an obvious way. The first and the third are submanifolds of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\vee}
.

The same argument as in Section
3.5.3
shows that after taking the quotients by the action of
G_{-}
on
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{w}
, of
G_{0}
on
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{w}
and of
\pi_{1}S
on both of them, we obtain manifolds
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}_{-}^{w}
,
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}_{0}^{w}
and
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{w}=\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}_{-}^{w}\cup\mathbb{S}(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}_{0}^{w})
, where the latter is a manifold with boundary. The quotients of the subsets with the superscripts
c
and
s
are denoted by the same superscripts. The elements of these spaces are triples
(\theta,f^{+},f^{-})
or
(\iota,f^{+},f^{-})
. We denote the respective surfaces defined by
f^{\pm}
by
\Sigma(f^{\pm})
. We have the respective induced metric maps
\mathcal{I}\mathcal{I}_{-}
,
\mathcal{I}\mathcal{I}_{0}
,
\mathcal{I}\mathcal{I}_{\vee}
defined on
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}_{-}^{c}
,
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}_{0}^{c}
and
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{c}
. Here, e.g.,
\mathcal{I}\mathcal{I}_{\vee}:\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{c}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{c}
. If we consider its restriction to
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{s}
, then it values in
\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{s}
, and we write
\mathcal{I}\mathcal{I}_{\vee}^{s}
for the respective map
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{s}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{s}
, the same convention holds for the other maps.
We have

Theorem M2
.

The map

\mathcal{I}\mathcal{I}_{0}^{s}:\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}_{0}^{s}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}_{0}^{s}

is a
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism.

This is a direct corollary of the results of
[
34
]
, though it is not formulated there explicitly. Hence, we now derive it from the results of
[
34
]
. Some ideas were used in
[
66
, Section 5]
.

Proof.

The fact that
\mathcal{I}\mathcal{I}_{0}^{s}
is injective and surjective is a direct reformulation of
[
34
, Theorem II’]
. The fact that it is
C^{1}
can be proven the same as such fact for the map
\mathcal{I}_{0}^{s}
, which is
[
34
, Lemma 2.14]
. The only fact that requires a bit of work is that its differential is nowhere degenerate.

Define the space

\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{+}:=T\mathcal{T}\times(\mathbb{R}^{2,1})^{V^{+}}.

There is its subset
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{s,+}
consisting of the configurations in the strictly future-convex positions. By taking its
G_{0}
- and
\pi_{1}S
-quotients we obtain the space
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{s,+}
, which is a manifold. Denote also
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{s,+}:=\mathcal{D}_{0}^{s}(V^{+})
. We have an induced metric map

\mathcal{I}_{0}^{s,+}:\mathcal{P}_{0}^{s,+}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{0}^{s,+}.

Theorem
M1
implies that it is a
C^{1}
-submersion. We similarly define the space
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{s,-}
, however we consider for it the configurations in the strictly
past-convex
positions. Define
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{s,-}:=\mathcal{D}_{0}^{s}(V^{-})
and an induced metric map

\mathcal{I}_{0}^{s,-}:\mathcal{P}_{0}^{s,-}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{0}^{s,-}.

The theory of past-convex embeddings is obtained from the theory of future-convex embeddings by a change of the time-orientation. Hence, Theorem
M1
implies that
\mathcal{I}_{0}^{s,-}
is also a
C^{1}
-submersion.

For
d\in\mathcal{D}_{0}^{s,\pm}
we define
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\pm}(d):=(\mathcal{I}_{0}^{s,\pm})^{-1}(d)\subset\mathcal{P}_{0}^{s,\pm}(d)
. Since
\mathcal{I}_{0}^{s,\pm}
are
C^{1}
-submersions, these sets are
C^{1}
-submanifolds. Consider the maps
\phi_{d}^{\pm}:\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\pm}(d)\rightarrow T\mathcal{T}
sending an element of
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\pm}(d)
to its holonomy. These are
C^{1}
-maps, see
[
34
, Section 4.1]
. In turn, Theorem
M1
also implies that the compositions of these maps with the projections to
\mathcal{T}
are
C^{1}
-diffeomorphisms. In particular, it follows that
\mathcal{\phi}_{d}^{\pm}
are
C^{1}
-immersions. Now pick
(d^{+},d^{-})\in\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}_{0}^{s}
. Then
[
34
, Theorem II]
implies that the intersection of the images of
\phi_{d^{+}}^{+}
and
\phi_{d^{-}}^{-}
is unique and transverse.

Suppose that for some
x\in\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}_{0}^{s}
there exists
y\in T_{x}\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}_{0}^{s}
such that
d\mathcal{I}\mathcal{I}_{0}^{s}(y)=0
. Let
\mathcal{I}\mathcal{I}_{0}^{s}(x)=(d^{+},d^{-})
. There are natural smooth submersions
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}_{0}^{s}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}_{0}^{s,\pm}
. Let
y^{\pm}
be the images of
y
by these submersions. Since
d\mathcal{I}\mathcal{I}_{0}^{s}(y)=0
and since the maps
\mathcal{I}_{0}^{s,\pm}
are
C^{1}
-submersions, the vectors
y^{\pm}
are tangent to
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\pm}(d^{\pm})
respectively. There is also the natural projection
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}_{0}^{s}\rightarrow T\mathcal{T}
. Let
y_{0}
be the image of
y
under this projection. Then

d\phi_{d^{+}}^{+}(y^{+})=d\phi_{d^{-}}^{-}(y^{-})=y_{0}.

Hence, the transversality of the images implies that
y_{0}=0
. But
\phi_{d^{\pm}}^{\pm}
are
C^{1}
-immersions, thus
y^{+}=y^{-}=0
. From this it follows that
y=0
, which finishes the proof.
∎

Similarly as in Section
3.6
one shows that
\mathcal{I}\mathcal{I}_{\vee}^{s}
is continuous and is
C^{1}
around
\partial\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{s}
. Thus, if we show that it is proper, we can finish the proof of Theorem
AdS2
exactly the same as we proved Theorem
AdS1
.
The proof of the properness, which occupies the next section, is the main part, where we require new ideas, compared to the proof of Theorem
AdS1
.

7
Properness for pairs of metrics

We need to show

Lemma AdS3
.

Let
x_{i}\in\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{s}
be a sequence such that
y_{i}:=\mathcal{I}\mathcal{I}_{\vee}(x_{i})
converge to
y\in\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{s}
. Then, up to subsequence,
x_{i}
converge to
x\in\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{s}
.

Again, since the behavior of
\mathcal{I}\mathcal{I}_{\vee}
is completely understood on
\partial\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{s}
, thanks to Theorem
M2
, we can assume that
x_{i}\in\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}_{-}^{s}
, so they can be denoted by
(\theta_{i},f_{i}^{+},f_{i}^{-})
. Then
y_{i}\in\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}_{-}^{s}
and can be denoted by
(d_{i}^{+},d_{i}^{-})
. Define
\Omega_{i}:=\Omega_{\theta_{i}}
,
C_{i}:=C_{\theta_{i}}
. Let
\textsc{ct}_{i}
be the cosmological time of
\Omega_{i}
, defined on the past of
\partial^{-}C_{i}
in
\Omega_{i}
.

7.1
Convergence of holonomies

First we show

Lemma 7.1
.

Under the conditions of Lemma
AdS3
, let
y
be in
\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}_{-}^{s}
. Then, up to subsequence,
\theta_{i}
converge to
\theta\in\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{T}
.

Similarly to the proof of Lemma
5.2
, it follows from Lemma
5.5
and
5.6
that, up to subsequence,
\rho_{i}^{\pm}\rightarrow\rho^{\pm}\in\mathcal{T}
, where
\rho_{i}^{\pm}
are the holonomies of the intrinsic metrics of
\partial^{\pm}C_{i}
. It is a result of Diallo that in such case, up to subsequence,
\theta_{i}\rightarrow\theta
. See
[
16
, Lemma A.2]
or the proof of
[
78
, Proposition 6.13]
by Tamburelli.

Now suppose that under the conditions of Lemma
AdS3
,
y
is in
\partial\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{s}
. We would like to prove that then
\theta_{i}
converge to
\theta\in\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{T}
and
\theta
belongs to the diagonal, so
\theta=(\rho,\rho)
for some
\rho\in\mathcal{T}
. However, we will do it here only under a stronger assumption. Later we will see how we can overcome this assumption by a density argument. To introduce the stronger assumption we first need few preliminaries.

Due to our construction of the blow-up, analogously to Lemma
5.30
, we have

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
(d_{i}^{+},d_{i}^{-})\in\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}^{c}_{-}
be a sequence converging to
y\in\partial\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{s}
in
\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{s}
. Then there exist representative metrics
(d^{+},d^{-})\in\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}_{0}^{s}
of
y
and a sequence
t_{i}\rightarrow 0
such that
\frac{d_{i}^{\pm}}{t_{i}}\rightarrow d^{\pm}
in the Lipschitz sense.

Let
\lambda_{i}^{\pm}
,
\Psi_{i}^{\pm}
be the bending laminations and the metric trees associated to
\Omega_{i}
.
Lemma
5.9
, which bounds the length functions of the trees
\Psi_{i}^{\pm}
by the length functions of Cauchy surfaces, implies that in our setting
\Psi^{\pm}_{i}\rightarrow 0
in
\mathcal{MT}
. Due to the Skora duality, Theorem
5.8
, this means that
\lambda^{\pm}_{i}\rightarrow 0
. The first option is that, up to subsequence,
\lambda_{i}^{\pm}=0
. Suppose the other case. Then there exists a sequence
t_{i}^{\prime}\rightarrow 0
such that, up to subsequence, at least one of the sequences
\frac{1}{t_{i}^{\prime}}\lambda_{i}^{\pm}
has a nonzero limit in
\mathcal{ML}
and the second sequence has a limit, which is possibly zero. We assume that
\frac{1}{t_{i}^{\prime}}\lambda_{i}^{-}\rightarrow\lambda^{-}\neq 0
.

In this case we will additionally assume that all
\lambda^{-}_{i}
are supported on simple closed curves. Afterwards we will show how to overcome it. In other words, we will now prove

Lemma 7.3
.

Under the conditions of Lemma
AdS3
, let
y
be in
\partial\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{s}
. Assume that either
\lambda_{i}^{\pm}=0
or that for a sequence
t_{i}^{\prime}\rightarrow 0
we have
\frac{1}{t_{i}^{\prime}}\lambda^{\pm}_{i}\rightarrow\lambda^{\pm}\in\mathcal{ML}
with
\lambda^{-}\neq 0
and all
\lambda^{-}_{i}
are supported on simple closed curves. Then, up to subsequence,
\theta_{i}
converge to
\theta=(\rho,\rho)\in\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{T}
for some
\rho\in\mathcal{T}
.

The first proof ingredient is a result of Bonsante–Schlenker
[
19
, Corollary 4.10]
:

Lemma 7.4
.

There are constants
C,h_{0}>0
, depending only on the genus of
S
, such that for every
\theta\in\mathcal{R}\times\mathcal{R}
, in
\Omega_{\theta}
we have

i(\lambda^{+},\lambda^{-})\geq Cl_{\rho^{-}}(\lambda^{-})\min\{h_{0},l_{\rho^{-}}(\lambda^{-})\}.

Here
i:\mathcal{ML\times ML}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is the geometric intersection number, see
[
12
]
.
Our second assumption in Lemma
7.3
implies that
{i(\lambda_{i}^{+},\lambda_{i}^{-})}/{t_{i}^{\prime 2}}
is bounded. Let
\mu_{i}
be the simple closed curve supporting
\lambda_{i}
, so
\lambda_{i}^{-}=s_{i}\mu_{i}
for some
s_{i}>0
. Since no sequence of simple closed curves in
\mathcal{ML}
(with weights one) converges to zero and
\frac{s_{i}}{t_{i}^{\prime}}\mu_{i}
is bounded in
\mathcal{ML}
, then
s_{i}/t_{i}^{\prime}
is bounded. Hence, Lemma
7.4
implies that there exists a constant
C>0
, depending only on
\theta_{i}
, such that

l_{\rho_{i}^{-}}(\mu_{i})\leq C.

(7.1)

In the case of the first assumption of Lemma
7.3
, we just consider
\mu_{i}
empty.
We can now obtain the key ingredient for a proof of Lemma
7.3
.

Lemma 7.5
.

There exists a constant
C>0
, depending only on
\theta_{i}
and
y
, such that

\inf_{p\in\Sigma(f^{+}_{i})}\textsc{ct}_{i}(p)\geq Ct_{i}.

Proof.

We first claim that there exists
C_{0}>0
with the following property.
Pick
p\in\Sigma(f^{+}_{i})
.
Then there exists a homotopically non-trivial curve
\chi:[0,1]\rightarrow\Sigma(f^{+}_{i})
such that it belongs to a single block of the canonical decomposition,
\chi(0)=\chi(1)=p
and for the projections
\psi
of
\chi
to
\partial^{-}C_{i}
along the gradient of the cosmological time we have
{\rm len}(\psi)\leq C_{0}
.

Actually, we first construct
\psi
and then lift it to
\chi
. Suppose that
p
is projected to
q\in\partial^{-}C_{i}
along the gradient of
\textsc{ct}_{i}
.
If
q\in\mu_{i}
, then we can take
\mu_{i}
as
\psi
and consider as
\chi
the intersection of
\Sigma(f^{+}_{i})
with any thin block associated to
\psi
. Then (
7.1
) shows that there exists
C_{1}>0
such that
{\rm len}(\psi)\leq C_{1}
.

If
q\in\partial^{-}C_{\theta}\backslash\mu_{i}
, then we consider a small embedded disk in
\partial^{-}C_{\theta}\backslash\mu_{i}
around
q
of radius
r
in the intrinsic metric of
\partial^{-}C_{\theta}
. We start to increase
r
until either the disk bumps into itself or bumps into
\mu_{i}
. Since
\partial^{-}C_{\theta}
intrinsically is a hyperbolic surface, due to the Gauss–Bonnet theorem, there exists a constant
C_{2}>0
, depending only on the genus, such that this happens for
r\leq C_{2}
. In the first case we obtain
\psi
as a homotopically non-trivial curve based at
p
and contained in the relative interior of a component of
\partial^{-}C_{\theta}\backslash\mu_{i}
. In the second case we follow the shortest path from
p
to
\mu_{i}
, do one turn around
\mu_{i}
and take the shortest path back. The length of the obtained curve is
\leq C_{1}+2C_{2}
. We then lift
\psi
to
\chi
along the gradient of the cosmological time in a straightforward way.

We have
{\rm len}(\chi)\geq\sys(d_{i}^{+})
. By Lemma
7.2
, for all large enough
i
we have
\sys(d_{i}^{+})\geq t_{i}\sys(d^{+})/2
. Now we apply Lemma
5.17
and get

\textsc{ct}_{i}(p)\geq\frac{{\rm len}(\chi)}{{\rm len}(\psi)\exp({\rm len}(\psi))}\geq\frac{t_{i}\sys(d^{+})}{2(C_{1}+2C_{2})\exp(C_{1}+2C_{2})},

which finishes the proof.
∎

We need an insight into the intrinsic geometry of the level surfaces of the cosmological time. They are instances of what is called a
grafted metric
. The initial interest to the grafted metrics comes from the study of
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
-structures on
S
, see, e.g.,
[
30
,
47
]
for details.

The canonical decomposition of
\Omega_{i}
in our case is just one or two thick blocks and zero or one Misner blocks. Looking at the metric inside the blocks (see, e.g.,
[
5
, Section 5.4]
), we observe that the intrinsic metric of the
r
-level surface
L_{i,r}
of
\textsc{ct}_{i}
,
0<r<\pi/2
, is obtained as follows. Let
m_{i}^{-}
be the intrinsic metric of
\partial^{-}C_{i}
, considered as a hyperbolic metric on
S
. Scale it by
\sin r
, cut along
\mu_{i}
and glue there the Euclidean cylinder of length equal to the length of
\mu_{i}
in the scaled metric and of width equal to
s_{i}\cos r
. The Euclidean cylinder corresponds to the intersection of
L_{i,r}
with the Misner block and the rest arises from the intersection with the thick blocks. Now we can prove Lemma
7.3
.

Proof of Lemma
7.3
.

Set
r_{i}:=Ct_{i}/2
, where
C
is the constant from Lemma
7.5
. Let
L_{i}
be the
r_{i}
-level surface of
\textsc{ct}_{i}
in
\Omega_{i}
. By Lemma
7.5
,
L_{i}
belongs to the strict past of
\Sigma(f^{+}_{i})
. By Lemma
B.1
, for every
\gamma\in\pi_{1}S
we have
{\rm len}_{L_{i}}(\gamma)\leq{\rm len}_{\Sigma(f^{+}_{i})}(\gamma)={\rm len}_{d_{i}^{+}}(\gamma)
.

Since the intrinsic metric of
L_{i}
is obtained by the grafting process from the intrinsic metric of
\partial^{-}C_{i}
scaled by
\sin r_{i}
, for every
\gamma\in\pi_{1}S
we have
l_{L_{i}}(\gamma)\geq\sin r_{i}l_{\rho_{i}^{-}}(\gamma)
. Altogether, this and Lemma
7.2
imply that for some constant
C_{0}>0
and all large enough
i
we have

l_{\rho_{i}^{-}}(\gamma)\leq C_{0}l_{d^{+}}(\gamma).

It is a standard compactness criterion for the Teichmüller space that there are finitely many classes
\gamma
in
\pi_{1}S
such that if
l_{\rho_{i}^{-}}(\gamma)
are uniformly bounded, then the sequence is precompact in
\mathcal{T}
. See, e.g.,
[
32
, Lemmas 7.10-7.11]
. Thus, up to subsequence,
\rho_{i}^{-}
converge to
\rho\in\mathcal{T}
.

We also have
\lambda_{i}^{\pm}\rightarrow 0
. By the Mess Theorem, Theorem
5.4
, we get
\rho^{l}_{i},\rho^{r}_{i}\rightarrow\rho
. Hence,
\theta_{i}\rightarrow(\rho,\rho)
.
∎

7.2
End of the proof

Convergence of the marked points follows from

Lemma 7.6
.

Under the conditions of Lemma
AdS3
, assume that
\theta_{i}
converge to
\theta\in\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{T}
. Furthermore, if
y\in\partial\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{s}
, assume additionally that
\theta
is on the diagonal. Then, up to subsequence,
x_{i}
converge to
x\in\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{s}
.

We claim that this, again, follows basically from the same arguments as the respective proofs in Part 1, in Sections
5.4
and
5.5
. First, consider the case
y\in\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}_{-}^{s}
. Our results from Section
5.4
were given for constant
\rho_{i}^{l}
. However, all the conclusions remain valid when instead it belongs to a compact set. This implies the convergence to
x\in\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{s}
in this case.

In the case when
y\in\partial\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{s}
, one just needs to modify our construction of the compactification from Section
5.5.1
. The spaces
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{\bdia}
and
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\bdia}
are constructed exactly the same as
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{\bdia}
and
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\bdia}
. Now fix
\rho\in\mathcal{R}
, set
\theta=(\rho,\rho)
, and consider a compact neighborhood
\widetilde{U}
of
\rho
in
\mathcal{R}
projecting to a compact neighborhood
U
of
\rho
in
\mathcal{T}
. Then
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{\bdia}(\widetilde{U})
is defined as a subset consisting of all
(\theta^{\prime},\widetilde{f}^{+},\widetilde{f}^{-})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}
such that (0)
\theta^{\prime}\in\widetilde{U}\times\widetilde{U}
; (1)
\widetilde{f}^{\pm}
have values in
\widetilde{\Omega}^{\pm}_{\theta^{\prime}}\cup\partial^{\pm}\widetilde{C}_{\theta^{\prime}}\cup\partial^{\pm}_{s}\widetilde{\Omega}^{\pm}_{\theta^{\prime}}
respectively; and (2)
\widetilde{f}^{\pm}
are in convex positions. Then
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}_{-}^{\bdia}(U)
is defined as the quotient of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{\bdia}(\widetilde{U})
by the
G_{-}
- and
\pi_{1}S
-actions. We define
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\vee}^{\bdia}(\widetilde{U}):=\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{\bdia}(\widetilde{U})\cup\mathbb{S}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}^{\bdia})
,
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{\bdia}(U):=\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}_{-}^{\bdia}(U)\cup\mathbb{S}(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\bdia})
. The first has topology from its inclusion to
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\vee}
and the topology on the second is induced as on a quotient of the first. Similarly as in Section
5.5.1
one can show that
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{\bdia}(U)
is compact.

In the context of Lemma
7.6
, when
y\in\partial\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{s}
, we have
\theta=(\rho,\rho)
. Then we pick a compact neighborhood
U
of this
\rho
to construct
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{\bdia}(U)
. The rest of the proof of this case continues exactly the same as in Section
5.5.2
.

To prove Lemma
AdS3
, it remains only to overcome the additional assumption in Lemma
7.3
that
\lambda_{i}^{-}
are supported on simple closed curves.

Proof of Lemma
AdS3
.

Due to Lemmas
7.1
and
7.6
, it remains to consider the case when
y\in\partial\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}_{\vee}^{s}
. As it is explained in Section
7.1
, there exists a sequence
t_{i}^{\prime}\rightarrow 0
such that, up to subsequence,
\frac{1}{t_{i}^{\prime}}\lambda_{i}^{\pm}\rightarrow\lambda^{\pm}
in
\mathcal{ML}
and one of
\lambda^{\pm}
is nonzero. We assume that it is
\lambda^{-}
. The space
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{s}
is metrizable, we pick a metric
D
on it.

The laminations that are supported on simple closed curves are dense in
\mathcal{ML}
. Due to this fact and the Mess Theorem, Theorem
5.4
, one can choose a sequence
\widehat{x}_{i}
such that for every
i
we have
D(x_{i},\widehat{x}_{i})\leq 2^{-i}
,
\mathcal{I}\mathcal{I}_{\vee}^{s}(\widehat{x}_{i})\rightarrow y
,
\frac{1}{t_{i}^{\prime}}\widehat{\lambda}_{i}^{\pm}\rightarrow\lambda^{\pm}
and
\widehat{\lambda}_{i}^{-}
are all supported on simple closed curves. Here
\widehat{\lambda}_{i}^{\pm}
are the respective bending laminations of
\Omega_{\widehat{\theta}_{i}}
, where
\widehat{\theta}_{i}
are the holonomies of
\widehat{x}_{i}
. Hence, Lemma
7.3
implies that, up to subsequence,
\widehat{\theta}_{i}
converge to
\theta\in\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{T}
. Furthermore,
\theta
is on the diagonal of
\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{T}
. Then Lemma
7.6
implies that, up to subsequence,
\widehat{x}_{i}
converge to
x\in\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}_{\vee}^{s}
. However, the condition
D(x_{i},\widehat{x}_{i})<2^{-i}
then shows that
x_{i}
also converge to
x
. The proof is finished.
∎

Appendix A.
Intrinsic metrics of convex surfaces

Denote by
d_{A}
the spacelike distance on
\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
, which is defined on the pairs of points in spacelike relation. Let
\chi:[a,b]\rightarrow\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
be a
C^{0}
-curve. We call it
spacelike
if for every
x\in[a,b]
there exists its neighborhood
X\subset[a,b]
such that every two points in
\chi(X)
are in spacelike relation. Let
t_{0}=a<t_{1}<\ldots<t_{n}=b
be a partition of
[a,b]
. The diameter of a partition is
\sup|t_{i+1}-t_{i}|
. Since
\chi
is spacelike, when the diameter is small enough, all pairs
\chi(t_{i})
,
\chi(t_{i+1})
are in spacelike relation. We call it a
spacelike partition
. We say that it is
spacelike rectifiable
if there exists

{\rm len}_{A}(\chi):=\limsup\sum d_{A}(\chi(t_{i}),\chi(t_{i+1})),

where the
\limsup
is taken over spacelike partitions as their diameters tend to zero. Then
{\rm len}_{A}(\chi)
is the
(spacelike) length
of
\chi
. If
\chi
is differentiable almost everywhere, then its tangent vectors are non-timelike and

{\rm len}_{A}(\chi)=\int_{a}^{b}\|\dot{\chi}\|.

(A.1)

Vice versa, a differentiable almost everywhere curve with non-timelike tangent vectors is spacelike rectifiable.

We say that a surface
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
is
entirely convex
if it is a boundary component of the intersection of a convex subset of
\mathbb{RP}^{3}
with
\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
. Let
\Sigma
be an entirely convex spacelike surface. The
intrinsic distance
between two points of
\Sigma
is the infimum of lengths of all spacelike rectifiable curves in
\Sigma
connecting the points. Clearly, at least one such curve exists between any pair of points. What is not immediate, however, that for distinct points the defined distance is positive, and, more generally, that the topology induced by the obtained intrinsic pseudo-metric is the same is the initial topology of
\Sigma
as of a submanifold of
\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
.

Lemma A.1
.

The intrinsic pseudo-metric
d
is a metric and does not alter the topology
\Sigma
.

Proof.

Pick
p\in\Sigma
. Consider the Minkowski chart and the standard Euclidean metric
d_{E}
on it. We may assume that
p=o
and the horizontal plane
\Pi
is supporting for
\Sigma
at
p
. For a small enough neighborhood
U
of
p
on
\Sigma
in the standard topology there exist
A_{1},A_{2}>0
such that for any
p,q\in U
we have

A_{1}d_{A}(p,q)\leq d_{E}(p,q)\leq A_{2}d_{A}(p,q).

Thus, spacelike rectifiable curves inside
U
are Euclidean rectifiable and vice versa. It implies that
d
is a metric and that every neighborhood of
p
with respect to the standard topology contains a neighborhood with respect to
d
and vice versa. Since both topologies are metric, it follows that
d
does not alter the topology of
\Sigma
.
∎

We note that it can happen that the intrinsic metric is incomplete. Since
\Sigma
is locally compact, when
d
is complete, a standard application of the Arzelà–Ascoli theorem implies the existence of a shortest path between any pair of points on
\Sigma
.

A
convex body
is a closed convex set
C\subset\mathbb{RP}^{3}
with non-empty interior. We call it
spacelike
if every plane supporting it at a point in
\partial C\cap\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
is spacelike. Let
C_{i}
be a sequence of spacelike convex bodies converging to a spacelike convex body
C
. Let
\Sigma_{i}
,
\Sigma
be connected components of
\partial C_{i}\cap\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
,
\partial C\cap\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
. We assume that
{\rm cl}(\Sigma_{i})
converge to
{\rm cl}(\Sigma)
and
\partial C_{i}\backslash\Sigma_{i}
converge to
\partial C\backslash\Sigma
. Pick a point
p\in{\rm int}(C)
, we assume that
p\in{\rm int}(C_{i})
for all
i
. Let
S^{2}
be the space of directions from
p
,
D_{i}
and
D
be the projections of
\Sigma_{i}
,
\Sigma
to
S^{2}
. Then
{\rm cl}(D_{i})
converge to
{\rm cl}(D)
and
S^{2}\backslash D_{i}
converge to
S^{2}\backslash D
. We consider the intrinsic metrics
d_{i}
,
d
of
\Sigma_{i}
,
\Sigma
pushed forward to
D_{i}
,
D
. We assume that they are complete. Pick
p,q\in D
, let
p_{i}
,
q_{i}
be two sequences converging to
p
and
q
respectively in
D
. We have
p_{i},q_{i}\in D_{i}
for all large enough
i
. We want to prove

Lemma A.2
.

We have
d_{i}(p_{i},q_{i})\rightarrow d(p,q)
. Furthermore, up to subsequence, there are shortest paths between
p_{i}
and
q_{i}
for
d_{i}
whose images converge in the Hausdorff sense to the image of a shortest path between
p
and
q
for
d
.

Note that one could show that
d_{i}
and
d
are CAT(0), hence the shortest paths are unique. We will need it only in particular, rather evident cases, so we will not prove it in full generality.

We need to make another digression first. Let
\Pi\subset\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
be a spacelike plane. We denote its metric by
d_{\Pi}
and its length function by
{\rm len}_{\Pi}
. The past-directed normal exponential map from
\Pi
is a diffeomorphism
\mathcal{E}_{\Pi}
from
\Pi\times[0,\pi_{2})
onto the image. Let
K\subset\Pi
be a compact convex subset with nonempty interior. We call a function
s:K\rightarrow[0,\pi/2)

C-convex
if it is continuous and its graph with respect to
\mathcal{E}_{\Pi}
is spacelike and future-convex. Here we say that a convex surface with boundary in
\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
is spacelike if each supporting plane at the interior points is spacelike and those supporting planes at the boundary points that are the limits of supporting planes at intrinsic points are spacelike. We will follow the paper
[
52
]
of Labeni, who treated the intrinsic geometry of graphs of C-convex functions. We note that Labeni works with the functions defined over
\Pi
, which does not matter for our context. Following the prior work
[
38
]
of Fillastre–Slutskiy on the Minkowski case, Labeni makes few technical assumptions on the functions he works with. To apply his work, we will need now to show that they are actually unnecessary, i.e., they hold for all C-convex functions.

Let
\chi:[a,b]\rightarrow K
be a Lipschitz curve for
d_{\Pi}
. Then
s\circ\chi
is a Lipschitz function, see
[
52
, Section 2.2]
. In particular, it is differentiable almost everywhere and so is the respective curve
\chi_{s}:[a,b]\rightarrow\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
in the graph of
s
obtained via
\mathcal{E}_{\Pi}
. The tangent vectors to
\chi_{s}
are spacelike, so it is spacelike rectifiable. Labeni defines
L_{s}(\chi):=\int_{a}^{b}\|\dot{\chi}_{s}\|
. Due to (
A.1
), we have
L_{s}(\chi)={\rm len}_{A}(\chi_{s})
. Labeni defines an intrinsic metric
d_{s}
on
K
from the length structure
L_{s}
.

On the other hand, let
\chi_{s}:[a,b]\rightarrow\mathbb{A}d\mathbb{S}^{3}
be a spacelike rectifiable curve in the graph of
s
and
