\chi:[a,b]\rightarrow K
be its projection. We assume that
\Pi
is the horizontal plane of the Minkowski chart and pick the standard Euclidean metric there. It is easy to see from compactness that there exists a constant
A>0
such that the Euclidean length of every chord of the graph of
s
is at most
A
times the spacelike length. Hence,
\chi_{s}
is rectifiable for the Euclidean metric. Hence,
\chi
is rectifiable for the Euclidean metric on
\Pi
. But then, again due to compactness,
\chi
is rectifiable for
d_{\Pi}
.

Now we consider a curve
\chi:[a,b]\rightarrow K
that is rectifiable for
d_{s}
. Since the projections to
\Pi
of spacelike segments in the image of
\mathcal{E}_{\Pi}
are rectifiable for
d_{\Pi}
and thus for
d_{s}
, one sees that the respective
\chi_{s}
is spacelike rectifiable and
{\rm len}_{A}(\chi_{s})\leq{\rm len}_{d_{s}}(\chi)
, where
{\rm len}_{d_{s}}
is the length structure induced by
d_{s}
. By the argument above,
\chi
is rectifiable for
d_{\Pi}
.

Furthermore, if
\chi:[a,b]\rightarrow K
is rectifiable for
d_{\Pi}
, then trivially
{\rm len}_{d_{s}}(\chi)\leq L_{s}(\chi)={\rm len}_{A}(\chi_{s})
. Since
{\rm len}_{A}(\chi_{s})\leq{\rm len}_{d_{s}}(\chi)
, we get

{\rm len}_{d_{s}}(\chi)=L_{s}(\chi)={\rm len}_{A}(\chi_{s}).

Let us sum it up. The rectifiable curves for
d_{s}
are rectifiable for
d_{\Pi}
and vice versa. Furthermore their
d_{s}
-lengths coincide with their
L_{s}
-lengths and with
{\rm len}_{A}
of their images in the graph of
s
. We denote this length structure now by
{\rm len}_{s}
on
U
. Just the same proof as of Lemma
A.1
shows that
d_{s}
does not alter the topology of
U
. Our conclusions allow us to apply the results of Labeni. In
[
52
, Lemma 2.11]
, Labeni showed

Lemma A.3
.

Over
K
we have
d_{s}\leq d_{\Pi}
.

Let
s_{i}
be a sequence of C-convex functions on
K
converging uniformly to a C-convex function
s
. Define
d_{i}:=d_{s_{i}}
,
{\rm len}_{i}:={\rm len}_{s_{i}}
. It follows from
[
52
, Lemma 3.4]
that

Lemma A.4
.

There exists
A>0
such that for all
i
we have
d_{i}\geq A\cdot d_{\Pi}
as well as
d_{s}\geq A\cdot d_{\Pi}
over
K
.

From Lemma
A.3
and
A.4
it follows

Corollary A.5
.

There exist
A_{1},A_{2}>0
such that over
K
for all
i
we have

A_{1}d_{s}\leq d_{i}\leq A_{2}d_{s},

A_{1}{\rm len}_{s}\leq{\rm len}_{i}\leq A_{2}{\rm len}_{s}.

Furthermore,
[
52
, Proposition 2.9]
gives us

Lemma A.6
.

Let
\chi:[0,1]\rightarrow K
be a rectifiable curve. Then
{\rm len}_{i}(\chi)\rightarrow{\rm len}_{s}(\chi)
.

Note that Labeni states his result “up to subsequence”, which one overcomes by applying Lemma
3.10
. Using
[
38
, Lemma 2.5 and Lemma 2.1]
, we deduce

Lemma A.7
.

We have
d_{i}\rightarrow d_{s}
uniformly on
K
.

Now we show

Lemma A.8
.

For every
p\in{\rm int}(K)
there exists its neighborhood
U_{p}\subset{\rm int}(K)
such that for all large enough
i
and all
q,q^{\prime}\in U_{p}
every shortest path between
q
and
q^{\prime}
for
d_{i}
is contained in
{\rm int}(K)
. The same claim holds for
d_{s}
.

Proof.

Consider the first claim.
Suppose the converse. Then, up to subsequence, there are
q_{i}\rightarrow p
,
q_{i}^{\prime}\rightarrow p
such that there is a shortest path
\chi_{i}:[0,1]\rightarrow K
for
d_{i}
between
q_{i}
and
q_{i}^{\prime}
that contains a point of
\partial K
. Because of Corollary
A.5
, we have
{\rm len}_{i}(\chi_{i})=d_{i}(q_{i},q^{\prime}_{i})\rightarrow 0
. By applying Corollary
A.5
again, we get
{\rm len}_{s}(\chi_{i})\rightarrow 0
. After reparameterizing
\chi_{i}
proportional to
{\rm len}_{s}
, by applying the Arzelà–Ascoli theorem, up to subsequence,
\chi_{i}
converge to a curve
\chi:[0,1]\rightarrow K
that passes through
p
as well as through a point of
\partial K
and
{\rm len}_{s}(\chi)\leq\liminf{\rm len}_{s}(\chi_{i})=0
. This is a contradiction to that
d_{s}
induces the standard topology on
K
. The second claim is proven the same.
∎

Now we return to our previous setting. We denote the lengths structures of
d
,
d_{i}
by
{\rm len}
,
{\rm len}_{i}
. Since spacelike surfaces are locally graphs over spacelike planes, Lemma
A.8
together with Corollary
A.5
and Lemma
A.6
yield

Corollary A.9
.

For every
p\in D
there exists its neighborhood
U_{p}\subset D
and
A_{1},A_{2}>0
, depending on
U_{p}
, such that over
U_{p}
for all
i
we have

A_{1}d\leq d_{i}\leq A_{2}d,

A_{1}{\rm len}\leq{\rm len}_{i}\leq A_{2}{\rm len}.

Corollary A.10
.

For every
p\in D
there exists its neighborhood
U_{p}\subset D
such that if
\chi:[0,1]\rightarrow U_{p}
is rectifiable curve, then
{\rm len}_{i}(\chi)\rightarrow{\rm len}(\chi)
.

In turn, these imply

Corollary A.11
.

For every compact
K\subset D
there exist
A_{1},A_{2}>0
, depending on
K
, such that for all
i
over rectifiable curves in
K
we have

A_{1}{\rm len}\leq{\rm len}_{i}\leq A_{2}{\rm len}.

Corollary A.12
.

Let
\chi:[0,1]\rightarrow D
be a rectifiable curve. Then
{\rm len}_{i}(\chi)\rightarrow{\rm len}(\chi)
.

Furthermore, we will need the following technical results.

Lemma A.13
.

Let
\chi_{i}:[0,1]\rightarrow D
be a sequence of rectifiable curves converging uniformly for
d
to a rectifiable curve
\chi:[0,1]\rightarrow D
. Then
{\rm len}(\chi)\leq\liminf{\rm len}_{i}(\chi_{i})
.

Proof.

We use some ideas from the proof of
[
38
, Proposition 3.12]
. Pick a partition
t_{0}=0<t_{1}<\ldots<t_{n}=1
and
\varepsilon>0
. By Corollary
A.9
, there exists
A>0
and for every
j=0,\ldots,n
there exists a neighborhood
U_{j}
of
\chi(t_{j})
such that if
p\in U_{j}
, then

d_{i}(\chi(t_{j}),p)<A\cdot d(\chi(t_{j}),p).

Then for all large enough
i
we have

d_{i}(\chi(t_{j}),\chi_{i}(t_{j}))\leq A\cdot d(\chi(t_{j}),\chi_{i}(t_{j}))\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{n+1}.

Hence
\sum_{j}d_{i}(\chi(t_{j}),\chi_{i}(t_{j}))\leq\varepsilon
. By the triangle inequality,

\sum_{j}d_{i}(\chi(t_{j}),\chi(t_{j+1}))\leq\sum_{j}d_{i}(\chi_{i}(t_{j}),\chi_{i}(t_{j+1}))+2\varepsilon.

By taking the suprema over partitions, we get
{\rm len}_{i}(\chi)\leq{\rm len}_{i}(\chi_{i})+2\varepsilon.
By Corollary
A.12
,
{\rm len}_{i}(\chi)\rightarrow{\rm len}(\chi)
. Since
\varepsilon>0
is arbitrary, it follows that
{\rm len}(\chi)\leq\liminf{\rm len}_{i}(\chi_{i})
.
∎

Lemma A.14
.

For
p\in D_{i}
and
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
denote by
B_{i}(p,r)
the closed
r
-ball for
d_{i}
around
p
. For every
p\in D
and every
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
there exists compact
K\subset D
such that for all large enough
i
we have
B_{i}(p,r)\subset K
.

Proof.

Suppose the converse.
We fix
p
and vary
r
. If the claim is true for some value of
r
, then trivially it is true for all smaller values. For all small enough
r
the claim is true by Corollary
A.9
. Let
r_{0}>0
be the supremum of those
r
for which the claim is true. Suppose that the claim is true for
r_{0}
. Let
K\subset D
be the respective compact set. Pick a strictly decreasing sequence
r_{i}
converging to
r_{0}
. Then, up to subsequence, there exist a sequence
\chi_{i}:[0,r_{i}]\rightarrow D_{i}
of shortest paths for
d_{i}
parameterized by lengths such that
\chi(0)=p
and
q_{i}:=\chi_{i}(r_{i})
leave every compact subset of
D
. Define
x_{i}:=\chi_{i}(r_{0})
. Then
x_{i}\in K
. Up to subsequence,
x_{i}
converge to
x\in K
. Pick a compact neighborhood
U_{x}\ni x
in
D
from Corollary
A.9
. Pick a simple closed curve
Y
around
x
in
U_{x}
. Since
q_{i}
leave every compact set, for all large enough
i
we have
Y\cap\chi_{i}((r_{0},r_{i}])\neq\emptyset
. Pick
y_{i}
in this intersection. Up to subsequence,
y_{i}
converge to
y\in Y
. Thus
y\neq x
, so
d(x,y)>0
. On the other hand, we have
d_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})\leq r_{i}-r_{0}\rightarrow 0
. By Corollary
A.9
, there exists
A>0
such that for all large enough
i
we have
d(x_{i},y_{i})\leq A\cdot d_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})
. Then
d(x_{i},y_{i})\rightarrow 0
. This is a contradiction.

Now suppose that the claim is not true for
r_{0}
. Pick a strictly increasing sequence
r_{j}
converging to
r_{0}
. Let
\chi_{i}:[0,r_{0}]\rightarrow D_{i}
be a sequence of shortest paths for
d_{i}
parameterized by lengths such that
q_{i}=\chi_{i}(r_{0})
leave every compact subset of
D
. Denote by
\chi_{i}^{j}
the restriction of
\chi_{i}
to
[0,r_{j}]
. For every
j
and all large enough
i
, by assumption,
\chi_{i}^{j}
belong to compact
K_{j}\subset D
. By Corollary
A.11
, there exists
A_{j}>0
such that for all
i
we have
{\rm len}(\chi_{i}^{j})\leq A_{j}{\rm len}_{i}(\chi_{i}^{j})\leq A_{j}r_{0}
. By the Arzelà–Ascoli theorem, after a reparameterization, up to subsequence,
\chi_{i}^{j}
converge in
i
to
\chi^{j}:[0,r_{j}]\rightarrow K_{j}
uniformly for
d
. By Lemma
A.13
, we have
{\rm len}(\chi^{j})\leq\liminf_{i}{\rm len}_{i}(\chi_{i}^{j})\leq r_{j}
. We do this subsequently, passing to further subsequences, and construct a curve
\chi:[0,r_{0})\rightarrow D
such that for every
r_{j}
from the sequence we have
{\rm len}(\chi|_{[0,r_{j}]})\leq r_{j}
. On the other hand,
\chi
leaves every compact subset of
D
. This contradicts to completness of
d
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
A.2
.

We prove the first claim also up to subsequence, then we can get rid of it by Lemma
3.10
. Let
\chi
be a shortest path between
p
and
q
for
d
,
\chi^{\prime}_{i}
be a shortest path between
p_{i}
and
p
for
d_{i}
and
\chi^{\prime\prime}_{i}
be a shortest path between
q
and
q_{i}
for
d_{i}
. Corollary
A.9
shows that
{\rm len}_{i}(\chi^{\prime}_{i})\rightarrow 0
,
{\rm len}_{i}(\chi^{\prime\prime}_{i})\rightarrow 0
. Corollary
A.12
says that
{\rm len}_{i}(\chi)\rightarrow{\rm len}(\chi)
. By considering the concatenation of paths
\chi^{\prime}_{i}
,
\chi
and
\chi^{\prime\prime}_{i}
, we see that
\limsup d_{i}(p_{i},q_{i})\leq d(p,q)
.

Due to Corollary
A.9
and Lemma
A.14
, there exists a compact
K\subset D
such that for all large enough
i
all shortest paths between
p_{i}
and
q_{i}
for
d_{i}
belong to
K
.
Let
\chi_{i}:[0,1]\rightarrow D_{i}
be shortest paths between
p_{i}
and
q_{i}
for
d_{i}
. Then, for all large enough
i
the images of
\chi_{i}
are in
K
. Due to Lemma
A.11
, there exists
A>0
such that
{\rm len}(\chi_{i})\leq A{\rm len}_{i}(\chi_{i})
for all
i
. Since
\limsup d_{i}(p_{i},q_{i})\leq d(p,q)
, we have
{\rm len}(\chi_{i})
bounded. Hence, after a reparameterization, we can apply the Arzelà–Ascoli theorem and see that, up to subsequence,
\chi_{i}
converge to a curve
\chi:[0,1]\rightarrow K
uniformly in
d
. By Lemma
A.13
,
{\rm len}(\chi)\leq\liminf{\rm len}_{i}(\chi_{i})
. Thus
d(p,q)\leq\liminf d_{i}(p_{i},q_{i}).
This also means that
\chi
is a shortest path between
p
and
q
for
d
, whose image is the Hausdorff limit of the images of
\chi_{i}
.
∎

Among others, we will need the following two applications of Lemma
A.2
. For
\rho\in\mathcal{T}
and a convex Cauchy surface
\Sigma\subset\Omega_{\rho}
let
l_{\Sigma}:\pi_{1}S\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{>0}
be the
length function
of
\Sigma
sending
\gamma\in\pi_{1}S
to the infimum of lengths of closed curves in the free homotopy class of
\gamma
.

Lemma A.15
.

Let
\Sigma_{i}\subset\Omega_{\rho}
be a sequence of future-convex Cauchy surfaces converging in the Hausdorff sense to a Cauchy surface
\Sigma
. Then
l_{\Sigma_{i}}\rightarrow l_{\Sigma}
pointwise.

Proof.

Pick
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}
and let
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{i}
,
\widetilde{\Sigma}
be the preimages of
\Sigma_{i}
,
\Sigma
. Then
{\rm cl}(\Sigma_{i})\rightarrow{\rm cl}(\Sigma)
. Using the convex bodies bounded by
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{i}\cup\Lambda_{\rho}\cup\partial^{-}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}
we get to the setting of Lemma
A.2
. Pick
\gamma\in\pi_{1}S
. From considering a representative curve in
D
for
l_{\Sigma}(\gamma)
and using Lemma
A.2
, we see that
\limsup l_{\Sigma_{i}}(\gamma)\leq l_{\Sigma}(\gamma)
. One can pick a compact fundamental domain
F\subset D
for the action of
\pi_{1}S
on
D
coming from the action on
\widetilde{\Sigma}
and pick compact fundamental domains
F_{i}
for the actions on
D_{i}
coming from
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{i}
so that
F_{i}\rightarrow F
in the Hausdorff sense. Pick representative curves
\chi_{i}
for
l_{\Sigma_{i}}(\gamma)
in
D_{i}
starting in
F_{i}
. Since
F_{i}\rightarrow F
and since
\limsup l_{\Sigma_{i}}(\gamma)\leq l_{\Sigma}(\gamma)
, for an arbitrary point
p\in F
there exists
r>0
such that the endpoints of
\chi_{i}
are contained in
B_{i}(p,r)
, where
B_{i}(p,r)
is the closed
r
-ball for
d_{i}
around
p
. Lemma
A.14
states that then these endpoints belong to a compact set
K\subset D
. Then it follows from Lemma
A.2
that
l_{\Sigma}(\gamma)\leq\liminf l_{\Sigma_{i}}(\gamma)
.
∎

Next, recall the notation from Section
3.4.5
. For
(\rho,f)\in\mathcal{P}_{-}^{w}
and
v\in V
such that
f(v)\in\Sigma(f)
, define
l_{f,v}:\pi_{1}S\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{>0}
to send
\gamma\in\pi_{1}S
to the infimum of lengths of closed curves on
\Sigma
based at
f(v)
in the homotopy class of
\gamma
.

Lemma A.16
.

Let
(\rho_{i},f)\rightarrow(\rho,f)
in
\mathcal{P}_{-}^{w}
and for
v\in V
we have
f_{i}(v)\in\Sigma(f_{i})
. Then also
f(v)\in\Sigma(f)
and
l_{f_{i},v}\rightarrow l_{f,v}
pointwise.

Proof.

The claim that
f(v)\in\Sigma(f)
is clear from the topology of
\mathcal{P}_{-}^{w}
, we only need to show the second claim. We lift all to
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{-}^{w}
so that we are in the setting of Lemma
A.2
. Clearly,
l_{f_{i},v}(\gamma)=d_{i}(\widetilde{f}_{i}(v),\widetilde{f}_{i}(\gamma v))
,
l_{f,v}(\gamma)=d(\widetilde{f}(v),\widetilde{f}(\gamma v))
. Now the claim follows from Lemma
A.2
.
∎

Appendix B.
A Busemann–Feller-type lemma

Here we prove a fact that seems important on its own. Pick arbitrary
\rho\in\mathcal{T}
. The goal of this section is to show

Lemma B.1
.

Let
\Sigma\subset\Omega_{\rho}
be a future-convex Cauchy surface and
\Sigma_{0}\subset\Omega_{\rho}
be a
C^{1}
Cauchy surface in the strict past of
\Sigma
. Then for any
\gamma\in\pi_{1}S
we have
l_{\Sigma_{0}}(\gamma)\leq l_{\Sigma}(\gamma)
.

This lemma resembles a Lorentzian analogue of the renowned Busemann–Feller lemma. There are multiple results of this flavor in the literature: see, e.g.,
[
5
, Proposition 6.1]
,
[
8
, Proposition 4.1]
or
[
21
, Lemma 5.3]
. However, they all do not apply to the generality that we require. We begin with

Lemma B.2
.

Suppose that we have a
C^{2}
-foliation of a globally hyperbolic (2+1)-spacetime
\Omega
by future-convex Cauchy surfaces.
Let
L\subset\Omega
be a leaf of the foliation and
\chi:[0,1]\rightarrow\Omega
be a spacelike rectifiable curve in the past of
L
. Let
\psi
be the projection of
\chi
to
L
along the normal flow of the foliation. Then
{\rm len}(\chi)\leq{\rm len}(\psi)
.

This is shown in
[
5
, Proposition 6.1]
by Barbot–Beguin–Zeghib. Note that there the authors speak about a precise foliation, but they only use the convexity of the leaves.

To prove Lemma
B.1
, we need to construct a foliation. First, we will employ

Lemma B.3
.

Let
\Sigma
be a future-convex Cauchy surface in
\Omega_{\rho}
. Then it can be approximated in the Hausdorff sense by smooth strictly future-convex Cauchy surfaces.

This is shown in
[
18
, Lemma 4.2]
by Bonsante–Schlenker. Next, we need

Lemma B.4
.

Let
\Sigma
be a smooth strictly future-convex Cauchy surface in
\Omega_{\rho}
and
L_{K}\subset\Omega_{\rho}
be a future-convex Cauchy surface of constant Gauss curvature
K
in the strict past of
\Sigma
with
K
greater than the supremum of the Gauss curvature of
\Sigma
. Then the domain between
\Sigma
and
L_{K}
in
\Omega_{\rho}
is
C^{3}
-smoothly foliated by strictly future-convex Cauchy surfaces. The foliation is
C^{3}
-smooth on
\Sigma
, though not necessarily on
L_{K}
.

Proof.

Let
\Sigma^{*}\subset\Omega
be the dual surface. To obtain it, one considers the preimage
\widetilde{\Sigma}\subset\widetilde{\Omega}_{\rho}
, defines the dual surface
\widetilde{\Sigma}^{*}
as the set of the dual points to the supporting planes to
\widetilde{\Sigma}
and considers the
\theta_{\rho}
-quotient
\Sigma^{*}
of
\widetilde{\Sigma}^{*}
. Then
\Sigma^{*}
is a smooth strictly past-convex Cauchy surface, whose Gauss curvature at a point is reciprocal to the Gauss curvature at the corresponding point of
\Sigma
, see
[
5
, Section 11]
. Similarly, one obtains the dual surface
L_{K}^{*}\subset\Omega_{\rho}
. It follows that it is a past-convex
K^{*}
-surface of curvature
K^{*}=1/K
.

Now we claim that there exists a
C^{4}
-smooth foliation of the domain between
\Sigma^{*}
and
L^{*}_{K}
by strictly past-convex Cauchy surfaces. That follows from results of Gerhardt
[
41
]
. Namely,
K^{*}
is less than the infimum of the Gauss curvature of
\Sigma^{*}
. In
[
41
]
the author describes a curvature flow that starts from
\Sigma^{*}
, evolves into the past, exists for all time and converges to a surface of constant curvature
K^{*}
. For the long-time existence and convergence of the flow, however, it is required to know that there exists a lower barrier, i.e., a smooth Cauchy surface in the past of
\Sigma^{*}
, whose supremum of the curvature is at most
K^{*}
. We use
L_{K}^{*}
for this purpose. Due to the maximum principle
[
5
, Corollary 4.7]
, the past-convex surface of curvature
K^{*}
is unique in
\Omega_{\rho}
, so the flow converges to
L_{K}^{*}
. By dualizing the flow, we construct the desired flow from
\Sigma
to
L_{K}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
B.1
.

Pick
\gamma\in\pi_{1}S
. Due to Lemma
B.3
,
\Sigma
can be approximated in the Hausdorff sense by smooth strictly future-convex surfaces. Due to Lemma
A.15
, for arbitrary
\varepsilon>0
there is such a surface
\Sigma^{\prime}
and a curve
\psi:[0,1]\rightarrow\Sigma^{\prime}
such that
{\rm len}(\psi)\leq l_{\Sigma}(\gamma)+\varepsilon
. Additionally, we can pick
\Sigma^{\prime}
in the future of
\Sigma_{0}
. Due to Theorem
3.24
, there exists a
K
-surface
L
in the strict past of
\Sigma_{0}
. By Lemma
B.4
, there exists a
C^{3}
-smooth foliation of the domain between
\Sigma^{\prime}
and
L
by strictly future-convex Cauchy surfaces. Let
\chi:[0,1]\rightarrow\Sigma_{0}
be the projection of
\psi
to
\Sigma_{0}
along the normal flow of the foliation. By Lemma
B.2
,
{\rm len}(\chi)\leq{\rm len}(\psi)\leq l_{\Sigma}(\gamma)+\varepsilon
.
Because
\varepsilon
is arbitrary, we obtain the desired result.
∎
